a16z Podcast 2 episode(s) · 83 insights

AI, Infrastructure, and the Next Investment Cycle52 min50 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
a16z growth team walks through its State of Markets slides: earnings-driven market gains, hyperscaler capex of $780B in 2026, shallow enterprise AI adoption (69% live vs 2% tracked), software bifurcation, and why top private companies stay private. Argues the cycle is early, not a bubble.
Tech as everything cycle and bubble debate · 4Hyperscaler capex and compute demand · 9Physical infrastructure and the age of atoms · 3AI adoption depth and cost deflation · 12Foundation labs and consumer AI · 7Public software, SaaS apocalypse and private markets · 12Next investment areas · 3
Tech as everything cycle and bubble debate 4
High-tech equipment, software and R&D account for roughly 55% of US capital spending, and tech is almost 40% of aggregate US stock market value, with eight of the top 10 valued companies being US tech firms.
data_point🗣 David Georgeconviction: highUS techS&P 500
So what: Economy-wide capex and index concentration are now a single tech trade.
The AI buildout has just surpassed railroads as a percentage of GDP, and a16z's growth team expects cumulative figures to be roughly 20x higher in five to ten years.
prediction🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumAI buildoutrailroads
So what: Historical analogy used to argue the cycle is early, not late.
US stocks are up about 20% while market multiples are down about 20%, with the S&P 500 forward multiple below 20x, so the rally is earnings-driven rather than multiple expansion, unlike the dot-com era's ~100x PE leaders.
contrarian🗣 David Georgeconviction: highS&P 500dot-com
So what: Core argument against the bubble thesis: earnings, not multiples, drive the market.
Since ChatGPT's launch almost four years ago the market is up 90% (17% annualized), yet it trades below 20x earnings growing ~15%, which differs from 2000 and 2021 when multiples and growth were decoupled.
data_point🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumS&P 500
So what: The instinct that 17% annualized must mean-revert ignores that earnings growth is matching it.
Hyperscaler capex and compute demand 9
Capex of Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft and Oracle is about $780 billion in 2026, up from $416 billion in 2025, with expectations of over $1 trillion annually from 2027.
data_point🗣 David Georgeconviction: highAlphabetAmazonMetaMicrosoftOracle
So what: Capex nearly doubling year over year sets order-book demand for chips, power and construction.
Successive forecasts for the five largest hyperscalers' capex have repeatedly moved sharply higher, and spending once viewed as a ceiling has become near-term; the economy has underestimated the trend for four years.
thesis🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: highhyperscalers
So what: Betting on capex plateau has been wrong repeatedly.
Hyperscalers are investing essentially all of their near-term operating cash flow into the buildout, and demand outstrips supply in almost every case, with some data center supply chain items unobtainable until 2028.
data_point🗣 David Georgeconviction: highhyperscalersdata centers
So what: Supply constraints extend through 2028, supporting suppliers' pricing power.
Agents running parallel and long-running tasks, including overnight, raise compute requirements far beyond the occasional-query model, making compute demand a 'model buster'.
causal🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: highagentscompute
So what: Demand forecasts based on chat-query usage understate agent-driven compute.
OpenAI's massive compute commitments were criticized as reckless a year ago but are now seen as prescient, and OpenAI recently had to pause new subscriptions on its pro plans because of insatiable demand.
anecdote🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumOpenAISam Altman
So what: Capacity, not demand, is the binding constraint for leading labs.
Microsoft, Google and Amazon have about $1.7 trillion of combined cloud backlog, and consensus has hyperscaler free cash flow, depressed during the buildout, recovering from 2028 with substantial growth after.
data_point🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumMicrosoftGoogleAmazon
So what: Backlog gives a demand-side anchor for the FCF J-curve.
Amazon described a J-curve in which GPUs and TPUs have useful economic lives longer than expected, so shells and chips bought up front earn returns over a long period.
causal🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumAmazonGPUsTPUs
So what: Depreciation-life assumptions underpin whether capex returns are adequate.
Hyperscalers that blinked and were less aggressive on capex and debt raising have regretted it, mirroring the model labs.
thesis🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: mediumhyperscalers
So what: Market punishes under-investment more than debt-funded over-investment in this cycle.
Hyperscaler capex should be viewed as someone else's order books, turning the tech boom into an industrial boom across chips, power and construction, and requiring financing, vendor management and capacity forecasting skills unlike traditional build-once software.
framework🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumhyperscalersdata centers
So what: Investing in this supply chain requires different underwriting than software IP monetization.
Physical infrastructure and the age of atoms 3
Global infrastructure investment needs are estimated at $90 trillion through 2040, extending beyond AI and data centers to power, water, roads and transit.
data_point🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: mediuminfrastructure
So what: The investable infrastructure opportunity is broader than data centers.
a16z portfolio examples of the physical buildout include Anduril's massive manufacturing facility (87 football fields), Waymo's depot expansion, and SpaceX's $100 billion investment in Louisiana.
anecdote🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: mediumAndurilWaymoSpaceX
So what: Winning founders increasingly execute factories as competitive advantage.
A recent US study found that for every 10% increase in data center capacity, residential electricity rates fell by 40 bps, because large stable customers spread the fixed costs of poles, wires and substations.
contrarian🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumdata centerselectricity rates
So what: Counters the political narrative that data centers raise household power bills.
AI adoption depth and cost deflation 12
S&P 500 live AI deployments are at 69%, quantifiable impact is 30%, but metrics tracked over time are only 2%, showing adoption is shallow relative to headlines.
data_point🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: highS&P 500AI adoption
So what: Large runway for deepening enterprise usage into recurring workflows.
Median AI vendor spending in the top 1% of companies is roughly eight times the level of the top 10%, showing power users pulling away.
data_point🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: mediumAI vendor spending
So what: AI spend is highly concentrated among the most intensive adopters.
Within AI-native a16z portfolio companies, top users spend about $7,500 to $9,000 per month on AI versus a median of $200 to $400, over 20x.
data_point🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediuma16z portfolio
So what: Per-user AI spend has no near ceiling, expanding the revenue pool for token consumers.
AI tool spend relative to headcount cost is about 1% at forward-leaning Fortune 500 companies versus as high as 10% at the most AI-native portfolio companies.
data_point🗣 Santiago Rodriguezconviction: mediumFortune 500
So what: Gap shows how much diffusion remains if laggards converge.
Chime reports reducing cost to serve by over 10% a year for four years (nearly 50% compounded), supported by Decagon.
data_point🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumChimeDecagon
So what: Hard public-company evidence of AI cost savings.
Shopify's AI Sidekick raised the share of merchants reaching five orders within 15 days of onboarding by 8%, a metric tied to retention.
data_point🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumShopify
So what: AI shows revenue-side impact via activation.
ServiceNow has reported more than $1 billion in AI ACV and a 9x increase in agentic deployments.
data_point🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: mediumServiceNow
So what: Incumbents are monetizing AI, not just startups.
Companies choosing to spend incremental AI dollars on efficiency rather than new revenue products reveal they see less opportunity; revenue upside is unbounded while cost savings are a latent, finite opportunity.
framework🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumStripe
So what: Use where CEOs direct AI dollars as a litmus test for opportunity.
Agent token usage on OpenRouter grew 14x, and caching has made Hebbia's financial chat workloads 10x cheaper to run.
data_point🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumOpenRouterHebbia
So what: Falling per-step costs make multi-step agents economical.
Databricks' smart router solves more problems at 35% lower cost than the strongest single model, and Elise AI's fine-tuned smaller model was 60% cheaper with much lower latency.
data_point🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumDatabricksElise AIHarvey
So what: Routing and fine-tuning are margin levers for application companies.
Application companies should price on the customer's job done rather than model cost, so better routing lets them expand margins by not using the most expensive model for every step.
thesis🗣 Santiago Rodriguezconviction: mediumapplication layer
So what: Expect gross margin improvement at the best app companies.
A two-orders-of-magnitude cost drop (cited from newer labs) is a classic Jevons paradox that would unlock use cases currently prohibitively expensive.
causal🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: mediumJevons paradox
So what: Falling inference cost expands total demand rather than shrinking spend.
Foundation labs and consumer AI 7
Companies in the foundation model layer have together raised over $350 billion, and OpenAI and Anthropic's combined annualized revenue and net new revenue added exceed those of the best software companies ever built.
data_point🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: highOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Lab revenue ramp is faster than any software precedent.
Only just over 2% of US households have a paying AI subscription, though AI subscriptions show some of the best retention ever seen in consumer, with smile-shaped curves.
data_point🗣 Santiago Rodriguezconviction: mediumAI subscriptions
So what: Low penetration with improving retention suggests long runway versus Amazon Prime (200M+ households) or Netflix (70M).
Persistent proactive AI agents working in the background will not show up as screen time, making outside-in engagement data less useful for evaluating consumer AI.
causal🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumAI agents
So what: Traditional engagement metrics (time spent) will mismeasure agent products.
Newer AI assistants along with ChatGPT are taking more queries away from traditional search, so consumer marketplaces must assess how much incremental demand agents can bring and how much profit comes from owning discovery.
thesis🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumChatGPTsearch
So what: Marketplaces face a strategic choice on agent access.
Amazon declined an agent integration while Instacart accepted; Amazon's advertising (over $70 billion) outpaces its operating profit ex-AWS, whereas Instacart has more online grocery penetration upside from incremental orders.
anecdote🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumAmazonInstacart
So what: Players dependent on ad-funded discovery resist agents; those needing orders embrace them.
Google's search resilience came partly because high-monetizing queries like insurance and hotels required action AI could not yet take; if AI can act on users' behalf this changes.
causal🗣 Santiago Rodriguezconviction: mediumGoogleMeta
So what: Agentic action capability is the real risk to search ad pools.
Lower-friction agent ordering could raise GMV and consumption, so profit pools may be reallocated while the overall economy gains, and the team expects meaningful macro productivity improvement within three years.
thesis🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumGMVproductivity
So what: Positive-sum view; advertising pools could migrate to agent channels.
Public software, SaaS apocalypse and private markets 12
Roughly 75% of public software companies in the sample are profitable but only 30% grow 20% or more, and fewer than five grow above 30%, while nearly every private company a16z sees grows well above 30%.
data_point🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: highpublic software
So what: Public software mix has shifted to slower-growth; growth is migrating to private AI-era companies.
IT budgets fund AI by cutting from or not adding to new SaaS projects, so incumbents must apply AI to existing distribution and show 98% gross dollar retention plus revenue growth acceleration to dispel SaaS-apocalypse fears.
causal🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: highSaaS
So what: Watch for revenue acceleration (target 10%+) at incumbents over 12 to 18 months.
The fear that everyone would vibe code their own software systems is clearly not what is happening in the market, with internal system-of-record rebuilds a poor use of engineering resources.
contrarian🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: mediumvibe codingsystems of record
So what: Build-vs-buy threat to SaaS has been overstated.
Cybersecurity, observability and vertical software have held up much better than horizontal applications, because AI-created agents and software generate new security and monitoring needs, as with CrowdStrike.
framework🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: highCrowdStrikecybersecurityobservability
So what: Screen software by how AI changes the customer's need for the product (Ali Ghodsi's 'chopping block').
The software index is back to where it began the year after an early-year SaaS apocalypse, but bifurcated into perceived AI winners and losers, with winners accelerating and capturing net new dollars.
data_point🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumsoftware index
So what: Dispersion within software is the opportunity.
Stripe's SaaS customer data, which Stripe calls the 'renaissance', shows growth accelerating into 2026 across both young and mature businesses.
data_point🗣 Sarah Wangconviction: mediumStripe
So what: Private operating data contradicts the narrative that SaaS is in decline.
Vertical AI companies such as Harvey, Abridge and EliseAI are growing faster than any precedents in their industries, which shows customers value orchestration and vertical workflows beyond the model.
thesis🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumHarveyAbridgeEliseAI
So what: Vertical AI remains a prime venture area.
The top six private companies by last round valuation (Anthropic, OpenAI, Databricks, Stripe, Waymo, Revolut) total about 2.4 trillion dollars, more than the combined market cap of IPOs in the last 10 years excluding SpaceX, and nearly the size of the Russell 2000.
data_point🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumAnthropicOpenAIDatabricksStripeWaymoRevolutRussell 2000
So what: Value creation is staying private longer; late-stage private is a major asset class.
a16z views the founder as the asset class and the IPO as just another financing event, with private status allowing longer-duration bets, while public listing can help trust, as Navan reaccelerated after going public.
thesis🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumNavan
So what: Stay-private vs IPO is a founder-specific tradeoff.
Carta data show only 58% participation in tender offers, meaning employees choose not to sell because of conviction; a16z also favors frequent valuation resets for fresh M&A currency.
data_point🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumCarta
So what: Employee holding behavior signals confidence; tenders compete with public RSUs.
Secondary-market discounts to last-round prices are now basically zero, versus meaningful discounts in 2021 to 2024.
data_point🗣 Alex Emermanconviction: mediumsecondary markets
So what: Private valuations are viewed as fresh with net new buyers at the same price.
AI-related companies account for 86% of US VC deal activity in the 2026 snapshot, up from 65% in 2025.
data_point🗣 David Georgeconviction: highventure capital
So what: Venture is now almost entirely an AI allocation.
Next investment areas 3
a16z believes robotics could be larger than LLMs but probably 3 to 5 years earlier in its cycle, making it a massive investment area over the next five years.
prediction🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumroboticsLLMs
So what: Early positioning in robotics is a core growth-team priority.
Uber and Lyft represent about 1% of US miles traveled; with autonomous networks 14 times safer than humans, a16z expects this to expand at least an order of magnitude, and thinks the 17 million new US cars sold per year will all be autonomous within 10 years.
prediction🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumUberLyftautonomous driving
So what: Autonomy could expand ride-hailing's share of miles dramatically.
American dynamism vendors such as Anduril, Saronic and Castelion are still less than 5% of military dollars spent, which a16z expects to grow dramatically.
prediction🗣 David Georgeconviction: mediumAndurilSaronicCastelion
So what: Defense retooling remains early-stage share gain for new vendors.
We Tested the New Wave of Personal AI Agents50 min33 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
a16z host and Assistant Bench author David Poland review the new wave of consumer personal AI agents (Instinct, Muse, Poke, ChatGPT voice): use cases, proactivity as the moat, multiplayer design, agentic commerce winners and losers (Amazon vs Shopify), and brutal unit economics against free incumbents.
Personal agent landscape and Assistant Bench data · 6What consumers actually use agents for · 9Surfaces, hardware and character design · 7Multiplayer agents and agent-to-agent systems · 3Agentic commerce and platform economics · 4Unit economics and startup strategy · 4
Personal agent landscape and Assistant Bench data 6
Assistant Bench, a consumer-facing benchmark launched 16 days before recording, compares personal AI assistants on identical one-shot prompts (e.g. book a flight to Chicago, find a vegetarian restaurant within five blocks) across 16 dimensions and drew over 100,000 visitors.
data_point🗣 David Polandconviction: highAssistant Bench
So what: Heavy organic interest signals a fast-forming, undecided consumer-agent category.
David Poland counts 122 personal-assistant products across categories, with about 64 in the general B2C category, and has personally tested 26 of them.
data_point🗣 David Polandconviction: highAssistant Bench
So what: The category is extremely fragmented, implying a coming shakeout.
Most assistants are horizontal generalists trying to do everything; specialized variants exist in travel (Sore, Miso) and email, and B2B executive-assistant style workflow products (Vellum) sit on the other side.
data_point🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumVellumSoreMiso
So what: Vertical niches are thin so far versus the crowded horizontal field.
Of the 122 assistants surveyed, 65 are paid (35 fully paid, 30 freemium) and only 13 are free, yet the dominant players Muse and Instinct are both free.
data_point🗣 David Polandconviction: highMuseInstinctOpenAI
So what: Long-tail paid startups must justify a price against free, subsidized leaders.
The current consumer agent wave is largely a preconfigured replication of Open Claw: little in today's personal assistant space cannot be done with Open Claw or Hermes, the difference being no configuration and a simple interface.
thesis🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumOpen ClawHermes
So what: Moat is packaging and UX, not underlying capability, so differentiation must come elsewhere.
“I honestly see the consumer agent is is literally just a replication of Open Claw, but it's just preconfigured.” ▶ 29:10
The agent cycle is described as a third 'seeing God' moment after ChatGPT (late 2022/2023) and coding agents (Claude Code, Codex), now driven by Instinct, Muse and ChatGPT work for consumer personal agents.
framework🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: mediumChatGPTInstinctMuse
So what: Frames consumer agents as the next platform-wave after chat and coding.
What consumers actually use agents for 9
In group chats of roughly 1,200 assistant enthusiasts, the top use cases are daily admin (inbox cleanup, forms), agent orchestration, development, and finance, while travel ranks only fourth despite being the most viral demo.
data_point🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumAssistant Bench
So what: Viral demos (flight check-in) overstate real usage; sample is tech-Twitter biased.
“Travel is the fourth most talked about use case” ▶ 7:25
Travel, an infrequent high-value behavior, will likely end up as a connector into a horizontal agent rather than supporting many standalone agents, with perhaps one independent winner.
prediction🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumSore
So what: Vertical travel agent startups face a ceiling; value accrues to horizontal agent plus connectors.
The general population does not care about being 10% more efficient; the winning agent works invisibly like a good employee who just gets things done and reports back.
thesis🗣 David Polandconviction: highpersonal agents
So what: Product design should target outcomes and zero management overhead rather than productivity gains.
“The general population does not care about being 10% more efficient.” ▶ 0:00
Cost-saving agent workflows, such as filing a year of receipts for HSA reimbursement, claiming airline travel credit when a ticket price drops, and a Grok bot tied to weather data that cut a friend's water bill by 50%, are the most compelling proactive use cases and fall in finance.
anecdote🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumGrokHSA
So what: Money-back framing is the consumer hook that sells agents.
The hook for mass-market adoption will be agents feeling like free money (HSA refunds, airline price-drop credits) because American consumers do not want to hear about saving money but respond to free money.
thesis🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: mediumDeltaHSA
So what: Marketing agents as refund finders, and consumer-apathy profit pools in finance, are a wedge.
Agent products that are infrequent but high-value in finance or travel may become billion-dollar businesses as vertical connectors rather than general horizontal agents.
prediction🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: lowHSA
So what: Possible exit path for vertical specialists is as connectors.
Finance is the most-discussed but least practical agent use case, since an agent that reliably made money would already be used by hedge funds; users nevertheless first ask how an agent will make them a million dollars.
contrarian🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumhedge funds
So what: Treat 'agent trades for you' claims skeptically; real finance value is admin and savings.
ChatGPT voice, newly able to use connectors (Gmail, calendar), let Poland triage email labels, reply, and send calendar invites over a 30-minute bike ride and reach inbox zero, which he called his second magic moment of the assistant space.
anecdote🗣 David Polandconviction: highChatGPT voiceGmail
So what: Hands-busy voice agents are a strong adoption vector, and OpenAI has a capability lead over Muse and Instinct here.
“categorize my emails, uh submitted them to different labels” ▶ 18:13
ChatGPT voice is based on a GPT live model with full duplex and can see all of a user's threads, a capability neither Muse nor Instinct had, though Meta announced full duplex voice.
data_point🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: mediumChatGPT voiceMuseInstinctMeta
So what: Voice with cross-thread memory is OpenAI's current differentiator; Meta is expected to close the gap.
Surfaces, hardware and character design 7
Agent surfaces are splitting between iMessage and standalone apps, and preference may fragment by generation (Gen Z texting, older users apps) and by gender or use case, so multiple surfaces can coexist.
thesis🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: mediumiMessageInstinct
So what: Distribution through iMessage is valuable but a single dominant interface is unlikely.
Poland's hot take is that Meta's Muse charm is less a hardware play than real-world data collection (camera and microphones, always on) feeding Zuckerberg's future metaverse mapping, with adoption mostly limited to the Twitter bubble.
contrarian🗣 David Polandconviction: lowMetaMuse charmZuckerberg
So what: Wearable agents may be strategic data-gathering for Meta rather than volume products.
“this Muse charm is actually less about trying to win the hardware game” ▶ 0:04
Audio-only smart glasses may be a sleeper hit because users get the connectivity without the social awkwardness of a camera on the face.
prediction🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: lowaudio-only glasses
So what: Camera-free wearables could widen the addressable market.
“I actually think that the audio-only glasses might be a sleeper hit” ▶ 17:02
Agent personality is not a defensible moat because it can be configured by telling the agent to save style preferences to memory or a soul file; Poke's sassy persona put off some users.
thesis🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumPoke
So what: Defensibility must come from capability, not tone.
“I don't think personality is really a defensible moat” ▶ 24:49
Defensibility instead lies in proactivity and decision-making behavior under ambiguity (presumptuous versus cautious agents), and the agent that is most proactive will separate the winner from the pack.
thesis🗣 David Polandconviction: highproactivity
So what: Compete on proactive action with calibrated risk, not chat style.
“There is massive defensibility around proactivity.” ▶ 0:23
Proactive actions that need no user action (drafting email, claiming a flight credit) are safe, but actions that change the user's position (switching insurance) require permission, and crossing that line once destroys all user trust.
framework🗣 David Polandconviction: highPoke
So what: Trust-boundary design is the central product risk for proactive agents.
“if you cross that line once, I think you immediately you lose all trust with your user” ▶ 27:15
Model progress on verifiable domains like coding is exponential, but prose quality and bedside manner have plateaued or worsened, limiting how much agents can enhance social group dynamics.
contrarian🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: mediumCodex
So what: Social/EQ agents lag utilitarian ones, so utilitarian multiplayer agents are the near-term bet.
Multiplayer agents and agent-to-agent systems 3
Three multiplayer approaches exist: adding an agent to a group chat, Instinct's agent network where agents converse behind the scenes, and Doc by Shane Mac (XMTP, an a16z company), a silent note-taking listener that pings individuals privately with action items.
framework🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumDocShane MacXMTPInstincta16z
So what: Invisible-listener design avoids the social cost of an intrusive bot in the chat.
Agent-native infrastructure that exists only for agent-to-agent interaction is emerging: agent emails, phone numbers, agent-to-agent service booking, and a new agent security industry analogous to cybersecurity following the internet.
prediction🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumInstinct
So what: Startup opportunity in identity, communication and security rails for agents.
“It does feel like there's going to be infrastructure and products that exist only for agent-to-agent interactions” ▶ 0:44
Agents could create a faster, higher-frequency secondary economy where agents act for humans (for example messaging a grandma's agent to knit custom socks), and recommendation quality becomes the key layer with Meta advantaged by Instagram and Facebook preference data.
prediction🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: lowMetaEtsy
So what: Agent-to-agent marketplaces open long-tail supply; preference data is a moat.
Agentic commerce and platform economics 4
Amazon blocked Muse while Shopify embraced it because Amazon earns heavily from ad revenue and agents purchasing remove human eyeballs, whereas Shopify benefits from democratizing selling.
causal🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumAmazonShopifyMuse
So what: Ad-funded marketplaces face disintermediation by agents and need a new monetization paradigm; open-commerce platforms gain.
“when you remove human eyeballs, it defeats the entire purpose of the system” ▶ 40:39
Amazon is rightly betting it must avoid being disintermediated, since it would lose both advertising revenue and impulse shopping, though agents themselves may become near-rational impulse buyers so marketing and advertising are transformed rather than eliminated.
thesis🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: mediumAmazon
So what: Expect advertising to target agents and their principals' sign-off rather than disappear.
Resy began shutting accounts using browser-use bots, and if every consumer has an agent, restaurants may gain power to allocate tables by loyalty, average order value and lifetime value, so proprietary supply grows in importance while demand-constrained categories shift to reverse auctions where merchants bid.
prediction🗣 David Polandconviction: mediumResyrestaurants
So what: Supply-constrained platforms must price friction away; demand-aggregation with merchant bidding is a new model.
Agents could produce a more consumer-aligned internet by choosing products on assessed quality rather than marketing and brand, with brand choice only on the user's explicit sign-off.
thesis🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: mediumagentic commerce
So what: Brand advertising spend loses power if agents choose on quality.
Unit economics and startup strategy 4
Running an ambitious consumer agent costs roughly $20 per user per day, potentially hundreds of millions a year for a startup, and browser use is expected to become exponentially cheaper, making free offerings viable later.
data_point🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: mediumbrowser use
So what: Startups launching now are betting on cost deflation within about six months, a game of chicken against free incumbents.
“it's costing something like $20 per user per day” ▶ 48:25
Rather than subsidized free products, the host wants to see a $1,000-per-month agent compelling enough that consumers pay for it, benefiting from both strong product-market fit and deflating costs.
thesis🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: lowMuse
So what: High-ARPU premium agents are an alternative to competing with free.
The 'narrow startups' thesis: software can be ambitious and highly valuable for a small number of users paying $200-$300 per month, enabling roughly $100M run-rate businesses with tens of thousands of customers, with advantage from taste and proprietary knowledge in specialized agents (e.g. for single mothers of young children, a New York concierge).
thesis🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: mediumnarrow startups
So what: Hyper-specialized agents with proprietary data are viable businesses next to horizontal giants.
Poke, now part of Cognition, was clever in launching in early September and might have won had it started six months later.
anecdote🗣 Host (a16z)conviction: lowPokeCognition
So what: Early movers in consumer agents risk being too early for the primitives to mature.

Peter H. Diamandis 2 episode(s) · 57 insights

Cathie Wood on Tesla-SpaceX Merger, $1M Bitcoin, More AIs Than Humans | EP #296 | Moonshots Live60 min38 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Cathie Wood and a Circle executive discuss on stage a likely Tesla-SpaceX merger, 7-8% real GDP growth, agents outnumbering humans next year, stablecoins under the GENIUS Act taking effect in January, a 1M Bitcoin target, and AI in healthcare as the most undervalued theme.
Tesla-SpaceX merger and Musk's compute thesis · 4AI-driven growth, productivity and US-China framing · 6AI agents as economic actors and agentic infrastructure · 9Stablecoins, USDC, GENIUS Act and Circle's ARC chain · 8Bitcoin outlook · 3Robotics, autonomy and healthcare · 6Policy, narrative and energy · 2
Tesla-SpaceX merger and Musk's compute thesis 4
Cathie Wood's team expects a Tesla-SpaceX merger to be announced this year, combining rockets, satellites, AI, EVs, robotaxis, humanoid robots and energy under one roof; she says both Tesla and SpaceX are her largest holdings and that they will be combined.
prediction🗣 Cathie Woodconviction: highTeslaSpaceXElon Musk
So what: Merger optionality is already embedded in her positioning; watch for a 2026 announcement and dilution terms.
“we do think it's going to happen.” ▶ 1:36
Wood argues Musk's long-term goal is Mars, with a global broadband connectivity business as an interim stop that is already very large and profitable in revenue terms.
thesis🗣 Cathie Woodconviction: mediumSpaceXElon MuskMars
So what: Satellite broadband is the cash engine funding the larger ambitions inside a combined entity.
Wood says Musk believes the frontier-model leader will be whoever has the most compute at the lowest cost, and that orbital data centers will make SpaceX that company, putting it on a path to compete directly with OpenAI and Anthropic after the pivot of xAI into a neocloud business.
thesis🗣 Cathie Woodconviction: mediumSpaceXxAIOpenAIAnthropicorbital data centersElon Musk
So what: Bull case for the merger rests on compute cost advantage via space-based infrastructure, not just EV/robot synergy.
The main merger roadblock Wood identifies is Tesla's China business set against Musk's defense-department ties; she reads Musk sitting alongside Trump and Xi as working all angles, including talk of a robotaxi system in China.
anecdote🗣 Cathie Woodconviction: lowTeslaChinaElon MuskDonald TrumpXi Jinping
So what: US-China politics is the likeliest friction point for a combined defense/China-exposed entity.

Anthropic's Wet Lab, Artificial Wombs in 24 Months, and Scalable Gene Editing | MOONSHOTS Live #29743 min19 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Colossal Biosciences CEO Ben Lamm discusses de-extinction progress, scaling multiplex gene edits from 20 to 300+ (testing 1,000), a 24-month target for mammalian artificial wombs, and why proprietary genomic data plus wet-lab validation matter as frontier AI labs like Anthropic enter biology.
Multiplex gene editing scaling · 4Artificial wombs and next milestones · 2AI and biology: data over models · 5Anthropic's wet lab and industry reaction · 1Biodiversity backup and funding · 3De-extinction scope and ethics · 3Mammoth climate hypothesis · 1
Multiplex gene editing scaling 4
Colossal says it now consistently delivers 300+ simultaneous edits at 90%+ efficiency, up from roughly 20 edits that most biopharma and academia would treat as a victory, and is testing 1,000-edit deliveries.
data_point🗣 Ben Lammconviction: mediumColossal
So what: Edit-count scaling is the capability curve underpinning large-genome engineering; a 1,000-edit success would be a step change over the field.
“we are delivering 300 plus at 90 plus% efficiency consistently.” ▶ 20:46
Lamm expects DNA synthesis of multi-gigabase constructs to replace multiplex editing sooner than editing will reach arbitrarily many edits, because large cargo swaps via synthesis become better past a certain scale.
prediction🗣 Ben Lammconviction: mediumColossalDNA synthesis
So what: Value may shift from editing delivery toward synthesis machines; Colossal has begun doing synthesis internally because few others have a business driver at that scale.
“I think synthesis will replace multiplex editing faster” ▶ 21:51
The first-generation woolly mouse shown to the public required eight base edits in a single delivery.
data_point🗣 Ben Lammconviction: highColossalwoolly mouse
So what: Gives a concrete baseline of how modest the edit count was for the public proof point versus the 300+ capability.
Lamm predicts that within roughly a decade it will be possible to engineer key phenotypes in base-level organisms by multiplex editing or synthesis and grow them ex utero, approaching a 'prompt an animal' capability, though he says DNA synthesis is not there yet.
prediction🗣 Ben Lammconviction: mediumColossal
So what: A decade-scale horizon for programmable organisms frames timelines for synthetic biology tooling and regulation.
Artificial wombs and next milestones 2
Lamm predicts Colossal will birth mammals fully ex utero, from gestation through delivery without ever entering a surrogate, within 24 months, and hopes sooner.
prediction🗣 Ben Lammconviction: highColossal
So what: A dated, falsifiable milestone for mammalian artificial gestation, which would also be a platform for conservation and potentially other uses.
“I I think within 24 months we will colossal will birth animal mammals um fully exudo” ▶ 22:33
Lamm names the next major Colossal inflection points as revealing another extinct species via precision gene editing, then mammalian artificial development and gestation, plus undisclosed projects he says are as notable as the dire wolves.
prediction🗣 Ben Lammconviction: mediumColossaldire wolf
So what: Signals the company's news-flow catalysts for the coming period.
AI and biology: data over models 5
Lamm argues that published biology is hard to reuse because genes are named, classified and experiments run differently across mouse papers, giving a 40-60% failure rate when exact experiments are rerun; AI's near-term value is bridging this nomenclature and communication layer.
causal🗣 Ben Lammconviction: mediummouse studiesPubMed
So what: Literature-mining and normalization is a tractable near-term AI use case in biology outside small-molecule discovery.
Lamm expects AI in biology over the next 3-5 years to mostly run simulation and design across many experiments and suggest the next experiment, while wet-lab validation remains necessary.
prediction🗣 Ben Lammconviction: mediumAnthropicGinkgo
So what: Wet-lab capacity and automation remain the bottleneck, favoring lab-automation and validation providers over pure in-silico plays.
Lamm argues a single genome fed to a frontier model will not return a recipe for immortality; instead, comparative genomics across thousands of genomes, such as species resistant to disease, is needed, and Colossal is testing this on cases like p53 and immortal jellyfish.
contrarian🗣 Ben Lammconviction: mediumMythosp53immortal jellyfish
So what: Proprietary, large-scale comparative genomic datasets, not generic model access, are the differentiator in genetic design.
“I do not think that a single genome is going to uh like you're not going to feed it into mythos” ▶ 8:43
Colossal's Biovault initiative aims to build global biobanks with governments to enable taxa-wide sequencing, and Lamm frames the resulting dataset as the key asset that complements frontier models.
thesis🗣 Ben Lammconviction: mediumColossalBiovault
So what: Data aggregation via government partnerships is Colossal's moat strategy; 'the data set is more valuable than the model' is echoed by the hosts.
Astromech, a Colossal spin-off, is building 'inflection' models that plug into frontier foundation models, using evolutionary and climate context to guide where to focus, rather than competing with frontier labs.
thesis🗣 Ben Lammconviction: mediumAstromechColossalAnthropic Mythos
So what: Positions niche-data models as complements/routers to frontier models, a pattern for vertical AI startups.

Mammoth climate hypothesis 1
Per George Church as relayed in the discussion, mammoths removing trees would let grass grow in the tundra, which sequesters about six times more carbon than trees and gives a 2-3x albedo effect, and Church projects a 6-8 degree summer permafrost cooling; Lamm notes scientists inside Colossal disagree on the magnitude but see net ecosystem benefit.
causal🗣 Dave Blundin / Ben Lammconviction: lowGeorge Churchwoolly mammothpermafrost

The Lawfare Podcast 2 episode(s) · 42 insights

Scaling Laws: Child Safety AI Policy at Scale with Vaishnavi J and Dave Willner49 min19 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Zentropy's Dave Willner and Vice's Vaishnavi J explain open-source, age-banded teen AI safety policies enforced by small policy-following classifiers that were optimized automatically from about 1,200 labeled examples. They argue for age-appropriate design over bans, efficacy measurement of mandated interventions, and structural insulation of safety teams.
Policy-as-prompt classifiers · 6Age-banded teen AI safety · 6Evidence-based regulation and measurement · 4Open-source safety infrastructure · 3
Policy-as-prompt classifiers 6
Zentropy builds classifiers that take a written policy text as input, so changing the policy text changes classifier behavior without the old pipeline of tens of thousands of human-labeled examples and a long retraining loop.
framework🗣 Dave Willnerconviction: highZentropy
So what: Collapses the cost and latency of trust-and-safety policy iteration, opening a commercial niche for policy-following models.
Zentropy's special-purpose small language models match a GPT-5-class model on policy-following classification while being about two orders of magnitude smaller, with basic versions open source and runnable on a decent MacBook Pro.
data_point🗣 Dave Willnerconviction: highZentropyGPT-5MacBook Pro
So what: Small specialized models undercut frontier-model inference cost for high-volume moderation, a cost/latency moat argument for narrow models.
Because the small classifier is so fast, Zentropy built 'optimizers' that automatically rewrite a content policy and score each rewrite by how well the classifier reproduces human labels, turning policy writing into an empirical, testable process.
framework🗣 Dave Willnerconviction: highZentropy
So what: Second-order product: speed of the base model enables meta-tooling (automated policy optimization) that slower, costlier models could not support.
Dave Willner had Claude browse X and gather examples of users asking Grok to undress photos, plus benign Grok requests, and with roughly 1,000 labeled examples and a couple of optimizer passes produced a published policy that was about 98% accurate at detecting nudification attempts, in a few hours over a weekend.
data_point🗣 Dave Willnerconviction: mediumClaudeGrokXZentropy
So what: Shows how cheap it is now to stand up a targeted classifier; the 98% figure is on easy, non-obfuscated data, so it is an upper-bound signal.
Willner argues xAI's Grok nonconsensual-intimate-image scandal was a choice, not a hard technical problem, since detecting nudification requests is an easy classification task and the platform was 'clearly not trying'.
contrarian🗣 Dave Willnerconviction: highxAIGrokX
So what: Frames regulatory and reputational risk for xAI as self-inflicted and cheaply avoidable, raising the bar for 'we couldn't prevent it' defenses.
For the optimizer approach, coverage matters more than volume: a representative set of around 1,000-1,200 labeled examples spanning the expected problem types works better than piling up more examples of the same kind.
data_point🗣 Dave Willnerconviction: highZentropyVice
So what: Lowers the data-labeling barrier to entry for new safety classifiers, reducing demand for large human-labeling operations.
Age-banded teen AI safety 6
Vice and Zentropy released open-source teen AI policies split into two age bands, 13-15 and 13-17 (skewed to older teens), with six policies in total and four enforcement types per band, each independently written and reassessed per age band.
data_point🗣 Vaishnavi Jconviction: highViceZentropy
So what: A concrete, forkable template that platforms facing teen-safety regulation can adopt as a baseline.
Age-banded scaffolding lowers the cost of inaccurate age-assurance: if a model misjudges a 16-year-old as 14, the teen simply gets the 13-15 experience rather than an all-or-nothing outcome, so age gating works more like a room with progressively more access than a switch.
causal🗣 Vaishnavi Jconviction: mediumage assurance
So what: Argues that graded policies make imperfect age-estimation models regulatorily and practically viable.
Policies on suicide and self-injury must be reweighted by age because of competing effects: the Werther contagion effect should weigh more for younger teens, while the Papageno protective effect of help-seeking is more valuable for older teens, so each policy has to be reassessed per age band rather than simply made more or less conservative.
framework🗣 Vaishnavi Jconviction: mediumWerther effectPapageno effect
So what: Shows that uniform conservative rules can be counterproductive; granular age-specific tuning is the product requirement.
A June study cited by Vaishnavi (RAND and JAMA Pediatrics) found 63% of teens using chatbots for mental-health advice told no one, so full bans are unenforceable when use is invisible.
data_point🗣 Vaishnavi Jconviction: mediumRANDJAMA Pediatrics
So what: Undercuts the ban-based regulatory model and favors design-level safeguards inside the products themselves.
The host cited Pew data (Feb 2026) that 36% of teens do not use AI chatbots, 57% of chatbot use is for searching information, 54% for schoolwork help and 12% for emotional support, and only 31% of 13-17 year olds expect AI's societal impact over 20 years to be positive versus 26% negative.
data_point🗣 Host (Scaling Laws)conviction: mediumPew Research
So what: Teen emotional-support use is a minority but material share, and teen sentiment is skeptical, which shapes the regulatory climate.
Early evidence from the UK suggests teen social/AI bans are being circumvented, and age verification carries the tradeoff of sharing sensitive data with third parties who can be hacked.
data_point🗣 Host (Scaling Laws)conviction: lowUKage verification
So what: Supports a policy shift toward age-appropriate design over bans and hard ID-based verification.
Evidence-based regulation and measurement 4
Laws increasingly mandate evaluations, protocols and interventions like parental controls or screen-time limits, but almost nobody measures whether those interventions work; Vaishnavi wants regulators to require efficacy measurement of the interventions themselves.
thesis🗣 Vaishnavi Jconviction: highparental controls
So what: Expect growing demand for independent evaluation and measurement vendors as compliance moves from 'did you ship it' to 'did it work'.
Snap publicly disclosed in 2024 that only 2% of US teens had parental controls turned on, and Vaishnavi says no other company would publish comparable figures.
data_point🗣 Vaishnavi Jconviction: mediumSnap
So what: Low parental-control adoption weakens the regulatory case for control-based solutions and favors default-on safeguards.
“only 2% of all US teens had parental controls turned on” ▶ 36:10
Willner argues that after 15-20 years and many billions of dollars spent on trust and safety, the darker conclusion is that companies have tried fairly hard and the industry is simply not good at it, with useful research locked inside companies and discovery-risk incentives discouraging studies.
contrarian🗣 Dave Willnerconviction: mediumtrust and safety
So what: Implies better tooling and public research, not just more spending or pressure, is the bottleneck.
Willner proposes that regulators focus on power dynamics inside companies, modeled on banks and airlines where safety teams are structurally insulated from commercial pressure, rather than prescriptive safety-practice rules or reporting forms that go stale in an adversarial space.
thesis🗣 Dave Willnerconviction: mediumbanksairlines
So what: A different regulatory design: governance and org-structure mandates could hit large platforms harder than disclosure-style rules; he expects big platforms to dislike it.
Open-source safety infrastructure 3
Vice and Zentropy open-sourced the policies and labeled dataset so platforms can fork them, revalidate against their own data and edit; Vaishnavi says clients will still need forked, customized versions, so giving the baseline away works as an easy entry point rather than charity.
thesis🗣 Vaishnavi Jconviction: mediumViceZentropy
So what: Open baseline as a customer-acquisition funnel for custom policy and classifier work.
ROOST (robust open source safety tools) is a community effort to provide baseline open classification systems and moderator review interfaces; both guests urge frontier labs to donate more open-source safety policies and classifiers, noting current public releases are binary and cover only about five or six categories.
thesis🗣 Vaishnavi Jconviction: mediumROOSTfrontier labs
So what: Shared safety infrastructure could commoditize basic trust-and-safety tooling, pushing vendor differentiation to product-specific tuning.
Willner describes policy work as gardening rather than reaching a perfect policy: the space is adversarial and socially shifting, so the goal is machinery that adapts faster with fewer human externalities.
framework🗣 Dave Willnerconviction: highZentropy
So what: Favors vendors selling continuous adaptation over static rule sets or one-time compliance deliverables.

The gentleman's agreement runs only to January, which Czin relays (via Matt Turpin) as being because Trump will not need it after the midterms; Czin worries about whether China pulls the rare-earth card proactively or tariffs return in a 'trade war 3.0' next year, though he does not think that is the administration's direction.

So what: Post-midterm early 2027 is the window for renewed US-China trade friction risk.

Stop planning for 2027: how OpenAI builds product 90 days at a time | Tara Sesha and Nan Yu (OpenAI)29 min19 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
OpenAI product leaders Tara Sesha and Nan Yu explain planning AI products on a 2-3 month horizon, shipping imperfect features fast, handling enterprise absorption limits, agent design, and predictions for voice, proactive agents and hardware in 2027.
90-day product horizon · 4Ship imperfect, iterate empirically · 3Enterprise adoption and capability overhang · 4Agent design and platform layers · 52027 product predictions · 3
90-day product horizon 4
OpenAI product leader Tara Sesha says the right planning horizon for AI products is about two to three months out: far enough to anticipate model capability, short enough to avoid wrong multi-year predictions.
framework🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Annual 2027 roadmaps for AI-native products are likely to be wrong; shorter cycles are the edge.
“it's like two to three months out” ▶ 20:28
Tara Sesha keeps a personal document of incorrectly predicted AI developments to remind herself she cannot predict years out, yet argues building only for today gets a product left behind.
anecdote🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Both over-anchoring on the present and over-futurism are failure modes.
Planning horizon should depend on market dynamics: Stripe's payments market can be modeled with bull/base/bear cases and annual planning, whereas OpenAI's business cannot be forecast that way.
contrarian🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: mediumStripeOpenAI
So what: Stable-structure businesses keep long plans; AI model-driven businesses cannot.
OpenAI's internal ship bar for a product is whether it aims at where models will be in two to three months, is not overly anchored in the present, and is not so futuristic as to be unusable.
framework🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Products timed to next-generation models capture value as capabilities arrive.
Ship imperfect, iterate empirically 3
Tara Sesha, after about seven years at Stripe's polished culture, says urgency and empirical user feedback now outweigh a priori perfection when shipping AI products.
thesis🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: highStripeOpenAI
So what: Speed of learning from real usage is the competitive variable in AI product development.
“nothing compares to like the actual empirical evidence of seeing users try it, use it, and then iterating from there” ▶ 1:31
OpenAI shipped an imperfect toggle to get an agentic harness to ChatGPT's billion-plus users without disrupting developer workflows, expecting the toggle to be removed in about a year.
anecdote🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: mediumOpenAIChatGPT
So what: Agent distribution via ChatGPT's user base is prioritized over UX elegance; expect UX simplification later.
OpenAI uses internal dogfooding as a quality gate: ship internally and look at retention, delight and novel model use cases; weak internal uptake signals a product should not ship.
framework🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Internal retention is a cheap leading indicator for external product-market fit.
Enterprise adoption and capability overhang 4
Nan Yu says the biggest constraint on AI products is people's ability to absorb capability, since models can do far more than users take advantage of (capability overhang).
thesis🗣 Nan Yu (OpenAI)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Adoption, onboarding and education, not model quality, bound realized value.
“people aren't necessarily taking advantage of all those abilities” ▶ 5:28
OpenAI pushed agents onto enterprises that mostly used ChatGPT for Q&A, knowingly breaking their processes, because failing to ship frontier features risked customers switching to alternative products.
causal🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: highOpenAIChatGPT
So what: Enterprise AI vendors face leapfrog risk, so rapid change is accepted despite customer pain.
“do what your users need, not what your users say they need” ▶ 7:26
Nan Yu argues onboarding matters far more in the AI era because of capability overhang, and users need to understand initial experience and offering.
thesis🗣 Nan Yu (OpenAI)conviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Onboarding quality becomes a differentiator for AI products.
Tara Sesha says agent features that act semi-autonomously require users to understand privacy, data use and product norms so they can predict behavior, replacing reliance on settings menus.
thesis🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Trust and predictability are product requirements for agents.
Agent design and platform layers 5
Nan Yu argues managing around 40 agents is unrealistic for human cognition; users in practice add a chief-of-staff agent, so agent products should bundle activity into a few managed groups.
thesis🗣 Nan Yu (OpenAI)conviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Hierarchical orchestrator agents are the likely dominant multi-agent UX.
“you're not managing seven you're managing one” ▶ 9:20
Tara Sesha says single versus multi-identity agent design depends on use-case specifics like data permissions, service-account versus user credentials, and segmented memory in private channels.
framework🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: mediumSlack
So what: Agent architecture choices hinge on permissions and memory scoping, an enterprise-grade concern.
OpenAI's platform approach layers capabilities: native functionality, plugins and third-party hooks, then computer use as fallback, so a task can always be completed even without integrations.
framework🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: mediumOpenAIChatGPTcodecscomputer use
So what: Computer use is a universal fallback that reduces dependence on third-party integrations and consumes extra tokens.
Nan Yu says an agent that gets 99 percent of a job done then fails is worse than a non-starter, and computer use is valued because it reliably completes the job even if slow and token-costly.
causal🗣 Nan Yu (OpenAI)conviction: mediumcomputer useMCP
So what: Reliability to completion beats elegance; computer use drives token consumption for model vendors.
“the last mile honestly feels sometimes worse than just like it's a non-starter” ▶ 15:56
Tara Sesha says working with research requires bringing specific user session examples and writing evals that show a prompt-and-skills outcome, which can then be post-trained into the model.
framework🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Product-written evals are the interface feeding user needs into model training.
“writing evals as much as possible is like the most important thing” ▶ 18:47
2027 product predictions 3
Tara Sesha predicts voice will be the major AI interface shift in 2027, citing GPT live, reduced family tech support, and ease onboarding OpenAI's people team.
prediction🗣 Tara Sesha (OpenAI)conviction: highGPT liveOpenAI
So what: Voice interfaces gain share as models improve in speed and quality.
“Voice I'm like so bullish on voice” ▶ 27:41
Nan Yu predicts self-driving-like products that use themselves on the user's behalf will solve the empty-input-box problem and become standard within a year.
prediction🗣 Nan Yu (OpenAI)conviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Proactive agents lower onboarding friction.
“empty input box problems” ▶ 28:01
Host Claire predicts hardware replacing the laptop, a small Codex-in-a-box device, will be a hot product in 2027.
prediction🗣 Claire (host)conviction: exploratorycodecs

Why Claude can’t be your PM (yet) | Anthropic CPO Panel29 min22 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Anthropic product leaders (Amy and Mike) at a Lenny's Podcast live panel argue the PM role persists as a convenor/judgment function even as building gets cheap, describe agent-native and malleable software, and explain how Anthropic runs overlapping bets, evals for parked projects, and consolidation.
PM role persists as convenor · 5Skills made obsolete by cheap building · 3Agent-native and malleable software · 4Exploring the frontier while serving a broad base · 5Product consolidation lessons · 3Outlook for the next year · 2
PM role persists as convenor 5
Anthropic's product leadership argues the PM role is not disappearing: the technology changes every two months while human problems change slowly, so the PM job of bridging real human problems and current technology persists.
thesis🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: highAnthropic
So what: AI-driven PM obsolescence narrative is overstated; the bridge function persists.
“the technology side is changing but the human side like the human side doesn't change that fast” ▶ 1:30
At Anthropic, a builder who moved to an IC role and assumed Claude could cover product management found a dedicated PM was still needed to supply glue and connective tissue, such as enabling customer success, safeguards and keeping people on track, before a launch serving prosumers through large enterprises.
anecdote🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: highAnthropicClaude
So what: Even at the most AI-native firm, launch coordination across customer segments is still a human job.
“all of the things and all the glue and all of the connective tissue” ▶ 3:10
Because AI lets teams move faster, the coordinating PM role requires greater operational excellence than before, and Anthropic sees this hat as increasingly important when recruiting for teams.
thesis🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: mediumAnthropic
So what: Operational execution, not ideation, is the scarce PM skill.
Anthropic's leaders say Claude is not yet a convenor: no one has seen Claude schedule a meeting among humans unprompted, though the limit is a settings choice rather than capability, and the 'convenor' is one archetype for the human role in a post-powerful-AI world.
thesis🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: mediumClaudeAnthropic
So what: Agentic coordination across people is a near-term capability that is gated by product choices, not models.
In a world where building is cheap and fast, the scarce skills are judgment about what to build on limited information and relentlessness in execution amid many forking paths.
thesis🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: high
So what: Hiring and evaluation should shift toward judgment and follow-through.
“judgment in the face of ambiguity” ▶ 6:45
Skills made obsolete by cheap building 3
A veteran product leader says the skill of agonizing over interaction details, such as button placement and thumb reach, is now obsolete because building three versions and testing them is faster than debating.
causal🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: high
So what: The cost of building collapsing devalues upfront product-design deliberation in favor of rapid prototyping.
“now it's actually faster to just build three versions and try them out” ▶ 8:30
Expertise-based identity creates a personal innovator's dilemma: people keep doing what they are best at after the technology makes it less important, so product leaders must throw away what they know about themselves in the job every couple of months.
framework🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: medium
So what: Adaptability, not accumulated craft, is the durable differentiator for talent.
“innovator's dilemma” ▶ 8:47
Anthropic leaders recommend 'framing the chaos' rather than mapping exact roadmaps and careers in uncertainty, since over-planning locks you out of trying new things, while pairing that chaos with clear DRIs such as 'bet leads' in Labs who decide to double down or wind down.
framework🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: mediumAnthropic
So what: Fast-moving orgs pair exploratory freedom with unambiguous decision owners.
Agent-native and malleable software 4
Anthropic's stated design principle for agent-native software is that everything a human can do in the product an agent should also be able to do, which unlocks emergent behavior where agents piece together and proactively offer new workflows; very few products get this right today.
framework🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: highAnthropic
So what: Incumbent SaaS with UI-only functionality is structurally behind agent-native entrants.
“everything a human could do an agent should be able to do as well” ▶ 13:31
Anthropic's next stage beyond agent-native is malleable interfaces that the agent can reshape, and internally much of the software employees use is now built, maintained and iterated on by Claude, such as a Claude-generated project status UI for a team with four independent workstreams.
data_point🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: highAnthropicClaude
So what: Internal tooling is shifting from bought or built-by-acceleration-team software to Claude-generated bespoke software, a threat to horizontal internal-tool vendors.
Malleable Claude-built software raises unresolved questions about who may update data (humans clicking versus Claude in the background) and about data provenance.
exploratory🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: exploratoryClaude
So what: Data governance and provenance become product features when agents write to systems of record.
For SaaS companies deciding whether to add agents, the advice is to build shared primitives or plumbing so agents and the REST API use the same paths; products show when this was designed in versus bolted on, which is hard for apps built over 20 years.
thesis🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: medium
So what: Legacy SaaS with entrenched architecture faces a costly retrofit to be agent-native.
Exploring the frontier while serving a broad base 5
Anthropic encourages overlapping parallel product bets and says the failure mode is over-constraining a product from a theory upfront; Amy would rather have five overlapping products than one overly constrained one, consolidating after product-market fit.
thesis🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: highAnthropic
So what: Expect messy, duplicative Anthropic product surfaces near term, followed by consolidation of winners.
“I would much rather have like five overlapping products” ▶ 19:17
Anthropic found that chat and co-work had different memory systems, MCP setups and file storage, so a Foundations team built shared infrastructure such as portable memory, letting experimental surfaces feel complementary even when overlapping.
data_point🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: mediumAnthropicMCP
So what: Shared memory and context layers are becoming the moat across Anthropic's product surfaces.
Anthropic Labs treats a project that only proves models are not ready as a win if it is externalized as an eval, then retried with each new model; its 2024 computer use product was too slow and error-prone for automation or education, and running it in an eval harness showed a model referred to as "37" to be a leap in computer use.
anecdote🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: highAnthropicPhotoshop
So what: Parked projects plus evals let a lab detect capability step-changes early; computer-use agents became viable with a specific model release.
People get capability blind: they try a task once, conclude models cannot do it and never retest, yet new models improve in unexpected ways within about three months, and many users still judge AI using older models.
contrarian🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: mediumFableAstraSonnet
So what: Skeptical assessments from users on stale models understate current capability; the adoption and capability gap is an opportunity.
“capability blind” ▶ 23:55
In an audience poll, many attendees had used Fable while fewer had used Astra.
data_point🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: lowFableAstra
So what: Rough anecdotal signal that the audience of product practitioners adopted Fable far more widely than Astra.
Product consolidation lessons 3
Instagram showed a strong power law in tab usage, with the main feed about 80% of use and Explore perhaps rising from 10% to 15%, so folding successful experiments into a product means defending the main experience and saying no to extra tabs or sidebar items.
data_point🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: mediumInstagram
So what: Most usage concentrates in one primary surface, so new AI features should be integrated rather than added as tabs.
“main feed was like 80% of the use” ▶ 26:27
The unsolved problem of turning parallel AI experiments into a coherent product, whether as tabs or separate apps, is something neither Anthropic nor OpenAI has fully figured out.
exploratory🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: lowAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Product packaging of AI capabilities remains an open competitive question across labs.
A team without conviction assigned to a manufactured product direction produces a poor product; Anthropic backs teams with high conviction in their own direction rather than assigning B-team explorations.
causal🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: mediumAnthropic
So what: Bottom-up conviction determines which bets get resourced.
“I've never been like successful tasking a team to go like take a product area that they're not excited about” ▶ 20:13
Outlook for the next year 2
Anthropic's stated goal for the coming year is closing the gap between what models can do and what most people use them for, so that researchers and business managers, not only heavy-agent software engineers, run long-horizon multi-agent work without feeling disempowered.
prediction🗣 Mike (Anthropic builder)conviction: mediumAnthropic
So what: Product-layer packaging, rather than raw model capability, is the main bottleneck to mass adoption.
“the gap between what the models are capable of and like what most folks are using them for” ▶ 28:01
Amy expects a year from now more small teams and solo builders starting businesses and building as AI tools make building easier, with benefits flowing to people who never use the tools directly.
prediction🗣 Amy (Anthropic product leader)conviction: medium
So what: Expect growth in solo-founder and small-team companies.

Unchained · Laura Shin 2 episode(s) · 41 insights

How a Third-Party Zero-Day Let Hackers Drain $388 Million From Bitget66 min27 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Bitget CEO Grace Chen explains the Sept 24 $388M hot/warm wallet hack via a third-party zero-day, the two attack rounds, near-zero freezes ($632K), a $556M protection fund, and ongoing social-engineering attempts. She also fields questions on ThorChain/Near laundering responses and ZachXBT's allegations.
Attack mechanics and timeline · 7Laundering, freezes and recovery · 8Balance sheet, protection fund and user flows · 4Social engineering and North Korea · 5Reputation and ZachXBT dispute · 3
Attack mechanics and timeline 7
Bitget's $388M hack began on 24 Sept with test withdrawals of 0.48 ETH from the Ethereum hot wallet and 93 TRX from the Tron hot wallet at 18:31 GMT, both below the risk-control threshold, so no alert fired.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: highBitget
So what: Attackers probe below alert thresholds first; threshold-based monitoring is blind to staged probing.
Starting 18:58 GMT the attacker executed 17 large transfers across chains and assets totaling about $361M, and Bitget's reconciliation system flagged the discrepancy 7 minutes after the first large transfer and auto-blocked all user withdrawals.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: highBitget
So what: Detection was fast, yet most of the loss still occurred; detection without containment of the wallet service is insufficient.
The attacker used an undisclosed third-party zero-day in a security product Bitget had adopted to harden security, gained access to a critical internal management system, impersonated users to create fraudulent withdrawal orders, and injected them directly into the wallet service, which executed them.
causal🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: highBitget
So what: Security vendors themselves are a supply-chain attack surface; the compromise was at the hot/warm wallet orchestration layer, not a cold-wallet signing flow.
After the transfers the attackers deleted traces of the fraudulent withdrawal orders, forcing investigators to examine a wider set of systems to find the root cause.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: highBitget
So what: Log tampering lengthens incident response and delays containment.
A second round of roughly $30M was stolen between 20:55 and 21:23 because Bitget consolidated hot and warm wallet funds into cold wallets, re-enabling wallet transfers while it could not rule out private key compromise; the last malicious transaction was at 21:23, under three hours after the first.
causal🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumBitget
So what: Emergency fund sweeps can reopen the attack window if the compromise is not yet scoped, a trade-off incident responders face.
Bitget says its hack differs from Bybit's: Bybit's was a clear single cause (Safe wallet interface affecting a cold wallet), whereas Bitget's involved hot and warm wallets, multiple systems and multiple blockchains, making root-cause analysis harder.
framework🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumBitgetBybit
So what: Hot-wallet operational compromises are more diffuse and slower to diagnose than cold-wallet UI attacks.
Bitget declined to name the vendor or vulnerability, said it accepts full responsibility rather than blaming the third party, and promised an incident report within about a week while having notified the vendor of the flaw.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumBitget
So what: Watch the incident report for vendor identity; other exchanges using the same product could be exposed to the same zero-day.
Laundering, freezes and recovery 8
Only about $632K of the $388M stolen was frozen as of the interview, with Tether and Circle named as main freezers, which implies near-total laundering success so far.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: highBitgetTetherCircle
So what: Recovery odds are very low (about 0.16%), consistent with prior large hacks.
Bitget published a dashboard tracking all stolen-fund addresses, offered a 5% bounty for voluntarily freezing and another 5% for returning funds, and will pay it even to parties that already froze funds or donate it if they decline, explicitly copying the Bybit playbook.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: highBitgetBybit
So what: The 5%+5% bounty template is becoming the industry standard response to major exchange hacks.
Circle acted quickly on Bitget's case, and Laura Shin noted this contrasted with Drift's roughly $280M incident where, per her earlier episode, it did not act; Bitget's Grace Chen attributed it possibly to rule changes or to the fast publication of all information.
contrarian🗣 Laura Shinconviction: lowCircleDriftBitget
So what: Stablecoin issuer freeze behavior may depend on how quickly and transparently victims publish addresses.
Bitget asked ThorChain to refuse service to hack-linked addresses; ThorChain publicly declined, and rival exchanges accused it of inconsistency because it halted its system when it was itself the victim. Grace Chen said she does not want ThorChain to abandon open source and had not had a direct conversation with its developers.
anecdote🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumThorChainBitget
So what: Permissionless cross-chain swap venues remain the main laundering rails and a governance fault line.
Near Intents built an intelligent shield layer that detects anomalies in data flows and aggregates signals from KYT providers and independent researchers to decide whether a transaction proceeds, while the deployment stays open source; Grace Chen praised this as the ideal compromise and its stance that permissionless does not mean neutral.
framework🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumNear Intent
So what: Compliance layers on top of open-source protocols could become a competitive differentiator and a template regulators favor.
Bitget argued that easy laundering of stolen funds directly increases the number and size of hacks, since attackers face few obstacles cashing out.
causal🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumBitget
So what: Making laundering harder is a deterrence lever; expect pressure on swap protocols and privacy-adjacent venues.
Grace Chen said North Korean laundering typically splits funds into very small chunks (down to about $10,000), uses tiny exchanges such as FixedFloat, and routes through privacy coins like Zcash and Monero; large exchanges like Bitget are not their first choice because of KYC/KYT.
framework🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumFixedFloatZcashMoneroBitget
So what: Small instant-swap services and privacy coins are the likely exit points to watch on-chain.
Bitget floated an industry coalition of the largest exchanges, security firms, intelligence agencies and regulators to prevent laundering of stolen funds.
prediction🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: exploratoryBitget
So what: A formal coalition would be a new industry structure; so far it is only an idea.
Balance sheet, protection fund and user flows 4
Bitget's Protection Fund, created in mid-2022 after the bear-market exchange failures and held in open, on-chain wallets, was used at scale for the first time; it now holds about $556M mostly in BTC and Bitget promised to restore it above $300M (initial target $200M, later raised to $300M) within a week using company capital.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: highBitget
So what: A transparent, self-insurance fund over 1.4x the loss let the exchange absorb the $388M hit without socializing losses; this is a credibility template for exchanges.
Bitget saw lower-than-expected withdrawals after reopening: ETH withdrawals started 08:00 UTC and one hour in, ETH inflow to Bitget was 9,674 ETH versus roughly 9,023 ETH outflow, a net inflow; BTC withdrawals began the day before.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumBitget
So what: No bank-run dynamic after a nine-figure hack suggests user trust anchored by the protection fund.
Grace Chen said the hack did not change Bitget's main financial plans, including a possible IPO in about three years, and that the company has stayed in contact with law enforcement and regulators.
thesis🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumBitget
So what: Management signals the IPO path is intact; regulatory reaction remains the uncertainty.
Grace Chen said three exchanges closed or went bankrupt or withdrew in June and July of this year, which she compared to the stress that motivated Bitget's 2022 protection fund.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: lowBitget
So what: Smaller-exchange attrition is ongoing; scale and reserves are consolidating share to larger venues.
Social engineering and North Korea 5
Grace Chen said the day after the hack, attackers impersonated a major investment firm's team with several actors playing roles and nearly started a real conversation with her before she verified through another known channel with the firm's founder and found it was a scam.
anecdote🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: highBitget
So what: Hackers exploit post-incident chaos when victims seek help from many outside partners; out-of-band identity verification is essential.
Grace Chen said the pattern behind many North Korean attacks begins as social engineering aimed at public figures, who are known to give many interviews and talk to many outside partners, with attackers impersonating those contacts; a request to use an unfamiliar livestream tool instead of Zoom or Google Meet is a red flag.
framework🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumBitget
So what: Executives and media-facing founders are primary targets; unusual meeting platforms are a practical tell.
Bitget's post-incident measures: isolate and take offline all related servers and systems to preserve forensic evidence, revoke and reissue all internal credentials, and require multi-party approvals for critical operations on highly sensitive systems.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumBitget
So what: Standard containment checklist; also an implicit admission those approvals were not sufficiently multi-party before.
Grace Chen conceded Bitget did not do enough due diligence on third-party vendors and will change that, and that there is a tension between sharing details with partners and customers and revealing its security architecture, team and vendors to attackers.
thesis🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: highBitget
So what: Vendor risk management is the lesson; incident reports may be deliberately limited in detail.
Grace Chen said Bitget could not say whether the post-hack social engineering was North Korea or opportunistic scammers, saying it could be both.
anecdote🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: lowBitget
So what: Attribution to North Korea for the main hack is not confirmed in this conversation beyond Laura Shin's framing of hallmarks.
Reputation and ZachXBT dispute 3
Laura Shin recounted that ZachXBT earlier alleged price-manipulation schemes originated on Bitget and Gate, claimed 95% of the Lab token supply was held by insiders and that insiders withdrew about $500M from Bitget, and later tweeted five pseudonyms of Chinese OTC launderers moving North Korean funds from this hack.
data_point🗣 Laura Shinconviction: mediumZachXBTBitget
So what: On-chain sleuths are shifting from hostile to helpful on this case; reputational overhang on Bitget remains.
Grace Chen did not directly respond to the manipulation allegations, saying the tokens are listed on many exchanges (Binance, Gate, Bybit, OKX and others), so manipulation cannot be attributed to Bitget alone, and said Bitget had not paid ZachXBT or talked to him directly.
contrarian🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumBitgetZachXBTOKX
So what: Defense is multi-venue listing, not a rebuttal of the specific claims; the allegations are unresolved.
Bitget works formally with SlowMist and Mandiant on incident reports and informally with volunteer white-hat groups such as Seal 911, and credits their independent tracing.
data_point🗣 Grace Chen (Bitget CEO)conviction: mediumSlowMistMandiantSeal 911Bitget
So what: Bitget outsourced forensics to named firms, so the incident report will carry third-party credibility.
How FTX Taught a Crypto Lawyer Not to Make Crypto Her Identity: DEX in the City56 min14 insights2026-10-01

Final episode of Dex in the City, a panel of three women crypto lawyers (ex-SEC, ex-DOJ, Chainlink GC) offering career advice, keeping perspective through volatility, and recounting the FTX collapse's emotional toll.
