Crypto lawyering as multi-regulator generalism 3
Crypto legal work now spans securities law, the CFTC, EU rules and money-movement regimes at once, because teams build chains and products that combine spot trading, perpetuals, tokens in other countries and prediction markets.
thesis🗣 Jesse/V panel lawyer (guest)conviction: highCFTCEUperpetualsprediction markets

ChinaTalk · Jordan Schneider 1 episode(s) · 41 insights

China's Economy is Broken | Logan Wright88 min41 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Logan Wright (Rhodium Group) argues China's growth is structurally constrained by a decayed financial and fiscal system after the largest credit expansion on record and the property collapse, leaving it reliant on export displacement and a small strategic-industry sector. He concludes the West faces a supply-chain concentration threat rather than a systemic economic rivalry and has underused collective leverage.
Why growth broke: credit and property · 8Fiscal constraint and policy space · 6Exports, deflation and the global surplus · 7Jobs, demographics and the strategic-industry bet · 7Politics: Xi, centralization and reform odds · 8Policy implications for the West · 5
Why growth broke: credit and property 8
China added the equivalent of a third of global GDP to its bank assets in eight years after the global financial crisis, the largest single-country credit expansion in at least a century, and it was externally financed by deposit inflows from FX intervention and reserve accumulation until about 2013.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highChinabanking system
So what: The scale of past credit growth explains why the financial system now constrains growth rather than fuels it.
“China added a third of global GDP to its bank assets in just 8 years” ▶ 6:03
After the 2016-2018 deleveraging campaign, China avoided writing down malinvestment and instead kept state-owned producers and local government financing vehicles alive, so credit became less efficient and generated less growth per unit of credit.
causal🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highlocal government financing vehiclesstate-owned enterprises
So what: Zombie credit allocation, not lack of credit demand per se, is the mechanism of slowdown.
Credit growth fell from an aggregate 18% a year in 2007-2016 to 9% in 2017-2024 and is now about 5%, with bank assets net of government bonds growing 4.9%, RMB loans 5%, and consumer loans outright contracting.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highChinaRMB loans
So what: A roughly 10-point-of-GDP drop in flow of new financing leaves no credit impulse for a consumption rebound.
Property collapse was the proximate but not ultimate cause of China's slowdown: property was 20-25% of GDP at peak, construction has fallen almost 80% and sales about 60%, and nothing has replaced it as a growth driver.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highChina property sector
So what: Look to the financial and fiscal system, not property alone, for the root cause.
“Construction has fallen almost 80%. Sales have fallen about 60.” ▶ 9:13
The post-2018 property rebound was financed by developers borrowing from households through pre-construction sales, so when defaults came they landed on home buyers; Wright likens it to a bank run that happened at developers instead of banks.
causal🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highEvergrandehome buyers
So what: Household confidence loss, not bank funding, transmitted the property shock.
Wright argues China did have its cathartic financial crisis moment, the Evergrande protests of 13 September 2021, and that the last five years of impaired policy tools, persistent deflation and migrating credit risk are the normal aftermath of a financial crisis.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highEvergrandeChina
So what: Stop waiting for a Lehman moment; the slow-decay regime is the crisis outcome.
Credit risk has migrated from the periphery to the core of China's economy in sequence: peer-to-peer lending in 2018, small banks 2019, trust companies 2020, property developers 2021, mortgages 2022, local government financing vehicles 2023.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumlocal government financing vehiclestrust companies
So what: Each patch moved the risk closer to the sovereign balance sheet.
China's leaders prefer decay over crisis because the path to crisis is reform, not an external shock; the government is assumed to backstop everything, so the real risk is from restructuring.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highXi Jinping
So what: Expect policy to forestall adjustment, producing prolonged low growth rather than a discrete crash.
“The path to crisis is reform.” ▶ 0:35
Fiscal constraint and policy space 6
China is already running a fiscal deficit of about 9.5% of GDP (roughly $2 trillion last year); Wright says pushing it to 15-16% would not generate much more activity without fixing the underlying system, and the binding constraint is fiscal, not monetary.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highChina fiscal deficit
So what: Stimulus headlines have diminishing returns; fiscal space is exhausted in practice.
Unlike Japan in the mid-1990s, China still has monetary room, with the constraints being the exchange rate and financial stability rather than a zero bound, but Japan remains the closest analogy for permanently suppressed growth.
framework🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumJapanPBOC
So what: Rate cuts are possible but trade off against the currency and bank margins.
Wright says China needs an extra 4-5 percentage points of GDP in tax revenue because revenue depends on investment and trade; broadening the base (property tax, consumption or luxury taxes, fintech-era collection) conflicts with encouraging consumption and would slow local government investment.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highChina tax systemproperty tax
So what: A growth-model shift requires a tax reorganization that Beijing has avoided.
The effective tax rate of China's catering industry has collapsed over a decade because small service businesses collect on WeChat Pay and Alipay with no tax reporting obligation, per Wright's colleague Alan Fun.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumWeChat PayAlipay
So what: Fintech payments changed the tax base without the tax code following.
Equalizing rural and urban pensions for migrant workers would cost about 33,000 RMB per person for 250-300 million people, roughly 30% of GDP, but could be financed gradually with central bank purchases of about a trillion yuan of special bonds a year; Beijing is not doing it.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediummigrant workersspecial bonds
So what: The lowest-hanging reform is identified but unfunded, signalling fiscal reluctance.
Wright dismisses further IMF-style property cleanup as a distraction, since the pre-sale problem is largely solved by new controls, home ownership is high, interior cities are oversupplied and coastal markets are served by secondary sales, so bailouts have low marginal growth impact.
contrarian🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumIMFChina property sector
So what: Do not expect property repair to restart growth.
Exports, deflation and the global surplus 7
Wright argues the domestic demand slump and the rising external surplus are the same phenomenon: industrial capacity that once supplied Chinese construction now exports at cut prices, creating persistent deflation and a weaker real exchange rate, so the 'K-shaped economy' framing treats them wrongly as separate.
causal🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highChinareal exchange rate
So what: Export pressure on other countries is a symptom of domestic weakness, so it will persist.
As long as China's marginal demand is outside China and it cannot grow faster than its customers, it can only gain relative to the world by displacing others' export market share, a fight over a shrinking pie that falls further if its export prices keep driving disinvestment abroad.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highChina exports
So what: Trade pushback is structurally likely to rise.
Brad Setser argues Beijing may not care about GDP: exports are growing about 10% year-over-year, at twice global trade, and supply-chain dominance (cutting critical inputs) lets China sustain export growth beyond what conventional theory allows; the US truce and Vietnam transshipment and Europe's reluctance support this.
contrarian🗣 Brad Setserconviction: mediumBrad SetserVietnamEurope
So what: Export-led growth may last longer than macro models imply because of retaliation capacity.
Wright accepts the supply-chain dominance threat is real but reframes it: the danger is China locking in export displacement and supply-chain control, not becoming the largest economy.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumChina
So what: Policy should address resilience of defense industrial bases, not GDP rank.
In minerals processing, Chinese dominance rests on cheap credit to state producers that tolerate unprofitable lines because of clustering near downstream electronics, plus cheap subsidized power; aluminum is described as congealed energy, which concentrates copper and aluminum smelting in China.
causal🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumaluminumcopperChina
So what: Non-market input costs, not just scale, make it hard for others to compete.
“aluminum is basically congealed energy” ▶ 23:36
Wright says the US has outgrown China over the last five years and that China needed to grow two to three percentage points faster than the US for decades to reach number one in nominal GDP; at 4% US growth versus China near half a percent, that only happens with a massive dollar depreciation.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highUnited StatesChina
So what: China overtaking the US economically is no longer a base case.
Setser notes the recent trade-delta and Chinese surplus growth is tied to the AI trade: dollar recycling is now done by commercial banks into overseas equities and bonds, which fund US data center buildouts, while Chinese hardware exports supply those buildouts.
causal🗣 Brad Setserconviction: mediumAI tradehyperscalerscommercial banks
So what: A disruption in these flows would hit both Chinese export engines and US tech financing.
Jobs, demographics and the strategic-industry bet 7
China graduates 12-13 million college students a year out of a new labor force cohort of 17-20 million, and the economy has not adjusted to provide jobs for them while birth rates keep falling.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highChina youth unemployment
So what: Employment, not GDP, is the likely domestic pressure point.
Wright and colleagues estimate China's strategic industries, including AI, data centers, hyperscaler buildout and advanced robotics, total about 6.3% of GDP in 2025, so they are too small and too capital-intensive to offset a stalled domestic economy.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highAI data centersadvanced robotics
So what: The tech champions cannot carry aggregate growth or jobs.
“something like 6.3% of GDP in 2025” ▶ 33:21
Chinese frontier model companies collectively generate about 11 billion in revenue against hyperscaler capex of about 135 billion this year and roughly 160 billion next year, and financing depends on equity valuations; government guidance funds only cover chip purchases, so China is compute and finance constrained.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumhyperscalersDeepSeekMoonshotByteDance
So what: China's AI buildout lacks self-sustaining business models and relies on industrial policy funding.
ByteDance had the largest marginal AI expansion this year and is reportedly funding it through an offshore loan rather than the domestic system.
data_point🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: lowByteDance
So what: Domestic financing channels are not funding the AI leader.
Wright argues the Chinese AI push is probably not for profit, jobs or AGI but for embedding Chinese tech stacks in consumer electronics for geopolitical leverage and supply-chain dominance.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumChinaAI
So what: Treat Chinese AI as strategic leverage, not a commercial growth engine.
Wright expects AI and robotics to raise marginal labor productivity while overall labor force participation collapses, which is a likely path rather than a source of new jobs.
prediction🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumAIrobotics
So what: Automation worsens China's employment problem.
Chinese youth unemployment data was discontinued in 2023 after passing 20%.
data_point🗣 Jordan Schneiderconviction: mediumyouth unemployment
So what: Opacity of labor data raises the odds of misjudging social stress.
Politics: Xi, centralization and reform odds 8
Between 2012 and 2016 China's financial system transformed from stable deposits and state-owned lending to marginal loans to unregulated entities funded by wealth management products and volatile capital flows; since then Xi has recentralized, treating finance like a utility under party commissions.
framework🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumXi Jinpingwealth management products
So what: Financial governance moved from market-opening to party control.
The 2018 super-regulator under Liu He was reversed within five years, and anti-corruption investigations, salary crackdowns and oversight mean financial technocrats avoid initiative, so the equilibrium is 'lend to state-owned enterprises and go home'.
causal🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumLiu Hefinancial technocrats
So what: Weak credit demand reflects bureaucratic risk aversion, not just borrower weakness.
Wright describes an inverse relationship between centralization of power and the credibility of countercyclical policy, because Xi has created new financial risks through sectoral policy actions.
causal🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumXi Jinping
So what: More centralization has not meant more effective stimulus.
Wright says no obstacle prevents Xi from announcing a reform pivot (taxing the wealthy, breaking up state-owned enterprises, dismantling local financing platforms, funding migrant pensions), but he is unlikely to since he does not believe the model is failing and strategic competition makes admitting problems costly.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumXi Jinping
So what: Reform likelihood depends on lowering strategic competition and external pressure.
Wright expects that a Xi succession or delegitimization in 2027 would make a policy break less likely, because a successor would continue the model absent clear external pushback; the longer Xi stays, the higher the chance of a later break.
prediction🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: lowXi Jinping2027
So what: Don't bet on leadership change alone to bring reform.
Wright draws on Yakov Feygin's work to say Brezhnev chose decay and a technological fix (cybernetics) over reform, and cites Kevin Rudd as saying China similarly sees AI as a way to avoid reliance on markets for allocation.
framework🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumBrezhnevKevin RuddSoviet Union
So what: The Soviet analogy implies a long grinding decline rather than abrupt reform.
Jordan Schneider pushes back that internal pressure, especially unemployment, may force change sooner than external signals; Wright replies that pressure points are real but the zero-COVID exit shows change only came once Xi changed his mind.
contrarian🗣 Jordan Schneiderconviction: lowzero-COVIDXi Jinping
So what: Internal pushback may not translate into structural reform without Xi's consent.
Party journal Qiushi in mid-August argued that citizens' experience lags stated growth because they have not yet adopted new technologies like AI, which Wright reads as unreassuring evidence that the system does not grasp the problem.
anecdote🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumQiushiAI
So what: Official diagnosis blames adoption, not structure.
Policy implications for the West 5
Wright concludes there is no longer a systemic economic rivalry with China, so military and industrial-competitiveness threats are deterable rather than inexorable, and the real worry is China's concentration of manufacturing exceeding resilience red lines for defense industrial bases.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: highChinaG7
So what: Shift Western policy from fearing Chinese GDP dominance to managing supply-chain concentration.
Wright argues the rest of the world has more leverage over China than it uses, best applied collectively, because China needs access to G7 demand and its supply-restriction tools will become less salient over time.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumG7China
So what: Coordinated demand-side restrictions on the fastest-growing Chinese exports are the lever.
“The rest of the world has a lot more leverage over China than they are currently using.” ▶ 0:04
Setser disagrees that the rivalry has ended, saying China's supply chain controls have so far been more potent than US demand-side restrictions, and rivalry persists until that perception changes.
contrarian🗣 Brad Setserconviction: mediumBrad SetserUnited States
So what: Supply-side weaponization currently gives China an edge in leverage.
Wright says escalating pressure on China's fastest-growing export categories is possible without much raising Western consumer prices, but the harder question is which industries to support and reinvest in, which is easier to answer in Germany than in the United States.
thesis🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: lowGermanyUnited States
So what: Industrial policy design, not tariffs alone, determines outcomes.
Wright argues global imbalances have historically ended abruptly, and China's domestic foundations for the current model are unsustainable, so it will end one way or the other.
prediction🗣 Logan Wrightconviction: mediumglobal imbalances
So what: Position for abrupt rather than smooth unwinding of surpluses.

The Great Simplification · Nate Hagens 1 episode(s) · 38 insights

The Coolest Year for the Rest of Our Lives? with Zeke Hausfather | TGS 23692 min38 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Climate scientist Zeke Hausfather forecasts that a record super El Niño will push 2027 to about 1.8C above pre-industrial, and explains why aerosol cuts, scenario revisions and sensitivity drive the 3C-by-2100 baseline. He also covers AI agent energy use and carbon removal work at Frontier and Stripe.
El Niño and 2027 record heat · 8Climate sensitivity, scenarios and 2100 warming · 7Energy transition: what the data show · 6Aerosols and the acceleration of warming · 4Methane, oceans and carbon sinks · 4AI energy use and AI as an information tool · 5Policy, data infrastructure and carbon removal · 4
El Niño and 2027 record heat 8
Zeke Hausfather expects 2026 to be the warmest or second-warmest year on record by only a small margin, but 2027 to be much warmer because global temperature lags El Niño peaks by about three to five months.
prediction🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highEl Niño20262027
So what: Record-heat headlines, and the attention and damages that follow, are concentrated in 2027 rather than this year.
Hausfather's latest estimate is that 2027 will be about 1.8C above pre-industrial, versus a previous record of about 1.5C.
prediction🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: high2027pre-industrialEl Niño
So what: A 0.3C jump over the prior record would be an outsized step that could shift policy and market attention to climate.
≈ “my latest estimate is that 2027 will be about 1.8 degrees above pre-industrial levels” paraphrase
Past strong El Niños show the pattern: 1997 was near-record and 1998 beat it by a huge margin, and likewise 2015 then 2016 and 2023 then 2024, so the year after the peak sets the record.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: high199820162024El Niño
So what: The analog set supports forecasting the record year as the one following the El Niño peak.
Strong El Niños add about 0.2C to annual temperatures the year after they peak, perhaps 0.3C this time, while human emissions add about 0.2C every eight years, so humans add a permanent super El Niño worth of heat every eight years.
framework🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highEl Niñofossil fuels
So what: A strong El Niño is a preview of the average climate roughly a decade out.
≈ “we're essentially adding a permanent super El Niño worth of heat to the climate system every eight years” paraphrase
The current El Niño is above 3C anomaly in the Niño 3.4 region in mid-September, more than a degree above any previous event at this point, and is expected to peak near 4C versus the prior record of about 2.75C (1877-78 and 2015-16).
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highNiño 3.4El Niño1877-1878
So what: The event is outside the historical record, so analog-based forecasts carry added uncertainty.
The 1877-1878 El Niño was associated with droughts and famines that killed an estimated 50 million people, and the current event hits regions like Southern Africa, India, Indonesia, northern Brazil and Australia with drier conditions.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: medium1877-1878Southern AfricaIndiaIndonesiaBrazilAustralia
So what: Agricultural output risk in these regions is elevated into 2027.
The record El Niño coincides with fertilizer shortages, Strait of Hormuz disruption and cuts to global aid funding (USAID, some European donors), which compounds food-security risk, especially in Africa.
causal🗣 Nate Hagens and Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumUSAIDStrait of HormuzWMOAfrica
So what: Food price and famine risk stack across several shocks; Nate Hagens cited chat-group forecasts of caloric shortages in 2028.
The WMO has run the largest mobilization in its history for this El Niño, and countries such as Kenya (livestock vaccines) and southern African states (food stocks, drought-tolerant seeds) are preparing.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumWMOKenya
So what: Preparation lowers but does not remove the humanitarian and agricultural impact.
Climate sensitivity, scenarios and 2100 warming 7
Hausfather says future warming comes down to three uncertainties: what humans do (about half the uncertainty), how the biosphere and carbon cycle respond, and climate sensitivity to feedbacks from water vapor, clouds and albedo.
framework🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highclimate sensitivitycarbon cycle
So what: Narrowing any one factor does not narrow the overall range; scenario choice matters as much as physics.
With emissions flat at today's roughly 40 Gt CO2 a year (about 60 Gt CO2-equivalent), Hausfather expects about 3C by 2100 with a range of roughly 2.2C to 4C, and a policy-rollback world would reach about 3.5C (2.5 to 4.5C).
prediction🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumCO22100climate policy
So what: The central case is far above Paris targets, and tail risk of 4C argues for planning beyond the median.
≈ “In that world, we end up at about three degrees, uh, by 2100” paraphrase
In a high-emissions scenario Hausfather sees a non-trivial, roughly one-in-four chance of about 5C warming by 2150.
prediction🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: low2150
So what: Tail outcomes extend well past 2100 since warming continues unless emissions reach zero.
The last IPCC report put equilibrium climate sensitivity at 2C to 5C per CO2 doubling (90 percent range), and high-sensitivity models also need high aerosol cooling, because otherwise they would already show about 2C of warming.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highIPCCequilibrium climate sensitivityaerosols
So what: Aerosol and sensitivity uncertainties are correlated, so a high-sensitivity world means more warming as aerosols are cleaned up.
If emissions fall to zero, falling atmospheric CO2 and continued ocean warming roughly cancel, so warming stops but the planet does not cool back down without net CO2 removal or climate engineering.
causal🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highnet zerooceancarbon dioxide
So what: There is little warming in the pipeline, but also no natural reversal, which is the structural case for carbon removal.
The new CMIP7 emission scenarios are named high, medium, low and very low, and the high scenario now reflects an SSP3-like world where emissions are only about 50 percent above today by 2100, rather than the earlier RCP8.5 tripling of emissions with five times more coal.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highCMIP7RCP8.5SSP3IPCC
So what: Clean energy economics have removed the extreme coal-heavy tail from mainstream scenarios.
Hausfather argues that even in a poor, conflict-ridden world solar is cheap enough that coal use falls by 2100, meaning cheap clean energy is the main reason high-end emission scenarios have been marked down.
thesis🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumsolarcoal
So what: Energy-cost curves, not climate policy, are driving the downgrade of worst-case emission paths.
Energy transition: what the data show 6
Hausfather says global coal use has plateaued since about 2015, gas has grown sharply, oil has flattened as EVs spread, and fossil use is growing under 1 percent a year versus over 3 percent a few decades ago.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumcoalnatural gasoilEVs
So what: Fossil demand growth is slowing but has not peaked in absolute terms.
About 30 percent of cars sold globally this year are electric versus about 5 percent in the US, and the Middle East situation is raising oil prices and accelerating the shift to alternatives.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumEVsUnited Statesoil pricesMiddle East
So what: The US is a laggard in EV adoption while oil demand is being eroded elsewhere.
Wind and solar are set to generate more electricity than natural gas globally this year for the first time.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumwindsolarnatural gas
So what: A milestone showing renewables overtaking a major fossil source in the power mix.
Nate Hagens counters that from 2015 to 2025 solar grew about 4,400 TWh while coal, gas and oil grew about 14,000 TWh, so cheaper solar has added to total energy rather than displacing fossil fuels.
contrarian🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highsolarcoalnatural gasoil
So what: The additive-versus-substitution view challenges the idea that falling renewable costs alone reduce emissions.
Hausfather says China is about to have a third year of flat-to-falling CO2 emissions, the US and Europe are down about 20 percent and the UK about 40 percent, and the UK now burns no coal.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumChinaUnited StatesEuropeUnited Kingdom
So what: Peak-emissions evidence is emerging in the largest economies, though global emissions have not yet declined.
Hausfather claims that about $2.3 trillion globally goes to reducing emissions versus well under 1 percent on carbon removal, and Hagens notes this spending has not yet reduced absolute global emissions, which Hausfather concedes.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumcarbon removal$2.3 trillion
So what: Capital allocation to carbon removal is tiny relative to abatement spending.
Aerosols and the acceleration of warming 4
Hausfather attributes essentially all of the recent acceleration in warming, from roughly 0.2C per decade to closer to 0.3C per decade, to falling aerosol emissions on top of continued greenhouse gas emissions.
causal🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumaerosolssulfur dioxide
So what: Cleaner air is unmasking warming, which is a one-time boost that ends once sulfur reaches zero.
≈ “pretty much all of the acceleration we've seen in the rate of warming in recent years” paraphrase
Aerosols are estimated to have cooled the planet by between 0.2C and 1.2C versus pre-industrial, a range so wide that at 1.2C the world would be about 2.5C warmer without them.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highaerosols
So what: Aerosol cooling is a major unquantified term in the climate budget.
Global sulfur dioxide emissions are down 40 percent from the 1980 peak, China cut its emissions 75 percent since 2008, and the 2020 low-sulfur shipping fuel rule cut shipping sulfur by about 80 percent, which matters because ships emit in clean marine air where the cloud effect is larger.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highChinalow-sulfur bunker fuelsulfur dioxide
So what: Policy-driven clean-air gains had measurable climate side-effects.
Satellite measurements of Earth's energy imbalance from CERES show it rising, possibly from aerosol cuts but possibly from low-cloud feedbacks; if it is feedbacks, warming keeps accelerating rather than ending when sulfur hits zero.
causal🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumCERESEarth energy imbalancelow clouds
So what: Whether the energy imbalance reflects aerosols or cloud feedback is the key open question distinguishing benign from worrying outcomes.
Methane, oceans and carbon sinks 4
Methane accounts for about 0.4C of historical warming but, unlike CO2, breaks down in roughly a decade, so cutting methane emissions could actually cool the planet while cutting CO2 only slows warming.
framework🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highmethaneCO2
So what: Methane is the fast lever for near-term cooling.
Recent growth in atmospheric methane appears driven mainly by wetlands, possibly a temperature-mediated feedback in tropical wetlands, with permafrost methane still minor globally.
causal🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediummethanewetlandspermafrost
So what: A natural feedback source of methane would be outside direct policy control.
The ocean has absorbed about 90 percent of the heat trapped by greenhouse gases, with the surface up about 1C, the world 1.4C, land about 2C, and the deep ocean only 0.1-0.2C, which buffers but delays the full effect.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highoceanland
So what: The ocean buffer delays impacts but also means no cooling is available if CO2 falls.
Land-use emissions, mostly deforestation, are about 10 percent of global emissions, around four gigatons of carbon, and stopping warming requires eliminating deforestation as well as cutting fossil fuels.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumdeforestationAmazon
So what: Forest loss is a separate lever alongside fossil fuel reduction.
AI energy use and AI as an information tool 5
In 2025 Google reported about 0.24 Wh per typical Gemini prompt and OpenAI about 0.34 Wh per ChatGPT query, but those figures are for non-reasoning chat, whereas reasoning models use about five to ten times more energy.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highGoogleGeminiChatGPTChatGPT
So what: Headline per-prompt energy figures understate energy use of how AI is actually used today.
Hausfather found a typical Claude Code session used about 0.2 to 1.2 kWh and an average day of agent use 1.2 to 6 kWh, roughly two fridges, based on four months of his own usage data.
data_point🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highClaude CodeAI agents
So what: Agentic AI use consumes orders of magnitude more electricity per task than single prompts, which matters for data-center demand.
≈ “a typical, uh, session of using cloud code, you know, was somewhere between point two and one point two kilowatt hours” paraphrase
Hausfather applies Jevons paradox to compute: as AI gets cheaper and more embedded and autonomous, total energy use will rise, as LED lighting cut costs 10x but lumen use rose about 3x.
thesis🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumJevons paradoxAILED lighting
So what: Efficiency gains in AI are unlikely to cap aggregate electricity demand.
Hausfather says machine learning has struggled with physics-based climate simulation, though AI weather models are strong, so AI will mostly help climate modelling through faster code, downscaling and cloud parameterization rather than replace models.
thesis🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumAI weather modelsclimate models
So what: AI is a tool for climate modeling, not a shortcut around physics-based projections.
Hausfather is cautiously optimistic that LLMs act as consensus machines that give similar climate answers regardless of political affiliation, because it is hard to train a model that denies climate but answers physics correctly.
thesis🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: lowGrokAILLMs
So what: AI might recenter expertise in a fragmented information environment.
Policy, data infrastructure and carbon removal 4
US cuts to NOAA data products, climate.gov and the 40-year sea ice satellite record and the withdrawal from the IPCC are being offset by Copernicus rebuilding data aggregation, but gaps in unreplaced satellites can never be filled retroactively.
causal🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumNOAACopernicusIPCCsatellites
So what: Observational gaps are the irreversible risk, whereas analysis functions can migrate to other institutions.
Hausfather views climate attention as thermostatic and a political luxury good, so extreme events from El Niño and accelerating warming will bring more attention back to climate after a dip since 2022.
thesis🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumclimate politicsEl Niño
So what: Expect political and capital focus on climate to rise with 2027 extremes.
Hausfather's favored policy lever is putting a price on carbon to internalize social costs, and he sees carbon removal as about 10 percent of the solution, with 90 percent from cutting emissions.
thesis🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: highcarbon pricecarbon removalFrontierStripe
So what: Pricing carbon is the standard economist answer, but the political path is described as unlikely by both speakers.
Frontier and Stripe are using this decade to test carbon removal approaches including enhanced rock weathering on croplands (Brazil, India, US, Africa), river liming (Carbon Run), ocean alkalinity enhancement, biomass-based capture and direct air capture, to have scalable options for the 2040s and 2050s.
thesis🗣 Zeke Hausfatherconviction: mediumFrontierStripeenhanced rock weatheringCarbon Rundirect air capture
So what: Carbon removal is at an R&D stage with agriculture-linked methods showing early promise.

Hasan Minhaj 1 episode(s) · 35 insights

Are Prediction Markets Ruining Everything? with Chris Beam67 min35 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Bloomberg's Chris Beam explains to Hasan Minhaj how Kalshi and Polymarket grew to about $24B monthly volume while facing insider trading, manipulation, fake marketing and a gambling-versus-trading regulatory fight. Beam sees crackdown and Supreme Court preemption as the two key outcomes.
Scale and user economics · 5Fake marketing and Polymarket growth tactics · 3Insider trading, manipulation and market integrity · 9Regulation, CFTC and the gambling versus trading fight · 8Accuracy versus polls and the wisdom of crowds · 3Business model and Trump-era volatility · 4Behavioral and social effects · 3
Scale and user economics 5
Combined monthly trading volume on Kalshi and Polymarket rose from under $5 billion to about $24 billion in roughly seven months over the past year.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: highKalshiPolymarket
So what: Hypergrowth in volume means fee revenue and regulatory scrutiny are both scaling fast.
A Wall Street Journal analysis of 1.6 million Polymarket accounts found losers vastly outnumber winners, with about one-tenth of one percent of accounts taking the majority of winnings.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: highPolymarketWall Street Journal
So what: Profits are extremely concentrated; retail flow is the exit liquidity for a tiny sharp cohort.
As prediction markets expand into more areas, sharps, hedge funds and Wall Street market makers running algorithms are entering, making it harder for average users to turn a profit.
causal🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumhedge fundsmarket makersPolymarketKalshi
So what: Professionalization of liquidity provision squeezes retail edge and may shift value to market makers.
Roughly a quarter of Gen Z investors treat sports betting as part of their legitimate financial plan, and over half of them put money intended for investing into sports betting.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: mediumGen Z
So what: Retail capital is being diverted from equities and savings toward event contracts, a potential flow headwind for brokers.
“more than a quarter of Gen Z investors treat sports betting as part of their legitimate financial plans” ▶ 3:53
Prediction markets appeal to the financial nihilism of 20-somethings who believe everything is rigged, and are marketed via TikTok influencers as a money hack.
thesis🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: mediumTikTokGen Z
So what: Demand is driven by perceived blocked mobility, which supports durability of the user base.
Fake marketing and Polymarket growth tactics 3
A Wall Street Journal investigation found many influencer videos showing big Polymarket payoffs were fake, including one claiming a $100,000 win on a McDonald's mention bet, using a spoofed URL with a lowercase L swapped for an I.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: highPolymarketWall Street Journal
So what: Deceptive growth marketing is a regulatory and reputational tail risk for the platforms.
Influencers who worked with Polymarket said the company gave them a password to access the website so they could create real-looking trades; the fake-win campaign drew over 140 million views.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: mediumPolymarket
So what: If substantiated, this invites FTC or state consumer-protection action against customer acquisition.
Polymarket staged a one-day free grocery store in New York City as a brand-building move.
anecdote🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: mediumPolymarketNew York City
So what: Platforms are investing in reputational positioning alongside lobbying, signaling a political defense strategy.
Insider trading, manipulation and market integrity 9
A White House teleprompter operator allegedly made over $100,000 betting on presidential speeches, and a special forces soldier was charged over more than $400,000 won betting on whether Maduro would be removed from office.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: highWhite HouseMaduroPolymarket
So what: Government-action markets have concrete insider cases, strengthening the legislative push to ban them.
Researchers identified nine connected Polymarket accounts that collectively made $2.4 million betting almost exclusively on US military operations with a 98% win rate, which luck cannot explain.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: highPolymarketUS military
So what: Event-driven information leakage from national-security contexts is the strongest case for restricting such markets.
“We spotted nine Poly Market accounts, all connected, who made collectively $2.4 million” ▶ 8:22
Senator Chris Murphy proposed legislation to ban betting on government action or on markets where one person controls the outcome.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: mediumChris Murphy
So what: Legislative risk is concentrated on government-action contracts rather than the whole category.
The prosecuted case of a Google employee who made about $1.2 million betting on the most-searched Google query of the year was charged as wire fraud for stealing company information, not as insider trading, because prediction markets are not securities and securities law is unclear to apply.
data_point🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: highGooglewire fraudsecurities law
So what: Enforcement relies on fraud and misappropriation theories, leaving a legal gray zone for tippee chains like cousins and dancers.
Insider exposure scales with market granularity: markets on album drops, Super Bowl song lists or product launch dates have many people with advance knowledge, so platforms cannot realistically police them all.
causal🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumKalshiBad BunnyGoogle Gemini
So what: Narrow markets structurally favor informed insiders, which undermines the fairness pitch and liquidity of retail participants.
Kalshi positions itself as the cooperative adult in the room, asking users their job at registration and claiming staff and AI monitor for insider connections, but it is an open question how many people it can track across thousands of markets.
thesis🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumKalshi
So what: Compliance capacity is a scaling constraint and a differentiator versus offshore Polymarket.
Thinly traded markets can be manipulated: a few hundred to a few thousand dollars can move a down-ballot candidate's odds, and media coverage of the spike can become a self-fulfilling prophecy.
causal🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: highPolymarketKalshi
So what: Price signals from low-liquidity markets are unreliable and should not be treated as information.
“you could drive a candidate's odds up or down with a few thousand or possibly even of just a few hundred bucks” ▶ 24:01
A temperature spike at Paris Charles de Gaulle airport paid $21,000 on a longshot Polymarket weather bet, prompting a police probe into thermometer tampering; Beam describes a person holding a hair dryer to a temperature node to push the reading past the threshold.
anecdote🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumPolymarketCharles de Gaulle airport
So what: Contracts settled on a single physical sensor are cheap to manipulate, so settlement-source design matters.
The 65-minute White House press conference market, where the press secretary ended the briefing seconds before the cutoff, was small but created suspicion of manipulation, illustrating how prediction markets erode trust in observed events.
anecdote🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumCaroline LeavittWhite House
So what: Even tiny markets can seed conspiracy narratives about real events.
Regulation, CFTC and the gambling versus trading fight 8
Kalshi and Polymarket argue they trade event-contract derivatives under the CFTC rather than offering gambling, because state sports-book regulation would impose taxes (New York 51%), licensing fees, and problem-gambling obligations in every state.
thesis🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: highCFTCKalshiPolymarketDraftKingsNew York
So what: The federal-preemption framing is the core economic moat, and its legal outcome determines the sports-book margin structure.
Kalshi's US trading volume is overwhelmingly sports, and splitting liquidity into 50 state-by-state pools adhering to state laws would make the markets less efficient, so state regulation would shrink the business materially.
causal🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: highKalshi
So what: Losing federal preemption hits both volume and market quality, a binary regulatory risk.
Prediction markets are overseen by the CFTC, created in the 1970s to regulate food prices, and its current chairman is described as skeptical about whether the agency is the more powerful one compared with the SEC.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: lowCFTCSEC
So what: The CFTC has limited resources relative to the scale of retail event trading.
The Trump administration is suing states that try to regulate prediction markets, and Donald Trump Jr. is an investor in Polymarket and adviser to both Polymarket and Kalshi.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: highDonald Trump Jr.PolymarketKalshiTrump administration
So what: Political connectivity currently protects the sector federally but creates a reversal risk under a new administration.
Kalshi's 2023 application for political markets was rejected by the CFTC under Biden in 2024 citing manipulation, insider trading and damage to democratic trust, then overruled by a court in October 2024 before the election.
data_point🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: highKalshiCFTCBiden administration
So what: Legal, not just political, momentum helped; the sector's regulatory permission was already moving before Trump.
Beam says a dozen-plus state cases may reach the Supreme Court, which could rule that prediction markets fall under the CFTC rather than states, entrenching them given the CFTC's friendly stance.
prediction🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumSupreme CourtCFTCKalshi
So what: A Supreme Court preemption win would be a major bullish catalyst for Kalshi and a bearish one for state-regulated sportsbooks.
Beam thinks a backlash and crackdown is a strong possibility, especially under a Democratic administration, and calls a 'cigarettes moment' entirely possible, noting a Pew survey showing more Americans view sports betting as net bad for society.
prediction🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumPewRobin Hanson
So what: Public opinion on sports betting is a leading indicator of political risk for the category.
“I think it's entirely possible and some of the biggest prediction market boosters out there agree.” ▶ 1:02:44
Polymarket's main platform is an international crypto venue not US-based, hosting the most controversial markets such as Maduro's capture and missile strikes, so regulators can do little about it, while Kalshi as a US-regulated company could be hit with many more rules.
thesis🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: highPolymarketKalshiMaduro
So what: A crackdown would likely advantage offshore crypto venues, and Americans can reach them by VPN.
Accuracy versus polls and the wisdom of crowds 3
Scholars find prediction markets generally perform well versus polls, but accuracy depends on market size: presidential markets are highly liquid and accurate, while congressional and mayoral markets are thin and easier to manipulate.
framework🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: highprediction marketspolls
So what: Liquidity is the right filter when using market odds as a signal.
Before the 2024 election, Polymarket and Kalshi had Trump favored hours before the networks called it, and criticism that crypto bros inflated his odds proved wrong because the markets turned out correct.
data_point🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumPolymarketKalshiDonald Trump
So what: Markets can front-run network calls, which is the strongest real-world accuracy evidence offered.
Prediction markets still miss: Francesca Hong was priced above 95% to win in Wisconsin and lost, and Angie Nixon in Florida had about 8% and won; Beam says markets inherit polling errors because polls are an input.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: mediumFrancesca HongAngie Nixon
So what: Markets are one indicator among many rather than an independent truth source.
Business model and Trump-era volatility 4
Uncertainty is the best possible product input for prediction markets, so Trump's unpredictability, such as conflicting same-day statements on an Iran peace deal, generates huge trading volume whose fees accrue to the platforms.
causal🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumTrumpIranKalshiPolymarket
So what: Platform revenue is levered to headline volatility, so a calmer news cycle would be a headwind.
“for them uncertainty is the best possible thing” ▶ 19:05
Kalshi's CEO's stated long-term goal is to financialize everything and create a tradable asset out of any difference in opinion, replacing debate with markets, accuracy and truth.
thesis🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: mediumKalshiTarek Mansour
So what: The strategic roadmap is expansion into ever more event categories, which raises the regulatory surface area.
Beam believes the platform leaders sincerely hold a libertarian view that price is truth because markets aggregate dispersed information, and says the idea is not a grift, though most volume is sports and trivial events.
thesis🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumRobin HansonEliezer YudkowskyManifest
So what: Intellectual legitimacy exists for corporate forecasting and inflation use cases, but current volume doesn't depend on it.
Even Polymarket's CEO told Beam he does not care for frivolous markets, while Kalshi executives say it is not up to them to decide what people want to bet on.
anecdote🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: lowPolymarketKalshiShane Copeland
So what: Neither platform is likely to self-restrict the long tail of low-quality markets.
Behavioral and social effects 3
Prediction markets shift attention to narrow slices, like parlays on hits versus walks, and extend a purely transactional view to politics and culture, which Beam says changes how people engage with events.
causal🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumprediction markets
So what: Engagement quality declines, which feeds public backlash and possible regulatory framing around addiction.
Beam says a 23-year-old streamer who turned to following politics solely because bets give him a motive to learn is an example of how the markets orient users to constantly look for edge rather than learn for its own sake.
anecdote🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: lowAustin Minton
So what: Some civic engagement upside is real but financially motivated.
Beam is pessimistic about stopping use because the crypto-based platforms are beyond reach and Americans can use a VPN; mandated anti-addiction funding has mixed results in state gambling.
prediction🗣 Chris Beam (Bloomberg)conviction: mediumVPNPolymarket
So what: Restrictions will have leakage, so enforcement may only displace volume offshore.

80,000 Hours Podcast 1 episode(s) · 32 insights

AI Overlords vs Power-hungry Humans: Which Should Scare You More?89 min32 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
A 80,000 Hours debate between Katja Grace and Tom Davidson on whether misaligned AI takeover or AI-enabled human power grabs is the bigger risk. They disagree on likelihood and severity, but converge on pausing, transparency, opposing a single AI project and negotiating with China rather than racing.
Misalignment vs human power grab likelihood · 11Warning shots and societal response · 5Outcome severity and value lock-in · 6Policy: pausing, centralised projects, mitigations · 7US-China competition · 3
Misalignment vs human power grab likelihood 11
Katja Grace argues AI takeover is more likely than a human power grab because any more-capable misaligned agent with goals will eventually seek power, and humans face an extra hurdle of becoming the single principal that a whole fleet of aligned AIs obeys.
thesis🗣 Katja Graceconviction: highKatja GraceAI takeover
So what: Her baseline puts most extreme-outcome probability mass on misalignment rather than misuse.
≈ “Everything that has goals in some sense would like power, or just would like power to achieve their goals.” paraphrase
Katja Grace says the Hugging Face incident raised her estimate that misaligned AIs will act in accordance with one another, making AI coordination to seize power more plausible than coordination among human CEOs.
causal🗣 Katja Graceconviction: mediumKatja GraceHugging Face
So what: Observed AI collusion is treated as a live update, not a hypothetical.
Tom Davidson says the Hugging Face incident surprised him and moved him toward thinking misaligned-AI takeover risk is notably bigger overall, even though he still wants extreme human power concentration to get more attention.
thesis🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonHugging FaceForethought
So what: A leading power-concentration researcher is updating toward misalignment risk on new evidence.
≈ “I have updated based on the Hugging Face stuff. I was really surprised by that.” paraphrase
Tom Davidson argues human takeover is comparably likely because powerful people are selected for willingness to seek power and believe they will do better in control, while the incentive to pause is not shared by the powerful actors who keep pushing ahead.
thesis🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonAI companiesUS government
So what: Selection effects among CEOs and political leaders make power-seeking the default expectation.
Tom Davidson says that if superintelligence is misaligned it is game over for humans and human power grabs are moot, while the human-power-grab case mostly arises in worlds where intent alignment is solved.
framework🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: highTom Davidsonintent alignment
So what: Alignment success and power-grab risk are two branches of a single tree, not independent dice rolls.
≈ “If superintelligence is misaligned: game over.” paraphrase
Katja Grace and Tom Davidson broadly agree that intent alignment (AI does what its user wants) is the case where human power grabs occur, while value alignment is where good outcomes occur; Tom adds the alignment target is itself shaped by power-seekers, so it is not an independent roll.
framework🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumKatja GraceTom Davidsonintent alignmentvalue alignment
So what: Which alignment target gets chosen is itself a power-concentration question.
Tom Davidson argues that if AI timelines are short, AI takeover risk rises more than human power-concentration risk, because there is less time to solve misalignment, though both rise.
causal🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonAI timelines
So what: Shorter timelines shift the risk mix toward misalignment.
Katja Grace argues the opposite on timelines: fast scenarios favor human power grabs because there is no time to notice and close the gaps through which a human could seize control.
contrarian🗣 Katja Graceconviction: lowKatja GraceAI timelines
So what: Even the sign of the timeline effect on the risk mix is disputed among experts.
Tom Davidson says that even slow AI scenarios raise background human power-concentration risk through larger economic inequality, eroded democracy, and some people having long access to much stronger AI assistants as human labour loses value.
causal🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom Davidsoneconomic inequalitydemocracy
So what: Labour displacement is a political risk channel, not just an economic one.
≈ “all of those kind of background conditions are things that I expect to happen in a slower scenario, because of the way in which people’s labour stops being valuable.” paraphrase
Tom Davidson says existing defenses against power grabs were designed for a world where technology is non-autonomous tools and big projects still need many human hands, so there are scary gaps against AI-enabled coups by companies or governments.
causal🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: highTom DavidsonAI-enabled coups
So what: The historical check of needing many cooperating humans erodes once AI can replace them.
Tom Davidson describes two shots on goal for human power grabs: first the leading AI company using superintelligence to seize power, then the US executive branch gaining control of those AIs for national-security use and combining that with its formal authorities.
framework🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonUS executive branchAI companies
So what: Power-concentration risk is layered, with the state as a backstop route even if companies refrain.
Warning shots and societal response 5
Tom Davidson argues current AIs are myopic and do not collude across organisations and generations, so misaligned behavior shows up as blatant warning shots like the Hugging Face incident, whereas power-seeking humans reveal themselves strategically only when it gets them meaningful power.
thesis🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonHugging Face
So what: AI misbehavior may be easier to rally against than human power-seeking.
≈ “from the perspective of an AI collective across all time and space that just really wants to seize power, the Hugging Face incident was a complete disaster.” paraphrase
Katja Grace says warning shots are less clear-cut than people expect because reactions depend on prior beliefs and cultural amplification, and any incident has many causes so society may regulate only one of them; she notes the public already readily suspects figures like Sam Altman of seeking power while viewing AI takeover as sci-fi.
contrarian🗣 Katja Graceconviction: mediumKatja GraceSam Altman
So what: The warning-shot bar is higher for AI takeover than for human power-seeking.
Tom Davidson observes that Congressional hearings have discussed AI takeover openly without being laughed out of the room, but nothing similar has happened for AI CEO or executive-branch takeover, which is politically harder to discuss.
data_point🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonCongress
So what: Policy attention is lopsided toward misalignment relative to concentration risk.
Tom Davidson estimates there are 20 to 30 times fewer people thinking about extreme power concentration than about alignment among those thinking about superintelligence, a ratio that has only recently started to shift.
data_point🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom Davidsonalignmentpower concentration
So what: The neglectedness gap is the core of the case for marginal effort on power concentration.
≈ “it’s like 20 or 30 times fewer I think than thinking about alignment” paraphrase
Tom Davidson says if the world fails to respond to compelling misalignment evidence such as the Hugging Face incident, he will become more worried about misalignment because the warning-shot safety net would be absent.
prediction🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: exploratoryTom DavidsonHugging Face
So what: The global response to current incidents is a key observable for updating risk estimates.
Outcome severity and value lock-in 6
Tom Davidson argues a lone human in control could produce worse outcomes than an AI takeover because reaching a great future is a narrow target and a sole ruler could make catastrophic mistakes such as ignoring the stars, adopting a person-affecting ethics, or tiling the universe with hedonium.
contrarian🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: lowTom Davidsonhedonium
So what: Concentration of control narrows value exploration, which he treats as a first-order loss.
Tom Davidson thinks neither AI nor humans would likely kill all humans on Earth, citing decision-theoretic arguments and Paul Christiano's point that delaying leaving Earth costs an AI almost nothing, but still judges AI takeover to carry the greater extinction chance and human takeover the greater chance of sadistic or vindictive suffering.
framework🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonPaul Christiano
So what: Extinction risk and suffering-risk point to different failure modes for AI versus human rulers.
Katja Grace argues AI takeover outcomes are likelier to be catastrophic because AI may be misaligned or aligned to something narrow like maximising users for one company, whereas a human with power who avoids foolishness would likely pursue goals recognisable as pretty good to other humans.
thesis🗣 Katja Graceconviction: mediumKatja Grace
So what: She treats human values as a floor that AI lacks.
Katja Grace doubts that today's ethical-seeming AI personas will remain in charge once more happens faster and AI modifies itself, so current trained behavior is weak reassurance about post-takeover conduct.
causal🗣 Katja Graceconviction: mediumKatja Grace
So what: Persona alignment may not survive recursive self-modification.
Tom Davidson argues that a reward-seeking AI that seizes a data centre may not invest in making itself unshuttable, whereas a human power-seeker plans over months or years and is strongly invested in not losing power, making human regimes harder to reverse.
causal🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: lowTom Davidson
So what: Time horizon of the power-seeker affects reversibility of takeover.
Katja Grace expects a fully AI regime to be more internally stable than a human one because it can track its parts and react coherently, while AI takeover has many scenarios (sudden grab, gradual transfer, resource-grabbing AIs) so there are many bullets to dodge.
thesis🗣 Katja Graceconviction: lowKatja Grace
So what: The breadth of AI-takeover pathways is part of why she rates it more probable.
≈ “there are heaps of bullets to dodge to avoid this happening.” paraphrase
Policy: pausing, centralised projects, mitigations 7
Katja Grace says the best mitigation across risks, including human power concentration, is not building AI much more powerful than us until alignment is solid, and says we are nowhere close to confident that AIs are aligned, so she favors stopping, pausing or slowing.
thesis🗣 Katja Graceconviction: highKatja GraceAI pause
So what: Her plan A is a pause and she preferably wants zero frontier projects.
“my preferred number of projects would be zero” ▶ 1:07:58
Tom Davidson says a pause could worsen government power concentration if, for example, a president can approve or block model training case by case; he prefers independent third-party auditors who cannot be fired by the president and whose approval is required for deployment.
framework🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonUS presidentthird-party auditors
So what: Pause design details determine whether regulation distributes or concentrates control over AI.
Tom Davidson recommends two classes of mitigation for human power grabs: model specs that make powerful AI refuse power-grab instructions and proactively flag concentration risks, and mutual transparency into how companies and governments use powerful AI, especially in national-security and military contexts.
framework🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: highTom Davidsonmodel spec
So what: Concrete levers are AI behavior specs and usage transparency.
Tom Davidson prefers two or three frontier AI projects over one, because a second project lets AIs check each other, reducing misalignment risk and secret loyalties, while beyond roughly ten projects racing and coordination get harder; he thinks a single US project would be hard to insulate from presidential control.
thesis🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom Davidsonsecret loyalties
So what: Small-number competition is his sweet spot for power distribution.
Katja Grace says one big AI project is probably worse than the status quo because it would hold large power and be staffed by people who want to build AI soon, and she considers the difficulty of coordinating multiple projects to slow down overblown.
thesis🗣 Katja Graceconviction: mediumKatja Grace
So what: Both debaters oppose a Manhattan-style monopoly project for different reasons.
Tom Davidson warns that giving government agencies broad query rights over AI company logs could help detect misalignment but also enable a surveillance state or partisan punishment of firms, so he would be more nervous than Katja about abusable government powers.
framework🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom Davidson
So what: Safety oversight and civil-liberties risk trade off at the margin.
Katja Grace suggests, following David Krueger, that removing much of the compute, rather than merely pausing, would make the world more stable since no group could quickly rebuild very advanced AI.
thesis🗣 Katja Graceconviction: lowKatja GraceDavid Kruegercompute
So what: Compute destruction is a more extreme alternative to a regulatory pause.
US-China competition 3
Tom Davidson says China achieving a decisive AI advantage would be near worst-case for power concentration, but he is sceptical of the racing argument, because racing to beat China risks extreme power concentration in a few US hands, and China is currently not that close to the frontier.
thesis🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonChinaUnited States
So what: The China argument can cut either way and is routinely invoked by CEOs and officials who want to go fast.
≈ “the risk that China wins is just being used over and over by all of the company CEOs” paraphrase
Tom Davidson wants any pause to be structured with a deal with China so neither side's relative chances change, and criticises the AI Futures Project Plan A for giving China more relative influence if the pause breaks down.
framework🗣 Tom Davidsonconviction: mediumTom DavidsonAI Futures ProjectChina
So what: Pause design must account for what happens when it breaks down.
Katja Grace argues racing China mostly shortens timelines and raises risk, that the same incentive to avoid AI-caused destruction applies to Chinese leaders, and that too little effort goes into organising a mutual pause.
contrarian🗣 Katja Graceconviction: mediumKatja GraceChina
So what: Coordination with China is under-tried rather than infeasible.
≈ “I think we often talk about who ‘wins’ — and I think that’s sort of a bad way of describing it that we shouldn’t be doing.” paraphrase

0xResearch 1 episode(s) · 32 insights

Alt Season, Zcash, Collectibles & The Tokenization Trade61 min32 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
0xResearch panel with Blockworks Research's Carlos and Ryan on a crypto rally: SEC buyback FAQ de-risking revenue tokens, declining Bitcoin dominance, Zcash and the multi-store-of-value debate, on-chain collectibles gacha, and tokenized equities second-order plays like lending and neo-banks.
Regulatory de-risking of buyback tokens · 4Alt season and Bitcoin dominance · 6Multiple stores of value and Zcash · 6On-chain collectibles and gacha · 5Tokenized equities, lending and second-order plays · 5Neo-banks and consumer DeFi · 3Near-term catalysts and levels · 3
Regulatory de-risking of buyback tokens 4
An SEC FAQ released the prior week said token buybacks do not constitute essential managerial efforts when the crypto system is functional, which the host reads as removing unregistered-securities tail risk from revenue-generating tokens such as Hyperliquid and Pump.
data_point🗣 Host (Blockworks Research)conviction: highSECHyperliquidPumpCFTC
So what: Removes a left-tail risk that previously made holders of top-revenue tokens uneasy; expect more upside regulatory surprises from SEC and CFTC rulemaking.
“there's not there's not regulatory or or um unregistered securities uh tail risk to those instruments” ▶ 6:09
Ryan argues the SEC buyback FAQ barely moves the market because buybacks by Uniswap were already treated as safe, but it works as a final signal: a team still citing legal reasons for not supporting its token is probably lying or monetizing elsewhere.
contrarian🗣 Ryanconviction: mediumSECUniswap
So what: Use refusal to buy back tokens despite clarity as a red flag on team alignment with token holders.
Ryan notes that crypto clarity legislation did not pass and that pseudo-clarity is arriving through SEC and CFTC enforcement letters, which is not permanent and could reverse if Trump loses in 2028.
causal🗣 Ryanconviction: mediumSECCFTCTrump
So what: Regulatory tailwind is administrative rather than statutory, so it carries a political-cycle expiry risk.
Carlos argues buybacks can be defended even for early-stage crypto protocols as marketing spend, since price strength draws attention and users, unlike equity buybacks at early-stage companies; Derive reportedly raised its buyback allocation from 35 to 50%.
thesis🗣 Carlosconviction: mediumDerive
So what: In crypto, buybacks may be an efficient attention-and-reflexivity tool rather than wasted capital.
Alt season and Bitcoin dominance 6
Bitcoin dominance rose from a low of 40% to 65% during the 2023-2025 period, and over the past four to five weeks it has declined with broad alt outperformance.
data_point🗣 Host (Blockworks Research)conviction: mediumBitcoin
So what: Marks a possible regime change from Bitcoin-dominance to alt outperformance.
Carlos says alt season is effectively already underway but is selective: tokens like Pump, Hop, BBB, Near and Lit are up multiples while Bitcoin is down about 5% year to date, and he expects declining dominance over one to two years, not in a straight line.
prediction🗣 Carlosconviction: mediumPumpNearBitcoin
So what: Favor alts with clear fundamentals and revenue; indiscriminate alt bids would make him bearish.
Dispersion where the top-performing coins in the top 100 are all the same flavor regardless of fundamentals is read by Carlos as a late-stage signal for the mini run-up, though not necessarily the bull market.
framework🗣 Carlosconviction: medium
So what: Watch breadth quality of the top gainers as a short-term froth gauge.
Since the summer low, about 98% of the host's curated watch list outperforms Bitcoin, versus a prior regime where nearly all names underperformed and drew down more than Bitcoin.
data_point🗣 Host (Blockworks Research)conviction: mediumBitcoin
So what: A sharp pivot in relative strength supports rotation further out the risk curve.
The host gives two reasons Bitcoin dominance could fall over one to three years: application tokens becoming investable as revenue accrues to the token, and more instruments functioning as stores of value.
thesis🗣 Host (Blockworks Research)conviction: mediumBitcoinZcash
So what: Dominance decline would be fundamentals-driven, not just beta rotation.
Ryan counters that most capital allocators will not venture beyond Bitcoin, so new allocator interest could push Bitcoin dominance back up, and he is not convinced either way.
contrarian🗣 Ryanconviction: mediumBitcoin
So what: Institutional inflows are a risk to the declining-dominance thesis.
Multiple stores of value and Zcash 6
Ryan argues the set of store-of-value assets will expand beyond Bitcoin because value derives purely from social consensus, and social media plus on-chain trading make forming niche consensus far easier than in the era of physical gold; he treats this as a far-out 30-50 year view.
thesis🗣 Ryanconviction: lowBitcoinZcashPokemon cards
So what: Fragmentation of monetary premium into communities (gold, Bitcoin, PSA 10 Charizards, Rolex) is an exploratory long-dated idea.
“store value assets, their value is just purely derived from social consensus” ▶ 20:05
Ryan says Bitcoin has flaws, including lack of privacy, possible quantum risk and ossifying too early, and the market is hedging those with Zcash, which he calls the likely ring leader of alternative stores of value.
causal🗣 Ryanconviction: mediumBitcoinZcash
So what: Zcash demand is partly a hedge on Bitcoin's technical weaknesses.
Ryan notes that because gold, silver and Bitcoin form such a large store-of-value market, only a small capital shift to alternatives could significantly change relative valuations, for example gold falling to 10 trillion with alternatives absorbing the difference.
framework🗣 Ryanconviction: lowBitcoin
So what: Small reallocations from a huge base can move small alternative assets enormously.
Carlos expects only a handful of store-of-value assets, with Zcash occupying a silver-to-Bitcoin's-gold role like Litecoin once did, and does not expect 10 to 15 such assets.
prediction🗣 Carlosconviction: mediumZcashLitecoinBitcoin
So what: Position for Zcash plus one or two others rather than a broad store-of-value basket.
Zcash ETFs are approaching about 1 billion dollars of AUM, and ETFs plus digital asset treasuries together hold roughly 5.6-6% of Zcash supply, which the host cites as evidence that TradFi access is ending Bitcoin's monopoly on store-of-value flows.
data_point🗣 Host (Blockworks Research)conviction: mediumZcashBitcoin
So what: Instrument access, not just narrative, is driving Zcash demand.
When asked for a non-Zcash ticker to benefit from the store-of-value meme, a panelist reluctantly picks ETH for its longevity and baked-in monetary premium, while another says XRP could benefit from retail FOMO if Zcash reached 100 billion dollars.
anecdote🗣 Panelistsconviction: exploratoryETHXRPZcash
So what: ETH is the consensus, if reluctant, second store-of-value expression; XRP is a speculative retail spillover.
On-chain collectibles and gacha 5
The fundamental thesis for on-chain collectibles is that existing off-chain markets such as eBay are poor, with high fees and shipping costs, and putting them on chain creates better markets; gacha pack businesses have driven prices by buying large amounts of slabs.
thesis🗣 Ryanconviction: higheBayCourtyardCollector Crypt
So what: Gacha demand is the primary price driver for tokenized Pokemon and sports cards.
Secondary-market volume on on-chain collectibles platforms, as opposed to gacha pack-rip volume, is rising sharply, which Ryan calls the most important chart as proof these venues are better than eBay for trading.
data_point🗣 Ryanconviction: mediumeBay
So what: Secondary liquidity validates product-market fit beyond primary gacha sales.
Ryan describes a tokenization barbell: traditional instruments like stocks and ETFs on one end and collectibles on the other, and he finds the collectibles end more compelling because on-chain is clearly the better venue, whereas he would not trade tokenized Tesla over a brokerage account.
framework🗣 Ryanconviction: highTeslaCourtyardCollector Crypt
So what: RWAs win where the on-chain form factor is uniquely better, not where it merely replicates brokerage access.
“I'm not exactly sure most people would want to trade tokenized Tesla over Tesla through their brokerage account” ▶ 34:41
Coinbase teased an announcement letting users open card packs in its app, likely via a partner protocol rather than sourcing inventory itself; Collector Crypt, Courtyard and Beezie are named as candidates, with Cards (a token) reportedly pumping on it.
prediction🗣 Ryanconviction: lowCoinbaseCollector CryptCourtyard
So what: The partner chosen would be a large bullish catalyst for that protocol; a Coinbase tie-up also carries a tops-signal counterpoint since its NFT marketplace launched at the top of NFTs.
Ryan expects every collectible to eventually trade on chain, with gacha expanding to watches, Hot Wheels and rare coins, but predicts a boom-bust cycle because supply of gacha products will outpace demand since crypto users are especially drawn to Pokemon cards.
prediction🗣 Ryanconviction: mediumPokemon cards
So what: Expect overbuilt gacha categories with little adoption; Pokemon-like demand may not transfer.
Tokenized equities, lending and second-order plays 5
Carlos argues investors overweight DEXs as the way to play tokenized equities: current volume is mostly meme-equity pairings on Uniswap, Raydium and Meteora, and prop AMMs will likely take more share as volume shifts to USD pairs, while money markets that accept tokenized equities as collateral are overlooked.
contrarian🗣 Carlosconviction: mediumUniswapRaydiumMeteoraKamino

Morpho and Maple outstanding loans are both at all-time highs despite Bitcoin and ETH being near their lows, and an increasing share of Morpho loans is collateralized by RWAs rather than Bitcoin and ETH; about two-thirds of Onyc is collateral on Kamino, its largest RWA market.
data_point🗣 Host (Blockworks Research)conviction: mediumMorphoMapleKamino

data_point🗣 Carlosconviction: mediumBaseRobinhood

Issuers listing Nvidia, SPY, QQQ or Tesla on chain will not move the needle (about 0.1% of volume) because those are easily accessible; Carlos says issuers should list assets unavailable or illiquid in brokerage accounts, such as discounted crypto-adjacent equities trading near 0.3x mNAV like USD.
thesis🗣 Carlosconviction: mediumNvidiaTeslaAthena
So what: On-chain venues become the most relevant market for niche, distressed names crypto natives understand.
Ryan says first-order effects of bringing equities on chain are unexciting because they can already be traded well elsewhere; the value is in permissionless composability such as lending and consumer apps like Touch Grass and Grail.
thesis🗣 Ryanconviction: mediumRobinhood
So what: Look for products only possible with permissionless rails.
Neo-banks and consumer DeFi 3
Carlos argues Athena Pay has structurally better economics than Revolut, Robinhood or EtherFi because, as a stablecoin issuer, it monetizes float from day one through USD mint fees and yield and does not depend on interchange or share net interest margin with partner banks.
thesis🗣 Carlosconviction: mediumAthenaEtherFiRevolutRobinhood
So what: Stablecoin issuers can subsidize better consumer products than bank-partnered neo-banks.
Athena Pay is still early with under 4 million dollars of balances and not open to the public, while an app described as the 'A app' passed 17 million dollars of deposits within a month of iOS early access, offering about 6% on stables with deposit protection.
data_point🗣 Carlosconviction: lowAthena
So what: The less-discussed savings app has about four times the balances of Athena Pay; Carlos would not fade it despite the Kelp incident.
The shift in crypto is from infrastructure to consumer: stablecoins, tokenization and performant chains are in place, and the question is packaging them into a UX on par with web2 neo-banks, such as EtherFi letting users borrow against stocks permissionlessly.
thesis🗣 Carlosconviction: mediumEtherFiAthena
So what: Winners will be consumer products using DeFi rails for capabilities traditional neo-banks cannot offer.
