Animal Spirits · Carlson & Batnick 1 episode(s) · 32 insights

Will Higher Rates Kill the Stock Market? | Animal Spirits 48460 min32 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Animal Spirits 484: Carlson and Batnick argue rising rates (10-year near 5.3%) are the market's obvious excuse to sell off but doubt it, citing multiple compression everywhere, narrow breadth and zero fear. They discuss buying bonds at 5% yields, strong household wealth, rents back on trend, private credit redemptions, and personal AI agents like Instinct disrupting subscriptions.
Rising rates versus resilient stocks · 6Doubt without fear: breadth and sentiment · 9Buying bonds at 5% yields · 4Household wealth and the K-shaped economy · 4Housing, rent and mortgage rates · 3Private credit stress · 1Personal AI agents and consumer disruption · 4Entertainment and creative-class decline · 1
Rising rates versus resilient stocks 6
The hosts argue rising rates are the obvious excuse for a stock selloff: Japan 1989, the dot-com bubble, 1929 and the GFC were all preceded by central bank tightening, and the market now prices the Fed raising rates to roughly 4.5-5%.
thesis🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumFedS&P 500
So what: Rates are the main identifiable risk to equities, yet both hosts still expect the market not to take the excuse.
“The market has an excuse to sell off if it wants it. Will it take it?” ▶ 1:00
The 10-year Treasury yield rose from about 4% to 5.2-5.3% in the first nine months of 2026 while the S&P 500 is still up about 13% year to date; the hosts note that people told this in advance would have predicted a 15% decline, not a 15% gain.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: high10-yearS&P 500
So what: Equities are rising through a roughly 130bp yield spike, so valuation-from-rates logic is not currently binding.
Treasury yields bottomed in March 2020 below 50bp and have since risen to about 5.2% while the S&P 500 returned about 19% annualized, which the hosts cite as proof that higher rates do not mechanically lower equity prices.
data_point🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: highS&P 500Treasury yields
So what: Finance-101 discounting math is a poor short-run timing tool; rate level alone has not capped stock returns.
“This is why there's no rules.” ▶ 9:37
The hosts' explanation for equities holding up despite surging yields is that rates are rising because growth is strong and hyperscaler AI spending supports earnings, with the Atlanta Fed GDPNow tracking Q3 GDP near 5%.
causal🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumAtlanta FedHyperscalersAI
So what: Equities can tolerate higher yields only while rates reflect growth rather than inflation or term-premium stress; a fall in GDPNow or earnings would remove the support.
Michael Batnick says the market usually does not give investors a long time to exit, and expects the current selloff beneath the indexes to pass and a rally into year end, though he flags he could be wrong.
prediction🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: lowS&P 500
So what: A year-end rip is the hosts' modest base case, conditional on rates not escalating.
“I would not be surprised if we get past this this bout of selling underneath the the market underneath the indexes and you get a rip into the year end.”
Both hosts concede there is no clear line in the sand for yields: if the 10-year reached 5.6% next week they do not see how stocks avoid cracking, and rates are the single caveat on their otherwise sanguine view.
thesis🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: medium10-year
So what: A further leg up in the 10-year toward 5.5-5.6% is the identifiable trigger for a real equity drawdown.
Doubt without fear: breadth and sentiment 9
A 22V Research basket of 81 AI capex-exposed companies across 12 segments trades at about 15x EV/EBITDA, only 1.14x its pre-2026 average, implying no valuation premium remains in the AI trade; Nvidia was cited at 16x earnings the prior week.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: medium22V researchNvidiaAI
So what: The AI complex has de-rated to near-normal multiples, so the bull case rests on earnings delivery rather than multiple expansion.
Per Duality Research, every S&P sector except consumer staples (up less than 1%) has seen multiple contraction in 2026, with double-digit compression in consumer discretionary, communication services, materials, technology, energy and the index overall.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: highduality researchconsumer staplesS&P 500
So what: Index strength is masking broad valuation compression; earnings, not multiples, are driving 2026 returns.
Despite the doubt, the VIX is about 16 and consumer staples and utilities relative to the S&P 500 are at new multi-year lows, meaning investors are not rotating into defensives, which the hosts read as lots of doubt but basically zero fear.
thesis🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumVIXconsumer staplesutilitiesS&P 500
So what: Absence of defensive buying and a low VIX suggest no hedging demand, which leaves little cushion if rates force a break.
“there's a lot of doubt, a lot of doubt, but basically zero fear”
The S&P 500 is within 1% of a new high while more than 70% of its members are at least 10% below their highs; the only other time in 30 years this occurred was right before the dot-com bubble burst.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumS&P 500Jason Gford
So what: Extreme narrowness is a historical warning sign, though the hosts note this signal can persist.
The 52-week-low list is dominated by rate-sensitive sectors: 21 of 28 names came from utilities, staples and real estate, so the deterioration reflects higher-rate exposure rather than broad weakness.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumduality researchutilitiesconsumer staplesreal estate
So what: Damage is concentrated in bond proxies, consistent with a rates shock rather than an earnings recession.
Year to date the S&P 500 is up about 13%, but only 35% of members outperform the index and about 40% are down, with around 20 stocks up 100% or more, making returns highly concentrated; memory names remain leaders even off highs.
data_point🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumS&P 500Y Charts
So what: Index return is a poor guide to the typical stock; stock-picker experience is far worse than the headline.
Tech ETF rolling 12-month net flows are at an unprecedented, roughly triple prior-cycle, level, Robinhood traders' 21-day net single-stock purchases are near record, and yet a Goldman positioning indicator is at its most negative since June 2025, so sentiment and flow gauges are contradicting each other.
data_point🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumCallum ThomasRobin HoodGoldman Sachs
So what: Crowded retail and ETF tech buying coexists with institutional caution; Ben Carlson says to trust flows over surveys.
Ben Carlson argues sentiment indicators are broken, because the stock market and consumer sentiment have decoupled (record highs versus record-low sentiment, below 1980s, COVID and GFC levels), so investors should watch what people actually do rather than what they say.
contrarian🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumconsumer sentiment
So what: Survey-based sentiment gauges give unreliable contrarian signals in this cycle; positioning and flows are more informative.
“you trust the flows, not the sentiment indicators” ▶ 12:06
Macro backdrop cited: mortgage rates about 7.3%, unemployment about 4%, bond yields above 5%, cash yields about 4%, oil near $100, gasoline about $5, inflation about 3.5%, all alongside stock-market all-time highs; the hosts say people would worry about these if stocks fell.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediummortgage ratesOilinflation
So what: Macro stress is being ignored because price is going up; a price decline would re-rate attention to the same data.
“Price price rules everything around me.” ▶ 10:30
Buying bonds at 5% yields 4
Ben Carlson bought bond ETFs in his brokerage account (a mix of short, intermediate and a long position via TLT bought shortly before recording) for yield rather than as a rate-timing call, while his 401k remains 100% stocks; he sold small stock positions such as Netflix at a roughly 20% loss and Spotify.
anecdote🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumTLTNetflixSpotify
So what: A prominent allocator is rotating small discretionary stock positions into 5% yields, a risk-reward rather than a top-calling trade.
Both hosts stress the bond purchase is not a call that rates have topped, since the trend is clearly higher, but a yield-and-risk-reward decision: they like earning roughly 5% for the first time in their investing lives.
thesis🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumbonds
So what: Yield level is now attractive enough to own duration without a view on timing, though they accept being early.
The Bloomberg US Aggregate Index (back-dated to 1976) has a negative rolling 5-year annualized return of about -0.3% for the first time ever and a 10-year return of about 1.4%, the worst 10-year real return in its history, because of low starting yields, rapidly rising rates and high inflation together.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: highBloombergLayman Brothers
So what: The worst-ever bond decade resets starting yields higher, which is the forward-looking argument for owning bonds.
“You couldn't ask for a worse bond market than we just lived through.” ▶ 18:52
Bonds offer a kicker versus T-bills: if inflation and growth stall, bond prices rise, whereas three-month T-bills at about 4% have no interest rate risk and would do fine if the Fed keeps hiking.
framework🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumT billsFed
So what: Cash at 4% is a legitimate alternative, and bonds are the convex bet on a growth or inflation stall.
Household wealth and the K-shaped economy 4
Bank of America data show US household assets rose above 10 times liabilities in Q2 2026, and the hosts argue the debate overweights liabilities relative to assets.
data_point🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumBank of America
So what: Household balance sheets are historically strong, supporting spending resilience despite weak sentiment.
A JP Morgan report finds 8% of people were net withdrawers from investments from February to April 2026, versus 4% in 2019 and 2.4% in 2015, and the top income segment's share rose from 6.6% in 2015 to 20%, with withdrawals above 10% of spending for the top 10%.
data_point🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: highJP Morgan
So what: The stock-market wealth effect is feeding consumption, so an equity decline would transmit directly to high-income spending.
Figures cited from the KO Institute (as transcribed) show the lowest income fifth has seen the biggest inflation-adjusted income gains since 1990, and the share of households earning $150,000 or more (inflation-adjusted) rose from 5% in 1967 to almost 30%, which contradicts the K-shaped economy narrative.
contrarian🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumKO InstituteWall Street Journal
So what: Income growth at the low end undermines the pure K-shaped framing and helps explain resilient consumption.
Michael Batnick dismisses the idea that baby boomers cashing out retirement accounts would crash the stock market as a dumb take, while allowing that boomer retirement partly explains rising investment withdrawals.
contrarian🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumbaby boomers401k
So what: Demographic selling fears have not materialized; the withdrawal trend is a spending-support story.
Housing, rent and mortgage rates 3
John Arnold's figure: national median rent is $1,390 as of August 2026, almost exactly where it would sit had rent growth stayed on its pre-pandemic ~2% annualized path from 2017; the 2021 spike has fully unwound since 2022.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: highJohn Arnoldrent
So what: Shelter inflation, the biggest outlay for most households, is back on trend, which is disinflationary even with 3.5% headline inflation.
With mortgage rates at about 7.3%, the hosts see no positive news for housing and think buyers can no longer afford to overpay as they did in 2025-26; Ben Carlson estimates his own 3% mortgage would be worth around 60 cents on the dollar, a loss borne by whoever holds the mortgage bonds.
thesis🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediummortgage rates
So what: Housing activity stays depressed while mortgage rates are above 7%, and lock-in persists.
A proposed Senate plan would give first-time buyers a 5-to-1 federal match on down payment savings up to $50,000; the hosts say they are fine with it though they would rather subsidize supply than demand.
anecdote🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: lowfirst-time home buyers
So what: Demand-side housing subsidies are politically live but do not address the supply shortage.
Private credit stress 1
Redemption requests at private credit funds are rising each quarter: about 15% for an Apollo fund of roughly $26 billion (versus about 17% prior quarter per the hosts) and 11% for a Morgan Stanley fund, while listed alternative managers have fallen sharply (Carlyle from $70 to $40 in a year, Blue Owl at new lows).
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumApolloMorgan StanleyCarlileBlue Owl
So what: Gated or stressed private-credit vehicles and weak alt-manager equities point to ongoing liquidity strain, with media waiting on a big default.
Personal AI agents and consumer disruption 4
Michael Batnick reports heavy use of the Instinct AI agent, a text-message assistant that executes tasks such as repricing a Disney subscription bundle, scheduling, and moving money, and says its 23-year-old founder reportedly raised a billion dollars at a $10 billion valuation while growing 10% a day, with 40% of users sharing a personal credit card after three weeks.
anecdote🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumInstinctMuse
So what: Consumer agents that execute rather than advise are gaining rapid adoption, but valuation and security risks are high.
“It doesn't just tell you what's happening and then makes you do it.” ▶ 32:07
Michael Batnick argues agents will cancel unused subscriptions, which explains the sell-off in a Goldman Sachs consumer inertia basket including Planet Fitness and the New York Times, whose businesses rely on customer inertia.
causal🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumGoldman SachsPlanet FitnessNew York Times
So what: Subscription businesses that depend on forgotten billing face churn risk, and may respond by raising prices or blocking agents.
Torsten Slok's scenario is that agentic assistants could sweep household cash automatically into accounts paying 3.3-5% versus about 0.1% on average checking accounts, depriving banks of cheap deposits; the hosts call this very stupid because banks can block account access and the barrier to moving cash is already low.
contrarian🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: lowTorson SllockMuse
So what: The agent-driven deposit flight thesis is contested; friction, bank access control and low behavioural elasticity weigh against it.
Ben Carlson says he does not care about AI slop, treating it as a filter for ignoring content, and rejects the idea that everything will become AI-produced; he worries more that AI-driven quality decline could hurt movies.
contrarian🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: lowAI slop
So what: AI-generated content will be discounted by consumers rather than crowd out human work, in his view.
Entertainment and creative-class decline 1
Joey Politano reports America has lost more than 200,000 creative jobs in four years, including 50,000 in the last year alone, among the worst stretches for media employment in modern US history comparable only to 2001 and 2008, while movie-theater tickets as a share of consumer spending remain far below the pre-COVID level.
data_point🗣 Ben Carlsonconviction: mediumJoey Politano
So what: Structural shrinkage of video and media spending and employment, with AI integration into the arts still to come.

The Ezra Klein Show 1 episode(s) · 31 insights

Bill Gates: A.I. ‘Makes Nuclear Weapons Look Like Nothing’ | The Ezra Klein Show74 min31 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Bill Gates tells Ezra Klein that AI has already crossed cyber and bio danger thresholds, argues for mandatory monitoring safeguards in all models, and lays out a bleak view of AI job displacement with an AI payroll tax proposal. He also covers AI's benefits in low-income countries, education, and Gates Foundation priorities.
AI cyber and bio threshold crossed · 11AI job displacement thesis · 9Global south, philanthropy and Gates Foundation · 6AI in education and kids · 5
AI cyber and bio threshold crossed 11
Bill Gates says AI crossed the cyber and bio capability thresholds early this year, meaning AI today can empower a bioterrorist to kill hundreds of millions or enable a cyberattack that scrambles bank accounts and shuts down the electric grid.
thesis🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highBill GatesAI
So what: Frames AI misuse by humans, not rogue AI, as the imminent risk; supports demand for safeguards regulation.
“we crossed the the cyber threshold and we crossed the bio threshold early this year” ▶ 12:15
Gates says recent frontier models leading up to Mythos find security bugs in code that humans reviewed for over 20 years, in minutes, leaving the world in a period of extreme vulnerability to cyberattack because there is far too much code to patch.
data_point🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highMythosAnthropicGlass Wing
So what: Cyber-defense demand and patching urgency rise; the offense/defense gap favors attackers near term.
“we're just in a a period of extreme vulnerability uh to cyber attack” ▶ 13:55
Gates argues AI-driven molecule design is ultimately dual use: capabilities once limited to nation states, such as engineering something worse than smallpox with a long asymptomatic infectious period, have passed to small groups using the latest AI tools.
causal🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highGates FoundationAI
So what: Biosecurity risk is diffusing downward in actor sophistication, a case for bio-screening and monitoring requirements.
Gates says open-source models can be modified to create bioweapons with all monitoring disabled and that this exists today, so there is no effective filtering, which undercuts the argument that product liability and corporate incentives suffice.
contrarian🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highopen-source modelsJensen HuangNvidia
So what: Rebuts the view (attributed to Jensen Huang, Trump, David Sacks) that the race dynamic is fake and self-regulation works.
“You can take an open- source model that can create boweapons and disable any monitoring of any kind and this exists today.” ▶ 16:11
Gates says Anthropic's Mythos and Fable filters are tuned so aggressively that cancer questions push users down to Opus, Sonnet and Haiku, so serious users must obtain special-permission copies or move to unsafeguarded open-source models, illustrating the difficulty of separating good from evil molecule work.
anecdote🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumAnthropicMythosFableOpusSonnetHaiku
So what: Over-restrictive safeguards push serious users toward unmonitored models, a product and policy tradeoff.
Gates says the imminent AI risk is not recursive self-improvement or loss of control but humans using the most powerful tools ever, which unlike nuclear weapons or rockets were not funded by government R&D and are not significantly purchased by government.
thesis🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highrecursive self-improvementUS governmentNSA
So what: Government lacks technical visibility and a customer-based lever, so oversight must be imposed by regulation.
“The control problem is a serious problem, but the imminent risk is not RSI.” ▶ 19:31
Gates says the necessary step is requiring monitoring and safeguards in all models, that this will not meaningfully slow progress, and that its only effect on open source is that models must stay on a platform where the sovereign can verify monitoring has not been removed.
prediction🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highopen sourceChina
So what: A concrete regulatory design (mandatory monitored platforms) rather than a slowdown, which may be the politically feasible path.
Gates predicts that without required safeguards the world will see major cyber and bio events within the next few years, after which monitoring will finally be mandated.
prediction🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highAIcyberattackbioattack
So what: Event-driven regulation is the default path; tail-risk events are the catalyst.
“in the next few years you will have major major cyber and bio events” ▶ 26:43
Gates rejects the framing that the US must beat China, saying neither can win over the other because Pandora's box is open for malicious actors, and he expects China would cooperate on humanity-level protections, a proposition he says should be tested.
contrarian🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumChinaUS
So what: Challenges the dominant policy argument against regulation; US-China cooperation on AI safety is an open option.
“you'd think we'd move up from that nationalistic view to kind of a humanity level view” ▶ 26:00
Gates says AI company safety charters that permit overriding profit maximization only work if there is a single company, and with many competitors a Pandora's box one lab keeps closed will be opened by another.
causal🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumOpenAIDeepMindAnthropic
So what: Voluntary lab commitments are structurally weak, which supports external regulation.
Gates says he now expects AI to be a peer in the Gates Foundation's strategy reviews for 2027 spending of about $10 billion, engaging in dialogue with ChatGPT, Claude and Copilot, whereas a year earlier the idea was a joke.
anecdote🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumGates FoundationChatGPTClaudeCopilot
So what: Illustrates how fast AI moved from toy to participant in high-level strategic work, though not final decisions.
AI job displacement thesis 9
Gates says AI has so far created more jobs than it destroyed, and coding is the only profession where AI has truly crossed the reliability threshold, but within a few years accounting, legal work, telesales and telesupport will cross it.
prediction🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highaccountinglegaltelesalestelesupport
So what: Near-term white-collar exposure is concentrated in reliability-gated, voice/text service work.
Gates argues Jevons-style demand elasticity does not protect jobs once a task is fully automated, because the spend moves into the token budget rather than the human salary budget; he cites radial tires lasting four times longer, which did not quadruple driving and cut tire factory employment to a quarter.
causal🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highJevons paradoxradial tiresAmazon
So what: Elasticity arguments work only where humans retain a role; demand growth does not recreate payroll.
“As soon as you complete the entire task, it doesn't matter that there's demand elasticity. That goes into the token budget.” ▶ 31:51
Gates says that as AI and humanoid robots cross reliability thresholds in white-collar and blue-collar work, jobs are destroyed with no high ground left, unlike past technologies where displaced labor moved to a scarcer human skill, because human cognition itself is no longer the scarce element.
thesis🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highhumanoid robots
So what: Rejects the historical reallocation analogy; labor-market risk is broad rather than sectoral.
“you destroy jobs and you leave no high ground” ▶ 32:16
Gates says insurers still processing medical claims with humans will be undercut by competitors with very few employees that change the industry's pricing model, so quality thresholds, not consumer preference for humans, determine adoption.
prediction🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediuminsuranceWaymoLimbic
So what: Competitive entrants with AI-native cost structures can reprice incumbent-heavy services industries.
Gates says Amazon's Mechanical Turk, which an economist questioned could still exist if AI were as good as he claimed, was completely shut down a week after he said it would not last.
anecdote🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: lowMechanical TurkAmazon
So what: Anecdotal early data point of human-microtask marketplaces being displaced by AI.
Gates expects the transition to a super-abundance period to take a probable 20-plus years, with job loss and bio events needing to be minimized, and says the good effects (lower cost of food, shelter, education, health) arrive more slowly than the bio, cyber, psychosocial and jobs risks because those sectors are highly regulated.
prediction🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumAI
So what: Risks front-load while benefits lag, creating a political permission-to-operate problem for AI within about 5 years.
“the good stuff if we're not careful arrives more slowly than the biocyber risk” ▶ 47:54
Gates proposes taxing AI labor by defining a unit of labor and charging the same FICA payroll tax whether delivered by a human or a robot, arguing the current tax structure favors capital and disadvantages labor and that pension funds like Social Security are already on a path to go bankrupt.
thesis🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumFICASocial Security
So what: A robot or AI payroll tax is a live policy idea that would raise automation's cost at the margin.
Gates proposes human-reserved jobs, where society decides in advance to maintain human employment in areas such as child care, elder care, some medical care and some education, likened to nature reserves.
framework🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: exploratorychild careelder care
So what: A speculative policy path that would carve out protected labor segments.
Gates says stopping data centers will not slow AI one iota because they will simply be built elsewhere, so opposing data centers to slow AI is wasted effort.
contrarian🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highdata centers
So what: Data center local opposition shifts location, not aggregate AI progress.
“stopping data centers isn't going to slow this thing one iota” ▶ 48:26
Global south, philanthropy and Gates Foundation 6
Gates says AI should be pushed as fast as possible in low-income countries, where it is overwhelmingly positive (language access, health advice, agriculture), while job effects hit middle-income countries such as the Philippines and India after rich countries and reach low-income countries later.
thesis🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highPhilippinesIndiaGates Foundation
So what: Sequencing implies BPO and services exposure in middle-income economies lags the rich world.
The Gates Foundation announced a five-year goal for about 3.4 billion people who speak underrepresented languages to use AI tools in their own language; Gates says Yoruba performance is about 10 times worse than English and only about 10 languages are close to English.
data_point🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highGates FoundationYoruba
So what: Large language gaps in model quality leave an opportunity for localized models and data.
Gates says over a million farmers in India already use an AI system giving crop disease, fertilizer and variety advice, and agriculture is the application moving full speed ahead, while health and education barriers in low-income countries are tokens, smartphones and connectivity rather than regulation.
data_point🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumIndiaGates Foundation
So what: Agriculture advisory is the early scaled AI use case in emerging markets.
Gates says two things changed his foundation plans: exponential AI capability, and a reduction in generosity toward the poorest; lives are saved for about $1,000 each, so less philanthropy and aid means fewer lives saved.
data_point🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: highGates FoundationUSAID
So what: Aid cuts reduce global-health outcomes regardless of AI gains.
Gates says US foreign aid fell to about half a percent of the budget and is heading toward a quarter percent, while Americans estimate it at more than 5%, and he expects any rebuilt aid to compete with AI-related safety-net spending.
data_point🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumUS foreign aid
So what: Fiscal competition for safety-net dollars is a political constraint on both aid and AI-displacement support.
Gates says the Gates Foundation plans to spend down about $200 billion by 2045 and that his AI-specific commitment of about a billion dollars excludes drug-discovery work.
data_point🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: lowGates Foundation
So what: Context on scale of AI-related philanthropic spend.
AI in education and kids 5
Gates says children in most rich countries, including the US, are learning less than 10 or 20 years ago, with England as a counter-trend outlier after going back to basics in teaching reading; he says high expectations are winning over free-form creativity.
data_point🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumEnglandUnited States
So what: Evidence-based back-to-basics teaching is a contrarian education signal.
Gates says small-scale AI classroom pilots, such as a New York City program where teachers get instant data on which concepts students struggle with, look promising, but education innovations historically wash out when scaled beyond self-selected teachers.
thesis🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumNew York City
So what: Expect implementation risk when scaling AI edtech.
Gates warns AI learning tools can widen gaps because motivated students benefit most, as with Khan Academy, and the median student may be discouraged, so equity requires AI that supplies personalized motivation.
causal🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumKhan Academy
So what: Edtech outcomes may skew toward already-motivated students.
Gates favors monitoring over a ban for kids using AI, with parents seeing full dialogue for young children and less visibility as children age, while noting parental controls often fail due to parents' technical limitations.
framework🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: mediumCommon Sense Media
So what: Demand for monitored, age-tiered AI platforms for children.
Gates says he is not worried about political engagement but about polarization: if the AI debate becomes one party for and one against, as with climate change, there is no room for a middle ground.
prediction🗣 Bill Gatesconviction: medium2028 presidential election
So what: Polarized AI politics would make bipartisan safeguards harder.

Conversations with Tyler · Cowen 1 episode(s) · 30 insights

Luis Garicano on Spain, Europe, and the Future of Work59 min30 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Tyler Cowen interviews economist Luis Garicano on Spain's housing rigidity, pension-driven stagnation and political economy, EU institutional gridlock, and his Messy Jobs thesis that AI will not cause mass unemployment because jobs get messier. He also argues Europe should be a fast second-mover AI adopter rather than pursue sovereignty.
Spain political economy and productivity · 7EU institutions, industrial policy and capital markets · 9Messy Jobs: AI and employment · 10AI sovereignty and Europe's AI strategy · 4
Spain political economy and productivity 7
Spain's housing supply is rigid: it built up to 800,000 houses a year before the bubble but now builds under 100,000 a year while taking in hundreds of thousands of immigrants annually, with essentially no YIMBY movement.
data_point🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highSpain
So what: Persistent Spanish housing scarcity and price pressure as immigration continues.
Post-bubble Spain restricted municipalities' land-use decision rights to curb corruption, which left decision-makers scared and supply completely rigid, a pattern of accountability reforms that disempower the people who could act.
causal🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumSpain
So what: Anti-corruption rules can create supply-side stagnation in housing.
Spain's productivity has not grown for about three decades; growth is extensive (immigration, tourism), and 100 percent of GDP growth since 2008 has gone to pensions because the country is governed by and for older retirees (fifth-lowest fertility, fifth-highest life expectancy).
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highSpain
So what: Low productive investment and fiscal drag from pensions cap Spain's medium-term growth.
Spain could become a Florida or Austin-like magnet for talent, energy and data centers thanks to cheap solar, wind and possible nuclear on empty land, but the constraint is political economy, and Spain is instead closing nuclear plants for political reasons.
prediction🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: lowSpainTexasAustin
So what: Spanish energy abundance is a real option for data-center siting but depends on policy reform.
Garicano expects Spain to reform only through a crisis, citing past reform episodes in 1959 under American pressure, the mid-1980s socialist government, the run-up to the euro, and 2012, with pre-euro reform momentum in Southern Europe fading once the euro removed urgency.
causal🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumSpainTano SantosJesús Fernández-Villaverde
So what: Without a crisis, expect Spanish reform inertia; a crisis is the catalyst for pro-growth change.
≈ “It could do the reforms it needs. It’s going to have to be through a crisis, I’m afraid.” paraphrase
Party-list proportional systems select for people whose outside option is zero and who are subordinate to the party, so politicians are not selected for competence; this explains the weak Spanish Socialist Party's silence amid corruption scandals around Prime Minister Sánchez and the choice of weak European Commission presidents.
causal🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumSánchezSocialist PartyEuropean Commission
So what: Political-selection adverse selection implies weak institutional leadership persists in Spain and the EU.
The Basque Country collects its own taxes and negotiates its contribution (the cupo) bilaterally, timing talks when it holds swing votes, so it is a net recipient despite being among the richest regions; Catalonia wanting the same arrangement would break the budget, making the system unstable with no solution.
causal🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumBasque CountryCataloniaSpain
So what: Fiscal-federalism instability in Spain remains a structural political risk.
EU institutions, industrial policy and capital markets 9
Industrial policy works only if losers are allowed to fail and objectives are clear; Garicano judges Europe fails both tests in autos, since aid goes to legacy carmakers and goals are a melange of conflicting environmental, labor and security aims.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highEuropean UnionDani Rodrik
So what: Skeptical on EU tariffs/quotas rescuing legacy automakers.
≈ “you need to have an objective, a clear-cut objective” paraphrase
European car companies are being destroyed in third-world markets, where only BYDs are visible in Latin America and Africa, so protection at home would not solve the competitive problem.
data_point🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumBYDLatin AmericaAfrica
So what: Chinese EV share gains extend beyond Europe, threatening European auto exports.
≈ “In Latin America, you only see BYDs now” paraphrase
Europe's core problems are labor regulation that blocks fast adaptation and weak incentives for radical rather than incremental innovation; Europe is good at incremental innovation and poor at radical change.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highEurope
So what: Structural, not tariff-based, explanation for European tech and industrial underperformance.
Rigid labor markets mainly raise the cost of making mistakes: Facebook can pivot from metaverse engineers to AI engineers in a week while Ericsson or Nokia would need months of labor-council negotiation and might go under in the meantime.
causal🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highFacebookEricssonNokia
So what: European incumbents are structurally slower to reallocate labor in fast technology shifts such as AI.
Garicano favors Danish-style flexicurity, high unemployment insurance combined with easy hiring and firing, as the best fix for European labor rigidity, while noting it costs significant money.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumDenmark
So what: Labor reform in Europe requires fiscal commitment to be politically viable.
The EU keeps unanimity on taxes, defense and foreign policy because they are core sovereignty; this causes duplicated armaments procurement with incompatible shells, and any single loser blocks change so only Pareto improvements pass. Garicano would move to majority rule for US-federal-like functions.
causal🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highEuropean UnionGermanyFranceIreland
So what: European defense-industrial fragmentation persists despite high spending.
Euro bonds cannot work at scale without joint revenue and shared spending control, comparable to housemates sharing a credit card; the recovery fund issued bonds without real joint revenue.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highEuro bondsrecovery and reconstruction fund
So what: Expect no durable eurozone fiscal union or large common safe asset under current institutions.
European startup financing is limited less by cash than by missing know-how: Europe exports money to US startups, and the talent leaves too, but it lacks experienced operators like a Spotify marketing chief turned investor; Sweden's capital-gains deferral on reinvestment in startups created a flywheel.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumPer StrömbergSwedenSpotifyAnthropic
So what: Operator-investor ecosystems and tax design matter more than harmonized capital-market rules.
The EU capital markets package gives ESMA more power without removing national regulators' power, so it layers rather than harmonizes and adds hurdles for fundraising.
contrarian🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumESMASEC
So what: Capital markets union reforms likely to disappoint as new layers are added without removing old ones.
≈ “we add this new law which is harmonized, but leave all the lower-layer laws there” paraphrase
Messy Jobs: AI and employment 10
Garicano's core claim is that AI will not cause mass unemployment, perhaps not even higher unemployment, because jobs will become messier and AI cannot do the messy parts.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highMessy JobsJin LiYanhui Wu
So what: Argues against mass-AI-unemployment narratives.
“I think that’s completely right. Yes.” ▶ 41:26
Clean, specifiable, verifiable single-task jobs, such as those offshored to India and much remote work, are the most exposed to AI because reinforcement-learning loops can be run on them.
causal🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highIndiareinforcement learning
So what: Offshored back-office and BPO work is the first at risk from AI.
The messiness that persists is relational and tied to the knowledge problem described by Hayek and Polanyi, which does not disappear as AI advances, though some contingent messiness AI can streamline.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumHayekPolanyi
So what: Tacit and relational knowledge work is a durable human niche.
Weak-bundle jobs break up with parts done by AI, while strong-bundle jobs, like a bank relationship manager whose analysis is bound to client meetings, are augmented rather than displaced.
framework🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highrelationship manager
So what: Job bundling strength predicts whether AI displaces or augments a role.
Retraining programs generally do not work, partly because job identity is tied to social identity; Garicano suggests use-case training, such as telling bank relationship managers what to ask an LLM before a client meeting, is easier than retraining into new jobs.
framework🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumRaffaella SadunHBS
So what: AI adoption gains come from task-level training, not occupational retraining.
The hardest skill with LLMs is knowing what they can do, and models are becoming easier to use as prompting skill becomes less necessary.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumLLMs
So what: Diffusion bottleneck is awareness of use cases rather than frontier access.
Garicano believes the natural rate of unemployment is rising more because of participation issues such as disability and mental illness than because of a permanent retraining need from automation.
contrarian🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: lownatural rate of unemployment
So what: Labor-market slack likely stems from health and participation, not AI displacement.
A Chinese CEO told Garicano and his co-authors that each IT worker was more productive yet departmental output did not change, suggesting workers are 'smoking the productivity gains in the corridor'.
anecdote🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: lowChinese CEO
So what: Individual AI productivity gains may not show up in firm-level output.
≈ “I think they are smoking the productivity gains in the corridor.” paraphrase
An NBER Summer Institute paper on medical scribes found adoption reached 100 percent quickly, with doctors scheduling more meetings and doing more; Garicano says Jevons-type effects mean productivity gains can raise work rather than leisure.
data_point🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumNBER Summer InstituteJevons effect
So what: AI productivity may be consumed as more output, not a leisure dividend.
Shadow use of personal AI at work delivers welfare gains through lower stress and leisure that corporate metrics miss.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: lowAI
So what: Official productivity data may undercount AI benefits.
AI sovereignty and Europe's AI strategy 4
Europe and Spain are nowhere on AI hardware (except ASML), frontier models (Mistral trails the Chinese models) or data centers, so Garicano rejects AI sovereignty as a realistic objective.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: highASMLMistralEuropeSpain
So what: Do not expect an independent European AI stack.
Garicano advocates a second-mover strategy for Europe: be a fast, aggressive AI adopter that captures the welfare gains of US investment, similar to railroads where investors did not capture the value, and use big-customer bargaining power as China did with Nvidia.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumNvidiaJesús Saa-RequejoChina
So what: Frames US AI capex as potentially yielding diffuse welfare gains rather than concentrated value capture.
≈ “Let’s be the ones who enjoy those welfare gains by adopting AI very aggressively” paraphrase
Garicano argues that frontier models are needed for research, innovation and adversarial applications like war, but law, medicine, accounting and proprietary corporate work can run on models three to four months behind the frontier if the harness and data are in-house.
contrarian🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumopen weightsPieter
So what: Supports open-weight and lagging models as sufficient for most enterprise use, a headwind to frontier-model premium for those uses.
≈ “you don’t need a frontier model. You can be three, four months behind the frontier” paraphrase
Model switching is easy at present, needing only a change of API call, so keeping applications interoperable preserves buyer leverage and a contestable market.
thesis🗣 Luis Garicanoconviction: mediumAPI
So what: Low switching costs limit model-vendor pricing power.

What Bitcoin Did · Peter McCormack 1 episode(s) · 30 insights

The Brutal Reality of the UK Economy | Ross Clark70 min30 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Journalist Ross Clark argues the UK is heading toward a sovereign debt crisis: debt near 100% of GDP, 109bn pounds of annual interest, a 133bn deficit, a quarter of gilts index-linked, and no party with a credible plan to cut spending. He argues the choice is cuts now or IMF-imposed austerity later.
UK debt and interest burden · 5Fiscal crisis scenarios and precedents · 7Political economy and voter behaviour · 6Budget cuts and welfare reform · 7Growth, taxes and private-sector incentives · 5
UK debt and interest burden 5
UK accumulated public debt is roughly equal to GDP at about 3.4 trillion pounds, and the government spent 109 billion pounds last year on debt interest alone, versus about 60 billion on defence and 120 billion on education.
data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: highUKgilts
So what: Debt service now rivals the largest budget lines, crowding out discretionary spending and raising fiscal-crisis sensitivity.
“We spent 109 billion pound last year just paying the interest on our debt.” ▶ 2:00
The UK deficit was 133 billion pounds last year, and no UK government has balanced the books in 25 years; the last surplus was Gordon Brown's small one in 2000-2001.
data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: highGordon BrownUK
So what: A structural deficit with no political plan to close it means persistent gilt supply.
Gilt yields have climbed about a full percentage point above their level under Liz Truss's 2022 mini-budget, which Clark reads as failing global investor confidence in the UK.
data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: highLiz Trussgilts
So what: Markets are already pricing worse fiscal credibility than the 2022 episode that was considered cataclysmic.
“the yield on um government bonds climb to a whole percentage point higher than it was under Liz Trust” ▶ 2:34
About a quarter of UK government debt is index-linked, a share Clark believes no other developed country matches, so any inflation rise immediately lifts government borrowing costs.
data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumindex linked bondsUK
So what: UK sovereign debt is unusually inflation-sensitive, undermining the 'inflate away the debt' escape route and making inflation shocks fiscally acute.
“a quarter of our government debt is in that form” ▶ 1:02:44
Clark argues the UK cannot inflate away its debt as a way out, because before inflation erodes the real value of the debt, the index-linked gilts would drive a sharp jump in debt servicing costs.
causal🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumindex linked bondsUK
So what: The usual 'inflation helps debtors' playbook is weaker for the UK than for peers.
Fiscal crisis scenarios and precedents 7
Clark says a UK fiscal crisis would likely be very quick, citing Greece in 2010 where a one-notch ratings downgrade tipped borrowing costs to 20% or more.
prediction🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumGreeceUKratings agencies
So what: Bond-market confidence can flip abruptly rather than deteriorate gradually; tail risk is nonlinear.
“If a fiscal crisis erupts it'd be very very quick.” ▶ 0:20
If UK debt costs doubled, interest would be about 218 billion pounds a year, wiping out the education budget or the defence budget twice over; the exit would be an emergency loan or money printing, and printing leads to hyperinflation.
prediction🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumUKIMF
So what: Illustrates the doom-loop arithmetic: debt service doubling forces either IMF-style conditionality or monetisation.
Clark expects that in a crisis a Chancellor John Healey would be replaced in effect by IMF officials setting the budget, echoing 1976 when Denis Healey went to the IMF for a loan of about 3.8 billion dollars conditional on cutting spending by 2.5 billion pounds a year.
prediction🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumIMFJohn HealeyDenis HealeyUK
So what: Historical template: cuts are made by choice now or imposed later more severely.
“you can either do your cuts through choice now where you have control over what you cut” ▶ 51:01
Greece needed 8 years and 15 austerity packages (public pay and pension cuts, state asset sales) to recover, and now has lower bond yields than Britain.
data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: highGreeceUK
So what: The recovery path shows both the pain required and that markets reward credible fiscal repair.
“It took them 8 years and 15 austerity packages to get back on the straight and narrow.” ▶ 1:00:48
A likely emergency measure in a UK crisis would be an across-the-board 10% public sector pay cut, as Greece was forced to do, because it is quick to enact.
prediction🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: lowGreeceUK
So what: Signals the form of any forced adjustment: pay and pension cuts rather than investment cuts.
Clark says bond markets care less about high-tax versus low-tax than about whether they get their money back, so Truss's unfunded tax cuts plus an open-ended energy price guarantee spooked them, not low taxes themselves.
causal🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: highLiz Trussgilts
So what: Market reaction is driven by funding credibility; unfunded fiscal expansion of any flavour is penalised.
“What they bother about is whether they're going to get their money back” ▶ 57:25
Clark argues Truss's biggest mistake was pairing tax cuts with no spending cuts, and that her energy bill subsidy was the most irresponsible fiscal policy of modern times, though her diagnosis of low growth was right.
thesis🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumLiz Truss
So what: A credible growth agenda needs deregulation plus spending restraint, not tax cuts alone.
Political economy and voter behaviour 6
Clark argues UK deficits persist because it is politically easier to spend than to raise money, and that real fiscal repair will probably only come after a deep crisis.
causal🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: highUK
So what: Don't expect pre-emptive consolidation; the catalyst is a market event.
Ending austerity in the 2018 budget by Philip Hammond came after Theresa May lost her majority in 2017, with a teacher telling Gavin Barwell she'd accept two years of wage restraint but not ten; the government was still borrowing about 40 billion pounds a year at that point.
anecdote🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumPhilip HammondTheresa MayGavin Barwell
So what: Shows how consolidation fatigue reversed the post-2010 repair.
The British Social Attitudes survey shows the share wanting higher taxes and spending at its lowest since 1983 and the share wanting lower taxes at its highest, suggesting a narrowing majority for a high-tax state.
data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumBritish Social Attitudes survey
So what: Public opinion may be turning toward tolerating cuts, a precondition for fiscal repair.
“This year a higher proportion um want lower taxes than in any year since 1983” ▶ 16:59

data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumbillionairesUK
So what: Wealth-tax narratives do not close the deficit; broad-based tax or cuts are required.
“It would keep us going six months, but that's it.” ▶ 7:57

contrarian🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumSwedenUK
So what: Undermines left-wing Nordic-model arguments and supports the view the UK taxes capital and business punitively.
McCormack says he has not voted in three elections because he sees only decline on offer, and says the main parties lack a plan.
anecdote🗣 Peter McCormackconviction: lowReform UK

Budget cuts and welfare reform 7
No major UK party has a plan to close the 133 billion pound deficit: the Conservatives propose about 23 billion of reduction, and Reform UK proposes 80 billion of welfare cuts but would give much of it back in tax cuts.
data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: highReform UKConservative Party
So what: No credible fiscal anchor exists in the next election menu, so gilt risk premium may persist.
The biggest spending item is welfare, nearly half of it the state pension, and the triple lock already costs about 15 billion pounds a year more than earnings-linked uprating would have over 12 years, with an upward ratchet.
data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: hightriple lockUK state pension
So what: Any serious consolidation must touch pensions, politically the hardest item.

Clark's book proposes returning the basic state pension to its 2011 level, a 10% cut, arguing hyperinflation would hurt pensioners far more.
thesis🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumstate pensionUK
So what: A concrete consolidation lever that is politically toxic but sizeable.

Around 100,000 people claim personal independence payments for ADHD, often self-diagnosed, and PIP can unlock Motability cars; Clark says PIP has got out of hand and attempts to cut 5 billion pounds from it in July 2025 triggered a Labour backbench revolt that started Starmer's downfall.
anecdote🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumPIPMotabilityKeir StarmerRachel Reeves

The car industry has become dependent on the Motability scheme, which sells new cars at prices most buyers would not pay, and would likely lobby against reform.
causal🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: lowMotability
So what: Welfare programmes create entrenched corporate beneficiaries that resist cuts.
Asylum system costs are about 5 billion pounds a year against a 133 billion pound deficit, so focusing on migrant hotels cannot close the gap.
data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumUK

data_point🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumNHSUK

prediction🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumcapital gains taxJohn HealeyUK property

Clark says that when tax burdens pass a threshold people avoid taxes, work less or emigrate, so tax rises may not raise revenue proportionally; he calculated he paid the government twice what he spent on himself.
causal🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumUK

“I just handed twice as much to the government as I've just spent on myself in the past 12 months.” ▶ 43:10

framework🗣 Ross Clarkconviction: mediumKeynesGordon Brown

Tokenization and 24/7 on-chain trading 7
Matt Hougan (Bitwise) argues tokenized stocks win first at the edges of the traditional market (after hours, weekends, holidays, and when leverage is wanted) rather than competing with brokerages at 11:00 New York time, following the usual pattern of technological disruption.
thesis🗣 Matt Houganconviction: highHyperliquidOndoRobinhoodBitwise
So what: Look for tokenized-equity volume to grow in off-hours niches before it threatens mainstream venues.
“It never goes after the mainstream application. It works on the edges.” ▶ 8:29

anecdote🗣 Matt Houganconviction: mediumHyperliquid
So what: Crisis-hour liquidity is a customer-acquisition channel for on-chain venues.

anecdote🗣 Hostconviction: mediumNvidiaHyperliquid
So what: Earnings-window trading is a concrete wedge for tokenized equities.
Hougan says the market is over-focused on perfecting the tokenized instrument's structure (SPV versus native tokenization); users just want the most liquid, accessible version, and over time it will move to natively tokenized assets or collateralized debt vehicles like European ETPs.
thesis🗣 Matt Houganconviction: mediumOndoRobinhood
So what: Counterparty risk on tokenized stocks is real but secondary in this early phase; pick large branded issuers with much at stake.
“you want to align with big brands that have a lot at stake” ▶ 10:36

prediction🗣 Matt Houganconviction: highUniswap
So what: Settlement speed is the structural argument for the migration of asset trading on chain.

framework🗣 Matt Houganconviction: highBlackRockApolloHarvard

causal🗣 Matt Houganconviction: highSolana

anecdote🗣 Chainsmokers co-founder (Drew/Alex)conviction: lowElton JohnSpotify
