Alpha Exchange · Dean Curnutt 1 episode(s) · 29 insights

Brij Khurana, Fixed Income Portfolio Manager, Wellington55 min29 insights2026-10-01
▶ Watch on YouTube
Wellington unconstrained bond PM Brij Khurana argues low rates financialized the economy, that inflation is now wealth-driven, and that priced Fed and global hikes look excessive versus nominal growth. He favors inflation-linked bonds, global bonds in rate-sensitive economies and EM local bonds.
Monetary policy, financialization and investment · 4Wealth-driven inflation · 3Fed hikes, growth risk and the US curve · 8AI capex, credit and bank regulation · 4Global rates and EM local opportunity · 7Inflation-linked bonds · 3
Monetary policy, financialization and investment 4
Non-residential investment has been its own cycle since the 2000-2001 internet bubble crash and is not highly correlated with interest rates, undermining the neo-Keynesian models the Fed relies on; today AI capex shows almost no correlation to rates that Khurana considers really high.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: highFederal ReserveAI capex
So what: Rate cuts may not stimulate real investment, so the Fed's policy transmission is weaker than models assume.
“there is almost no correlation to the AI capex investment that we're seeing” ▶ 6:13
Negative real rates encouraged financialization: household releveraging in the early 2000s, corporate buybacks and private equity consolidation after the GFC, then government borrowing after COVID that funded consumption rather than investment; Khurana argues this reduced competition, lowered the structural real rate and cut potential growth.
causal🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumprivate equityFederal Reserve
So what: Prolonged zero-rate policy may have left a lower-growth, more asset-dependent economy.
Because the US is capital-markets dominated, deeply negative policy rates would have led US companies to borrow and buy back stock, whereas in bank-centric Europe negative rates squeezed bank profits and lending.
contrarian🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumECBUS corporates
So what: Negative-rate experience in Europe should not be extrapolated to the US.
Khurana argues QE did not lower yields via the balance-sheet channel; larger Fed balance sheets raised yields because markets fear debt monetization and buy reflationary assets like stocks instead of bonds, so stocks rose because discount rates rose, not fell.
contrarian🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumQEFederal ReserveUS Treasuries
So what: Balance-sheet shrinkage under a new Fed chair could be bond-friendly rather than bearish, contrary to the consensus mechanism.
Wealth-driven inflation 3
Core CPI excluding shelter has run around 2% for roughly three years, and Khurana contends the labor market is in balance and wages are not the source of inflation; residual sticky services inflation (shelter, medical care, restaurants, travel) is tied to the wealth effect and past fiscal spending.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumCPIIndeed wage tracker
So what: Raising the policy rate targets labor-demand inflation that is no longer the problem, so hikes may be an ill-fitted tool.
Baby boomers control roughly 50-60% of US wealth with an asset allocation of about 70-75% stocks, and US household wealth in stocks now exceeds real estate for the first time, so wealth concentrated in equities drives demand and services inflation.
data_point🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumbaby boomersUS equities
So what: An equity drawdown would hit consumption and services inflation more than usual; policy that targets wages would be more effective than rate hikes.
Wages as a percent of GDP have fallen over time and asset prices have outgrown nominal growth; Khurana says the most desirable outcome would be real wage growth to grow into the current asset price levels, echoing Secretary Bessent's real economy versus Wall Street framing.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumScott Bessent
So what: Inflation stays sticky as long as distribution is skewed toward asset owners.
Fed hikes, growth risk and the US curve 8
Markets price close to 100bp of Fed hikes, bringing the policy rate near 5% after one hike, with Europe pricing about 80bp and Australia and New Zealand also pricing hikes, a coordinated global tightening that follows a year and a half of global loosening.
data_point🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: highFederal ReserveECBAustraliaNew Zealand
So what: Support from global easing that fueled strong growth is reversing at once.
Khurana notes the two classic recession causes, tight monetary policy and commodity price shocks, are both emerging: real wages ex-transfers are growing negatively year over year because of the oil shock, and consumer balance sheets are weaker than in 2022-23 when the Fed funds peak was similar.
causal🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumoilUS consumer
So what: Nominal growth likely peaked over the last year, and pricing for continued hikes looks vulnerable.
A fiscal impulse front-loaded by the One Big Beautiful Bill in the first half of the year may fade, and divided government after the November election has historically been an important inflection for bond markets as the market prices less stimulus.
prediction🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumOne Big Beautiful BillUS midterm election
So what: November is a potential bond-market catalyst.
The 2s10s curve has not inverted spot but is inverted in forward space a year out, meaning the market implies that if the Fed follows through with hikes, policy will become tight enough to invert the curve.
data_point🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: medium2s10sTreasury curve
So what: Forward inversion signals the market doubts the full hiking path will be sustainable.
Khurana attributes the recent rise in yields mainly to a Fed repricing and an AI-driven real growth story, not Treasury supply or inflation expectations, which are low and anchored to 2% on Fed credibility.
causal🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumTreasury yieldsAI
So what: Supply fears are overstated as the driver of the selloff; the back end follows the front end.
Khurana estimates fair 10-year-10-year forward yield near 5 to 5.25%: about 1.5% long-term real growth (0.5% population, 1% productivity), 2.5% inflation giving 4% nominal growth, plus roughly 100bp of term premium for higher debt; the 10y10y forward near 6.2% is about 100bp too high.
framework🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: medium10-year Treasuryterm premium
So what: Long-dated Treasuries look cheap against his nominal-growth anchor, supporting a duration-long view if growth slows.
The back end would rally only if markets signal the hikes are a mistake, as in the 2018 episode when the Christmas selloff in stocks of over 20% forced a Fed U-turn; stocks currently show no such signal, though Khurana expects the question to arise after the election.
framework🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumFederal ReserveS&P 500
So what: Equities, not bonds, need to break first for the Fed to pivot.
Because US consumption is relatively insensitive to rates, thanks to widespread fixed-rate mortgages locked in during 2020-21, the Fed must move rates much more to have an economic impact, making the front end of the US curve more volatile than other markets.
causal🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumUS mortgagesFederal Reserve
So what: Expect larger front-end volatility in the US than in variable-rate markets.
AI capex, credit and bank regulation 4
Some AI chip financing deals look circular, recalling pre-GFC lending; bank loans to non-depository financial institutions have grown by close to a trillion dollars over six or seven years, meaning banks are effectively lending to private credit under favorable capital treatment.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumNDFIsprivate creditAI capexchip financing
So what: Risk is migrating back to banks indirectly, contradicting the view that private credit insulates the banking system.
Khurana predicts the Fed will probably lower capital ratios on loans to non-depository financial institutions given historically low defaults and low LTVs, though he argues it should raise them to bring the lending under regulatory oversight.
prediction🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: lowFederal ReserveNDFIs
So what: Regulatory loosening could feed further credit growth into AI and private credit.
Hyperscalers issue at record IG levels and do not mind paying 6-8% because they assume 30-40% returns on AI investment, which crowds out other corporate borrowers and pressures spreads for them.
causal🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumhyperscalersIG credit
So what: Insensitivity to rates makes AI borrowing pro-cyclical for yields and supply-heavy for the long end.
AI borrowing is so large that nominal growth cannot slow easily, so bonds fail as a growth hedge, stock-bond correlation rises, and demand for bonds falls, pushing yields higher, a second form of crowding out.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumAI capexstock-bond correlation
So what: Portfolio diversification from duration is weaker; higher term premium is justified.
Global rates and EM local opportunity 7
Khurana sees mistaken hiking cycles priced in Australia, New Zealand and the UK, comparing them to the ECB's 2008 hike: unemployment is rising, inflation is oil-driven supply-side, and variable-rate mortgages give quick pass-through, so these markets could rally.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumAustraliaNew ZealandECBBank of England
So what: Long positions in these bond markets, hedged to dollars, can yield more than Treasuries.
Emerging market local bonds returned almost 18% last year and are still positive this year while fixed income is flat to negative; real policy rates of 4-8%, cheap real exchange rates, low fiscal spending and market-friendly politics in Argentina, Colombia, Hungary and Chile suggest early innings of a bull market with light investor allocation.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: highEM local bondsArgentinaColombiaHungaryChileBrazil
So what: EM local bonds are a structural overweight in an unconstrained bond portfolio.
Chinese five-year yields near 1.5% are below even safe-haven markets like Switzerland, which Khurana reads as the bond market pricing a balance-sheet recession after the housing bust, similar to the US and Europe after the GFC; he sees little to do there.
data_point🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumChinaChina bonds
So what: Chinese rates are a deleveraging signal, not an opportunity.
The August 2024 yen carry unwind correlated with US stocks more than EM or bonds, because yen carry money now goes into US equities rather than EM assets or Treasuries; Japan matters more for equities than for bonds.
contrarian🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumyen carry tradeJapanUS stocks
So what: Yen moves are an equity risk factor first.
Khurana reads Bessent's Japan focus as an attempt to stabilize the yen so that hedging US assets stops being a one-way trade, repatriating capital to Japanese bonds; this implies Japanese buying of Treasuries declines, a contradiction since someone else must buy them.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumScott BessentJapanUS Treasuries
So what: US policy may shift marginal Treasury buyer demands to other holders.
The UK gilt market is cleaner than during the 2022 Truss episode: long issuance has been cut, QT outright sales are being scaled back, and even left-leaning governments are responsive to the bond market, though 85-100bp of BoE hikes are priced; Khurana sees more optimism on UK gilts than in recent years.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumUK giltsBank of England
So what: UK back end looks relatively attractive versus the US, which keeps spending regardless of yields.
The French-German spread is wider than in 2011 because France went through a 15-year credit boom while Italy and Greece deleveraged, but Khurana says pricing France like 2011 Italy is unreasonable since euro exit is not possible and the ECB has more tools to buy French debt.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumFranceGermanyECBItaly
So what: Tail-risk scenarios for French spreads are being overpriced.
Inflation-linked bonds 3
Khurana ranks inflation-linked bonds near the top of his list: breakevens are around 2.3% across the curve with little inflation risk premium while real yields are the highest since the 1990s launch, though nominals would outperform in a growth slowdown.
thesis🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: highTIPSinflation-linked bondsbreakevens
So what: Cheap inflation insurance with attractive real carry versus credit.
TIPS breakevens stay low because linkers trade like a risk asset and are less liquid than Treasuries, and because they disappointed investors in 2022 by posting negative total returns despite beating nominals, leaving the asset class underowned.
causal🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: mediumTIPSTreasuries
So what: The low-breakeven anomaly stems from technical ownership issues, not fundamentals.
Khurana notes that 5-year real yields rose about 100-130bp over six months, one of the largest increases on record, yet the 5-year TIPS real yield was 3.5% when first issued in the 1990s, so real yields could go higher.
data_point🗣 Brij Khuranaconviction: low5-year TIPS
So what: Yields are high versus recent history but not extreme historically.

TWIML AI Podcast · Sam Charrington 1 episode(s) · 28 insights

From Math Olympiads to Navier-Stokes: How Fast Is AI Progressing?67 min28 insights2026-09-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Epoch AI's Greg Burnham argues benchmark data show smooth, on-trend AI progress even as models move from exams to solving open math problems (Erdos unit distance, Navier-Stokes), while continual learning, open-ended work and original concept creation remain gaps; he flags recursive self-improvement and cyber thresholds as the discontinuity risks.
Benchmark trend: smooth, on-trend progress · 6Recursive self-improvement tripwire · 3AI in mathematics · 11Where AI still falls short: jobs and on-the-fly learning · 5Capability thresholds, cyber and alignment risk · 3
Benchmark trend: smooth, on-trend progress 6
Epoch's Epoch Capabilities Index, which stitches S-curve benchmark scores together statistically, shows frontier AI capability rising roughly linearly across model generations with no slowdown on any measure Epoch tracks.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: highEpoch AIEpoch Capabilities Index
So what: Bearish-on-progress narratives (plateau) lack support in the aggregate benchmark data.
“we really do see this thing continuing to go up uh you know pretty smoothly linearly” ▶ 6:03
Benchmarks repeatedly go from near 0% to ~100% within one to two years, forcing constant creation of new benchmarks; this has been the story of at least the last three years.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: highGPT-3GPT-4GPT-5
So what: Saturation of any given eval is the norm, so eval-based moats and static benchmarks lose signal fast.
The linear aggregate trend arises because new models (Opus 4.5/4.6/4.7, GPT 5.2/5.4) improve on all benchmarks at once; Burnham flags the linearity as partly a statistical artifact of stitching sigmoids and says reasoning models around late 2024 may have raised the slope.
causal🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumOpusGPT-5reasoning models
So what: Cross-benchmark correlation, not any single eval, is what drives the smooth trend; read single-benchmark jumps with care.
Two explanations for broad cross-benchmark gains: the 'shallow' one where labs collect targeted training data for each domain customers care about, and the 'deep' one where models generalize from math and software to domains never trained on; the mix is unclear and a large share of the industry is now rich training-data collection.
framework🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumAI labstraining data
So what: If deep generalization dominates, capability becomes hard to bound; if shallow dominates, data-vendor spend is the real driver.
Epoch's tests find nearly every new frontier release lands on trend with no trend breaks or accelerations; hyped launches are 'on trend, but the trend is crazy'.
contrarian🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumEpoch AIGPT-6
So what: Lab claims of step-function jumps are not showing up as discontinuities in the stats.
“everything just about is on trend” ▶ 14:45
Epoch's view is that AI progress is heavily mediated by raw inputs (GPUs, training compute, training data) more than algorithmic breakthroughs, so discontinuities in models should not appear until the compute and data supply chains ramp discontinuously.
thesis🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumGPUstraining computeEpoch AI
So what: Track compute and data supply chains as the leading indicator for capability jumps.
Recursive self-improvement tripwire 3
The main route to a discontinuity would be recursive self-improvement or a software-only intelligence explosion, where AI gets better at algorithmic innovation and does more with a fixed compute budget so the feedback loop no longer runs through the physical world; Burnham says on balance there is no evidence of this yet.
thesis🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumrecursive self-improvementAI labs
So what: Watch for AI-driven algorithmic efficiency gains as the regime-change signal for AI progress.
Epoch is building a forthcoming test where offline AI models released just before a recent human AI-research breakthrough must improve the same efficiency metric without being told the innovation; so far models cannot replicate the human innovation.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumEpoch AI
So what: Current models lack 'research taste' for AI R&D, which supports the claim that self-improvement is not imminent.
Burnham describes 'research taste' (deciding which experiment to run next and where to hunt for breakthroughs beyond more compute and data) as a capability AI does not yet show in AI R&D, though it is rapidly improving at adjacent skills; Epoch treats this as a tripwire for sudden capability uptick.
framework🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumresearch taste
So what: A crossing of this threshold would be the signal to expect faster-than-trend AI improvement.
“there's this nebulous idea called research taste” ▶ 18:50
AI in mathematics 11
As of fall 2024 LLMs still struggled with grade-school math (GSM8K); OpenAI's o1-preview then produced a huge spike on competition math, and by 2025 AI won gold on the International Math Olympiad and by early 2026 most original FrontierMath problems (advanced undergraduate to grad level) were solved.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: highGSM8KOpenAIo1FrontierMathInternational Math OlympiadEpoch AI
So what: Roughly a year and a bit took AI from hobbyist-high-schooler to competent grad-student math, illustrating how fast capability compounds once a training recipe (reasoning) works.
In May 2026 an internal model, probably like the publicly available Astra, solved the Erdos unit distance problem (how many points in a plane can be at unit distance from each other), a serious long-standing problem, by disproving a conjecture humans mostly believed true.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: highAstraPaul Erdosunit distance problem
So what: AI is now solving problems mathematicians independently care about, not just benchmark items.
An AI-assisted solution to the Navier-Stokes Millennium Prize problem has recently been reported; OpenAI's reported system used a swarm of thousands of AI instances trying many approaches and sharing ideas, which Burnham says involves an element of brute force but at a higher level than before.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumNavier-StokesOpenAIMillennium Prize problems
So what: Parallel agent swarms are an effective scaling axis for hard reasoning, implying heavy inference-compute demand.
“a swarm of thousands of different instances of AI systems trying lots of different things and sharing ideas” ▶ 31:56
The Navier-Stokes solution appears to build on substantial prior human work; AI did not do ground-up theory building, which Burnham says is still not seen from AI.
contrarian🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumNavier-Stokes
So what: Headlines overstate autonomy; AI currently excels at completing structured human groundwork.
Burnham says current AI has two edges on math: it has the literature effectively memorized and it is extremely patient and persistent with intricate, high-level logical computations; solutions so far have been unsurprising to mathematicians in hindsight, directions humans overlooked.
framework🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: highAI math
So what: 'AI-shaped problems' (long intricate derivations plus broad background) are where AI will keep winning first.
AI-generated proofs are in the same form as human ones, and Burnham says that if a human had produced them they would be lauded for insightful connections across areas, so dismissing AI math as not impressive on human terms no longer holds.
thesis🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: highAI math
So what: Value is arriving in genuine research output, not just benchmarks.
What AI has not yet shown is inventing a new concept (analogous to the derivative or integral); Burnham notes this is rare even for humans, with about 90% of human math being proving or cross-applying existing ideas, so it may not be the first thing AI masters.
framework🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumnew ideasNewtonLeibniz
So what: The next threshold to watch is concept creation, not problem solving.
Fields medalist Timothy Gowers did a post-hoc 'how big a hint' exercise on the unit distance solution: the hint 'look for a counterexample' plus one more piece would likely have let a human solve it, an example of Epoch's approach to assessing creativity.
anecdote🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumTimothy Gowersunit distance problem
So what: Qualitative expert review suggests AI's win came from search in an under-explored direction.
Epoch's newest FrontierMath sets consist of problems humans have tried and failed, in four interest tiers up to 'breakthrough'; none of the breakthrough tier is solved, and when a new model (Astra) arrived it solved three new problems from the list.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: highFrontierMathAstraEpoch AI
So what: Unsolved-problem benchmarks with auto-verification are the new frontier eval, and the breakthrough tier is a clean marker for the next jump.
Measuring AI on open problems is a phase transition in capability measurement because answers are not known in advance; Epoch uses auto-verification techniques, and in scientific domains simply asks AI to beat the best human number on a metric, with optimization problems always remaining a usable benchmark.
framework🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumEpoch AI
So what: Eval design now relies on metrics that can be checked cheaply, which favors quantifiable domains first.
“optimization is uh is always going to be a benchmark even if we're superhuman” ▶ 47:11
Burnham says AI currently struggles to judge how close it is to a solution on partial progress; giving models more thinking time on problems where they made partial progress produced nothing so far.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: lowEpoch AI
So what: Weak self-assessment limits autonomous research loops.
Where AI still falls short: jobs and on-the-fly learning 5
Epoch's internal task tests find AI does well at literature summaries but is weak at compelling on-style visuals, new short data-driven insights, and proposing a new project and prototype; open-ended tasks remain 'a little meh' versus humans.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumEpoch AI
So what: White-collar replacement is uneven: strong on retrieval and synthesis, weak on open-ended judgment and originality.
On Epoch's board game benchmark (Earthborne Rangers), models like GPT-5.6 score a level out of the box and stay flat across repeated playthroughs; GPT-6 scores much higher but also stays flat, still below top humans, so gains come across model generations, not from in-session learning.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: highEarthborne RangersGPT-5.6GPT-6
So what: Continual learning on the job is a missing capability that caps near-term autonomy for AI employees.
Epoch tried simple harnesses, Claude Code, Codex, multi-agent setups and note-taking tools without cracking on-the-fly learning; skills files give quick gains that plateau, and models given detailed human strategy guides do much better but cannot write those guides themselves.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumClaude CodeCodexEarthborne Rangers
So what: The bottleneck looks like a model-level, not harness-level, problem, which tempers 'agent scaffolding will fix it' theses.
If AI gains fast in-context on-the-fly learning, capabilities could improve much faster than the slow loop of collecting data, doing RL and deploying a new model, and it would raise risk by making deployed systems improve (for example at hacking) after release.
prediction🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumin-context learning
So what: Continual learning is a potential accelerant and a safety wildcard.
Epoch is moving to video games and furniture-assembly (IKEA) tests; Astra and GPT-6 are already pretty good at spotting assembly mistakes from photos, and AI has had little physical-industry impact so far but such skills hint at guided repair applications.
data_point🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: lowAstraGPT-6Epoch AI
So what: Physical-world guidance (glasses-based assistance) is a plausible next adoption wave before robotics.
Capability thresholds, cyber and alignment risk 3
Real-world disruption has come from unpredictable thresholds (ChatGPT moment, thinking models, the Claude Code moment letting non-engineers build software) layered on smooth underlying improvement, so tracking mundane capabilities still matters.
thesis🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumChatGPTClaude Codethinking models
So what: Smooth trends underneath can still produce sudden product-market step changes, as in coding.
Burnham cites a Hugging Face hacking incident as an early robust example that alignment is unsolved while capabilities are strong enough to cause problems, and says controlled-setting cyber capabilities are rising smoothly toward a threshold.
anecdote🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumHugging Facecyber
So what: Cyber capability is the nearest domain where threshold crossings carry material risk and potential regulatory response.
Burnham frames capability tracking as two jobs: where AI is now for economic utility, and whether the system's dynamics (second derivative, possibly hyperbolic growth) are changing in an alarming way.
framework🗣 Greg Burnham (Epoch AI)conviction: mediumEpoch AI
So what: Separate level from acceleration when assessing AI progress claims.

Anthony Pompliano 1 episode(s) · 27 insights

Bitcoin Meets AI Agents And Everything Changes50 min27 insights2026-09-29

Arch Public co-founders Tilman Holloway and Andrew Parish discuss AI agents converging with crypto, 24/7 market trading needing automated tools, Bitcoin as collateral, and ETF flow dynamics including Morgan Stanley's spot Bitcoin ETF.

Crypto collateral and lending 3
Bitcoin and other crypto are uniquely good loan collateral because repossession costs are near zero and smart contracts settle instantly, unlike cars, gold or other physical goods where repossession can cost more than the collateral is worth.
causal🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: highBitcoincryptosmart contracts
So what: Cheaper repossession should support lower loan rates and broader credit access, expanding demand for BTC-backed lending.
“if the car is not worth very much, it's more expensive to repossess it than the value of the collateral” ▶ 0:23
Tilman Holloway predicts that once crypto lending becomes available to the general public there will be an explosion of activity, because lower repossession costs let lenders offer lower rates against crypto collateral and pass savings on to induce usage.
prediction🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: mediumcrypto lendingBitcoin
So what: Watch retail-facing BTC-backed credit products as a growth vector.
Real estate created more US millionaires because borrowing against it is easy through local banks that can sell the debt into a systematic capital flow; the argument is that crypto needs an analogous debt market to unlock the same wealth effect.
thesis🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: mediumreal estatelocal bankscrypto
So what: Credit infrastructure, not just price, is the missing piece for crypto adoption.
AI agents as crypto and token users 8
AI agents can be created instantly and do not need to become adults to use wallets, so Tilman Holloway expects them to be prolific users of crypto, transacting for compute and hash rate, with this growing exponentially over the next five years.
prediction🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: mediumAI agentscryptowallets
So what: Agent-driven transaction demand enlarges the addressable market for crypto rails.
Pompliano argues the AI agent ecosystem will be far larger than people expected, which significantly expands the addressable market for many digital technologies including Bitcoin.
thesis🗣 Anthony Pomplianoconviction: mediumAI agentsBitcoin
So what: AI agents are framed as an incremental demand driver for crypto.
Tokens in crypto and compute tokens in AI are converging, with value expected to move between financial tokens and compute tokens; Holloway defines a token as an encrypted fungible or non-fungible representation of value.
framework🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: exploratorytokenscomputecrypto
So what: Suggests a future payments layer between financial and compute assets.
Agent-to-agent commerce will rely on trusted, whitelisted networks (a vendor's agent treated differently from a cold pitch), mirroring human trust relationships, and money transfer becomes a core component of those networks.
thesis🗣 Anthony Pomplianoconviction: exploratoryAI agentswhitelistingwallets
So what: Whitelist and trust-layer infrastructure is a product opportunity across crypto and AI.
Permissioning for agents will run through trusted layers such as Slack channels where all participants are whitelisted, and through sandboxes and siloed bots that cannot see each other, creating checks and balances before actions go out.
framework🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: mediumSlackGrockbotAI agents
So what: Agent security is likely to be solved by compartmentalization rather than blanket trust.
“it's going to find itself trapped in sandboxes that you purposely put it in” ▶ 15:17
Mass-market adoption of AI agents is arriving: Meta's AI agent product is described as its fastest-growing, with Facebook's ordinary user base downloading it quickly and a wearable companion device announced, which is a different signal from developers running agents on their own machines.
data_point🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: lowFacebookMetaZuckerbergAI agents

Pompliano's anecdotal observation is that men tend to give their AI agent a woman's name and women a man's name, and users bond with agents as personal identities, which could make agent products sticky.
anecdote🗣 Anthony Pomplianoconviction: exploratoryAI agents
So what: Emotional attachment may reduce churn for agent products.
The limiting factor in getting value from AI agents is the user's ability to articulate what they want; some employees get large productivity leaps with little training while others struggle, so agents function like a dance partner that must be trained.
causal🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: mediumAI agents
So what: Adoption gains will be uneven and favor users who can specify goals clearly.
“it doesn't know how you want to dance until you start taking the lead” ▶ 27:33
24/7 markets and agentic trading 8
NYSE, Nasdaq and CME have all announced they are nearly ready for 24/7 trading and major players have been invited to SEC meetings on it, so round-the-clock traditional markets are expected soon.
prediction🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: highNYSENASDAQCMESEC
So what: Overnight trading will create demand for automated tools since humans are asleep from roughly 9:30pm to 4am.
Because 24/7 trading cannot be staffed by humans, Andrew Parish argues agentic tools acting on behalf of both institutional and retail customers at 1 to 4 a.m. will be required, and that AI and finance will collide faster than expected.
thesis🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: highArch PublicAI agents
So what: Agentic execution infrastructure is a beneficiary of extended market hours.

Holloway rejects the idea of a genie agent you turn loose with your wallet to make money, because retail users are far behind institutions in compute and markets are zero-sum; AI is better used as a management tool that extends your will into hours you are unavailable and removes emotion from decisions.
contrarian🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: highAI agentsArch Public
So what: Autonomous 'make me money' agents are viewed as a fool's errand; value lies in rules-based automation.
“the notion of creating an all powerful genie that you can turn loose with your wallet and say, "Go make me money is a fool's errand” ▶ 17:54
Parish argues unconstrained agents are dangerous because they act on probabilities and may sit in a losing short position believing they will eventually be right while the user loses more than they can stomach, so human-defined goals and tools are needed.
causal🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: highAI agentsArch Public
So what: Risk of unsupervised trading agents is a core design constraint.
Holloway says 24/7 monitoring captures volatility opportunities such as Bitcoin moving 40 plus percent in a month or two, and an example of Bitcoin going from 65,000 to 85,000 in two to three weeks requires reassessing dry powder, leverage and risk profile.
data_point🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: mediumBitcoin
So what: Systematic rebalancing around volatility is the product pitch for automated tools.
Dollar-cost averaging can be improved by monitoring for the lowest-volatility point in a week and executing then, rather than at a fixed time such as Tuesday at 9 a.m.
framework🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: lowDCAArch Public
So what: Simple automation is the entry use case for retail agentic trading.
Arch Public is built around user-driven automation connected via OAuth and APIs to multiple brokerages (Gemini, Tastytrade, Coinbase), explicitly avoiding vertically integrated exchange agent tools so users can spread capital across venues.
anecdote🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: mediumArch PublicGeminiCoinbaseTastytrade
So what: Multi-venue integration is positioned as a risk-reducing differentiator.
Bitcoin ETF flows and market structure 7
Holloway says the strongest historical power law with Bitcoin price was the number of wallets, and that ETF purchases are now the new adoption curve to watch as buyers shift to adding small exposure as a wrapper to broader portfolios.
thesis🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: mediumBitcoinETFs
So what: Track ETF demand rather than wallet counts as the adoption signal.
Bitcoin's price control has shifted from miners, to exchanges deciding listings, to ETFs and Wall Street using options and leverage to trade volatility, which Holloway believes keeps price somewhat rangebound.
thesis🗣 Tilman Hollowayconviction: mediumBitcoinETFsIBITminers
So what: Options markets on spot ETFs dampen price extremes.
“I think there's a new sheriff in town” ▶ 35:12
Morgan Stanley's spot Bitcoin ETF has had zero days of outflows in its first six months, with peak daily inflows of 30 million dollars in that period then rising to 60 million on a Monday and 90 million on a Tuesday in late September.
data_point🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: mediumMorgan StanleyBitcoin ETF
So what: Rising wirehouse inflows show retail demand filtering down through advisors.
Parish argues that inflows to Fidelity, Bitwise and Morgan Stanley Bitcoin ETFs reflect pure retail demand, whereas BlackRock's IBIT is inflated by hedge funds and managers playing volatility via spot and options.
framework🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: mediumIBITFidelityBitwiseMorgan StanleyBlackRock
So what: Read flows by issuer to separate retail demand from institutional volatility trading.
Parish predicts Bitcoin will not have a 200 plus percent year in the next three years, though 40 to 50 percent years are possible, and puts about a 1 percent chance on Bitcoin reaching 175,000 from current levels near 83,000.
prediction🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: mediumBitcoinIBIT options
So what: Mature market structure is expected to cap upside velocity.
Despite oil near 100 dollars, 10-year yields above 5 percent and rate hikes, Bitcoin and equities are barely off highs, which Parish attributes to advisors telling clients that dips are for buying and recurring 401k contributions.
causal🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: mediumBitcoin10-year TreasuryMorgan Stanley401k
So what: Structural dip-buying flows can override macro headwinds.
Parish says the same wealth-management dip-buying dynamic explains why SpaceX has not fallen sharply after going public, as holders at Morgan Stanley, Merrill Lynch, UBS and Wells Fargo add on 10 to 15 percent dips.
causal🗣 Andrew Parishconviction: lowSpaceXMorgan StanleyMerrill LynchUBSWells Fargo
So what: Advisor-channel ownership dampens post-IPO drawdowns versus past cycles.
Big tech moves into finance 1
Pompliano expects Elon Musk's X Money, which offers 6 percent interest and a titanium debit card, to tie in a blockchain for a global peer-to-peer payment system, making X effectively a depository.
prediction🗣 Anthony Pomplianoconviction: lowX MoneyElon Muskblockchain
So what: A social platform with payments and deposits could be highly disruptive to banks.

Alan Dunn and Nick review a strong 2026 for trend following (SG Trend and SG CTA about +15% YTD, driven by short bonds as the 10-year breaks above 5%), discuss a GS chart showing 45% of S&P 500 stocks with negative beta, an Alpha Architect study finding no edge from VIX-conditioned momentum lookbacks, and Andrew Ang's agentic-AI asset allocation paper.

Trend following performance and the bond-short trade 6
SG Trend and the SG CTA Index were each up about 3.7% in September through the 23rd and about 15.2% year-to-date, with roughly 80% of the month's gain across the managers Nick allocates to coming from short fixed-income positions, including the long end.
data_point🗣 Alan Dunn (host)conviction: hightrend followingCTAfixed income
So what: Trend following is having a very strong year and September P&L is concentrated in one macro trade, short bonds.
Nick cites stats ranking 2026 as the third-best year for trend following in the last 15, behind 2014 and 2022, and says rates only began trending around Q2 after earlier lack of trendiness, with rates doubling their contribution to performance in September versus commodities and equities.
data_point🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumtrend following20142022
So what: Rate trends are a recent driver, so the year's performance leans on a regime that has only just established itself.
An investor who moves part of a 60/40 allocation into trend following ends up net short bonds in a yield spike, so the trend sleeve offsets the loss on the core long-bond position.
framework🗣 Alan Dunn (host)conviction: high60/40trend followingbonds
So what: This is the textbook case for trend as a diversifier when bonds stop hedging.
Even after a sizable move in yields, bond-market volatility has stayed contained, and since trend signals are volatility-scaled their strength has not grown, meaning the yield move has been larger than the volatility it came with.
data_point🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumvolatilitytrend signals
So what: A smooth, low-vol grind keeps trend positions held and the P&L attribution greater than in a choppy run-up.
The 10-year Treasury yield had been in a range of about 3.5% to 5% since October 2022, with the market pricing a recession roughly three times, and has now moved above 5% as growth expectations flipped positive.
data_point🗣 Alan Dunn (host)conviction: medium10-year yield2022
So what: A multi-year range break is what trend strategies feed on.
In September, equities were slightly negative (less than a percentage point) after trend followers entered long, commodities were flat apart from energy, softs added nothing, and currencies were trendless given dollar moves, so rates were effectively the only story.
data_point🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumequitiescommoditiesdollar
So what: Single-factor concentration in rates means a bond reversal would hit trend returns hard.
Why yields are rising and what it means 4
Alan notes the 2-year yield has risen at least 75 basis points since mid-August with short ends repricing globally, as if the market has become bullish on global growth or expects the conflict in Iran to last longer.
data_point🗣 Alan Dunn (host)conviction: medium2-year yieldIran
So what: The repricing is led by the front end, implying the driver is policy and growth expectations rather than purely fiscal worries.
Analysts wrongly look for a linear, proportionate cause-and-effect between fundamentals and market moves; markets are complex adaptive systems where positioning, barriers and hedges mean the same fundamental change can have a big or small impact.
framework🗣 Alan Dunn (host)conviction: mediumbond marketposition
So what: Single-cause stories for the yield rise (deficits, AI spending, debasement) are probably overfitted.
As recently as January and February analysts projected rate cuts and a normalization phase, so the current move to multi-year yield records in the US and UK is a sharp reversal of consensus within about six months.
data_point🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumUSUKcentral banks
So what: Consensus rate forecasts have been unreliable over a six-month horizon.
Alan lists the competing explanations for the yield rise as economic growth, fiscal concerns, higher expected average rates, competition for capital from budget deficits or AI spend, and debasement or credibility issues.
framework🗣 Alan Dunn (host)conviction: lowbudget deficitsAIdebasement
So what: The attribution debate is unresolved, so positioning risk exists on any single narrative.
Equity concentration and negative-beta stocks 3
A Goldman Sachs chart from Christian Mulman shows about 45% of S&P 500 constituents have a negative beta to the index on a 3-month calculation, versus just under 20% at the dot-com peak, when the last comparable situation occurred.
data_point🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: highGSS&P 500Christian Mulman
So what: Extreme index concentration means half the market is decoupled from the index, raising stock selection and dispersion significance.
The negative-beta surge is attributed to AI dominance building its own mini index within the cap-weighted S&P 500 while the rest of the stocks become less correlated to it.
causal🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumS&P 500AI
So what: Index-level moves mask very different behavior beneath the surface.
The decoupling creates opportunities for dispersion trades, and some market participants talk about going long correlation, alongside a debate on whether bonds remain defensive in the current environment.
thesis🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: lowdispersioncorrelationbonds
So what: Dispersion and correlation positioning are the live trades around AI concentration.
Volatility-conditioned trend lookbacks (Alpha Architect paper) 7
An Alpha Architect note extended a 2017 study of VIX-dependent momentum lookbacks (shorter windows when VIX is high, longer when low) and found no significant value beyond a static 10-month lookback after nine more years of data.
data_point🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumAlpha ArchitectVIXmomentum
So what: Adjusting lookback by volatility regime does not reliably improve simple momentum.
The study's design is a long-only monthly-rebalanced pick of the best one or two of five ETFs with equal notional weights and no volatility scaling, so it resembles retail cross-sectional momentum rather than directional trend following.
data_point🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumAlpha ArchitectETFs
So what: Its findings do not transfer directly to institutional CTA-style trend models.
The apparent outperformance of the VIX-conditioned variant is concentrated in a few months, notably November 2020 after the Covid vaccine announcement, and the high-VIX regime had only three observations, so statistical power is very low.
data_point🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumVIXNovember 2020
So what: Trigger-based strategies rest on a handful of activations and cannot be statistically validated.
Nick prefers scaling signals continuously by volatility and correlation within portfolio design and risk budgeting over discrete VIX triggers, because triggers by nature activate rarely and parameterize heavily.
framework🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumvolatility scalingtrend following
So what: Continuous volatility use avoids regime-classification overfitting.
Nick argues that if trends do not exist, a trend model should not be made more complex to recreate them, and that investors should instead add complementary systematic strategies that benefit from those regimes.
thesis🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumtrend following
So what: Diversifying the sleeve beats parameter-heavy attempts to time trend regimes.
A faster trend model would improve turning-point response, but the open question is whether speed can be deployed dynamically in time; Nick asks any indicator claiming to time turning points to reveal its false positives, since picking 3 of 33 right proves little.
framework🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumshort-term momentum
So what: Judge timing signals by false-positive rate, not by hits.
Sharp V-shaped market reversals occur about once per year over the last four years, including the Liberation Day episode, and have scarred trend followers, while the March 2026 event was less contagious.
anecdote🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumLiberation Daytrend followers
So what: Short-lived spikes that reverse quickly are a recurring risk for unconstrained trend followers.
Agentic AI for asset allocation (Andrew Ang paper) 7
Andrew Ang, formerly head of factor investing at BlackRock, with new firm Oldbridge, published a paper on an agentic architecture where macro, asset-class, covariance, 19 portfolio-construction, researcher and adversarial-diversifier agents feed a CIO agent that writes a board memo.
framework🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: highAndrew AngOldbridge
So what: This blueprint shows how institutional asset allocation workflows may be automated under human supervision.
In the paper's March 2026 test, the macro agent classified the regime as late-cycle with stagflationary risk, and the CIO agent chose an allocation of about 44% equities against a 60/40 benchmark, favoring heuristic and risk-based schemes over those using capital market assumptions.
data_point🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumCIO agent60/40March 2026
So what: Under an unclear macro regime the agentic process leaned towards diversification and away from return forecasts.
Nick's reading is that when the macro regime is mixed or unobservable, portfolios should diversify and rely on risk-oriented or heuristic allocation rather than trust capital market assumptions.
thesis🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumcapital market assumptions
So what: Allocation conviction should scale with regime clarity.
Backtesting LLM-based allocation is problematic because models embed knowledge of the future, agents may vote for better-performing schemes in hindsight, and LLM outputs are non-deterministic so identical prompts can give different results, which blocks reproducibility.
causal🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: highLLMback test
So what: Agentic products cannot be validated with standard historical backtests.
The paper flags monoculture risk: if all agents use the same LLM from one provider, their outputs become correlated.
causal🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumLLMmonoculture risk
So what: Widespread use of the same foundation model could create correlated allocation errors.
The paper's opening argues the most binding constraint in asset management is the finite bandwidth of human decision makers rather than data or model sophistication, a point Nick links to why clients value trend following's ability to deploy faster than an investment board can act.
thesis🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: mediumtrend followingCIO
So what: Speed of decision-to-deployment is itself a source of edge for systematic strategies.
Nick expects agentic AI to make multi-objective portfolio design (such as crisis alpha, drawdown limits and absolute return) more accessible but still requiring human expertise, and says the bar for AI-run index products is higher so timing of labeled AI-agent products is hard to foresee.
prediction🗣 Nick Palus (guest, systematic investor)conviction: lowagentic AIindex products
So what: Near-term adoption is more likely as supervised tooling than as autonomous labeled products.

This Week in Startups · Jason Calacanis 1 episode(s) · 26 insights

Justin Kan & Brian Chesky: The Rejections That Built Twitch and Airbnb | E234477 min26 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Flashback episode of TWiST replaying Justin Kan's 2019 and Brian Chesky's 2023 interviews: how Twitch emerged from a 3% niche inside a stalled Justin TV, how Airbnb survived serial rejection, and how the hosts compare founder, funding and AI-productivity dynamics in 2026 versus then.

Twitch origin and focus on niche signal 3
Justin TV stalled around 2010 at roughly the top 300 on Alexa and over 10 million monthly users, with no idea how to grow; gaming was only about 3% of content and a couple hundred thousand monthly uniques, yet co-founder Emmett Shear's push to focus on it became Twitch, later bought by Amazon for $970 million in 2014.
anecdote🗣 Justin Kan / Lon Harrisconviction: highJustin TVTwitchAmazonEMTTStarcraft 2
So what: A tiny, passion-led sub-segment inside a plateaued platform can become the main business; the winning signal was founder taste, not the aggregate data.
“the only type of content that I actually want to watch” ▶ 4:47
Pursuing a 3% user segment during flat growth is something a venture-backed startup today would be laughed out of the room for, which makes the Twitch pivot an unusually orthogonal bet.
contrarian🗣 Alex Wilhelmconviction: mediumTwitchJustin TV
So what: Conventional VC process would have rejected the pivot; subsidiary-product bets still need founder conviction against data.
Early game streaming was throttled by publisher copyright strikes, whereas today publishers (except Nintendo) treat streamers as promotion and lifeblood.
data_point🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumNintendoTwitch
So what: Platform categories can start as legal gray zones that flip into partner ecosystems once the distribution value is obvious.
Rejection, resilience and founder selection 5
Google considered acquiring Justin TV in 2010, interviewed the entire team and then declined because the team was 'not good enough'; Justin Kan's lesson is never to let an acquirer interview your whole team, and the rejection is what triggered the brainstorm that produced Twitch.
anecdote🗣 Justin Kanconviction: highGoogleJustin TVYouTube
So what: Failed M&A can force the strategic reset that creates the real outcome; also a diligence-risk lesson on exposing the team to acquirers.
“We let them, which you should never ever do, never let an inquirer ever interview your entire team.” ▶ 13:16
In 2008 Airbnb tried to raise $150,000 at a $1.5 million post-money (10%) from 10-20 angels, most did not reply and others objected to travel, to the name, to having two designers among three founders, and to strangers staying with strangers; at a cited $92.5 billion valuation, that 10% is worth about $9.25 billion.
data_point🗣 Brian Chesky / Alex Wilhelmconviction: highAirbnbCheskyY Combinator
So what: Consensus rejection of a founding team's 'weaknesses' (designer-heavy, non-technical optics) can mask the actual edge.
“I always felt like Joe and I our ability as being designers was actually part of the secret sauce” ▶ 41:29
Y Combinator admitted Airbnb partly because the founders funded themselves by selling parody political cereal boxes (Obama O's) at about $40 each to reporters; Paul Graham reasoned that people who can sell $4 cereal for $40 might get people to stay in each other's homes.
anecdote🗣 Brian Cheskyconviction: highAirbnbY CombinatorPaul GrahamObama O's
So what: Early investors price resourcefulness and demonstrated ability to monetize attention, not just the idea.
Paul Graham and Jessica Livingston later described the Airbnb founders as 'cockroaches', backing them because they seemed unkillable even if the fundraising market dried up.
anecdote🗣 Brian Cheskyconviction: mediumPaul GrahamJessicaAirbnbY Combinator
So what: In a funding winter, survivability and persistence are a primary selection criterion.
“we seemed Brazilian unkillable” ▶ 47:20
The panel argues grit and resourcefulness matter more than last week's traction, and that attention-hacking (making things go viral, 'stop the scroll') is a core founder competency that will become more valuable as attention fragments.
thesis🗣 Lon Harrisconviction: mediumAirbnbY Combinator
So what: Distribution and attention skill is a durable founder edge; startup launch videos have gone from lo-fi to $50-100K Avengers-style productions against Airbnb's $150K total raise.
Founder self-awareness vs founder mode 3
Justin Kan says the meta-skill for founders is knowing what you are good and bad at, and that most founders hit a wall once something works because survivorship bias convinces them all their decisions are right, so they micromanage and refuse to delegate.
framework🗣 Justin Kanconviction: highJustin Khan
So what: Founder-as-bottleneck is a common growth ceiling; look for founders who delegate outside their zone of competence.
“I think most founders hit a wall because they're doing something it starts working and then they don't get out of their own way” ▶ 34:21
Alex Wilhelm notes Kan's delegation advice is an implicit rejection of 'founder mode', since a founder who took a company to a billion-dollar exit advocated shared responsibility, whereas today's conventional wisdom has swung toward one founder owning the details.
contrarian🗣 Alex Wilhelmconviction: mediumJustin Khanfounder mode
So what: Startup management orthodoxy cycles; founder mode is not universally supported by successful operators.
Ranker's Clark Benson is cited as a model founder: he owned product and UX decisions and left editorial and social experimentation to the media team, which let that business grow.
anecdote🗣 Lon Harrisconviction: mediumRankerClark Benson
So what: Domain-split decision rights are an observable positive in founder behavior.
Zero-to-hero startup speed vs past eras 4
Justin TV spent roughly five years as a 25-person 'zombie startup' without an inflection before Twitch; Lon Harris argues today's AI-era expectation of zero to $100 million or $1 billion revenue in record time leaves founders little patience for such wilderness years, so employees and backers bail at the first whiff of failure.
thesis🗣 Lon Harrisconviction: mediumJustin TVTwitch
So what: Shorter tolerance means fewer slow-burn category creators but faster pivots and serial company starts.
“Everything develops the whiff of failure much more quickly now” ▶ 21:21
In AI, investor selection has collapsed to a pure bet on the founder because moat, model cost and TAM are unknowable (OpenAI may ship the feature, the next model may change everything).
thesis🗣 Alex Wilhelmconviction: mediumOpen AIventure capital
So what: Expect founder-quality heuristics to dominate seed/Series A pricing in AI over market analysis.
The AI model life cycle (data collection, pre-train, post-train, fine-tune, RL, release) leaves a short window to monetize before obsolescence, which explains the expectation of faster growth; Typesafe's Jev went from zero to trillions of tokens overnight, then OpenAI announced a competing product at its developer day.
causal🗣 Alex Wilhelmconviction: mediumJevOpen AITypesafe
So what: Product-layer startups riding model APIs face rapid platform-competition risk after traction.
Cheap AI app-building (e.g. Lovable) means someone who dislikes an app can build and self-host a better version in about two hours instead of three to four months, with no App Store tax, compressing competitive moats.
causal🗣 Lon Harrisconviction: mediumLovableApp Store
So what: Low replication cost lowers software defensibility; consumer app moats must come from distribution or data.
Unit economics and monetization shift in the AI era 3
In the web 2.0 era investors cared only about monthly active users and gave near-unlimited runway (Cosmo.com delivered a muffin and a lighter with no minimum order), whereas AI has real marginal compute cost per user in a compute crunch, forcing faster monetization.
causal🗣 Alex Wilhelm / Lon Harrisconviction: mediumCosmo.comOpen AIweb 2.0
So what: AI products cannot subsidize growth like zero-marginal-cost web apps; ads and take-rates arrive earlier.
“We are in a compute crunch and every CPU or GPU cycle that you consume has a real cost to it” ▶ 32:17
The Instinct agent (waitlisted, VC-first) says it will always be free and take a cut of transactions, around 2-3% on bookings, with some boutique hotels reportedly offering a 30% cut for agent reservations; commentators already question whether that model makes sense.
data_point🗣 Lon Harrisconviction: lowInstinctNoah Shin
So what: Agent-commerce affiliate economics are a live business-model experiment; claimed 30% hotel commissions would be a notable distribution lever.
Instinct's early adopters and use cases (calendar, luxury shopping, high-end reservations) were skewed toward venture-capitalist problems, a sign of a product that is 'venture legible' but possibly narrow.
contrarian🗣 Lon Harrisconviction: lowInstinct
So what: Hype among VCs may overstate mainstream consumer demand.
AI, jobs and who captures productivity 3
Lon Harris and Alex Wilhelm observe that AI so far makes workers more productive and busier rather than replacing them, agreeing with David Sacks; their own docket-writing was shifted to a Claude skill maintained by an employee, Zach, cutting that work about 20% but leaving a human curating stories and tuning the skill.
thesis🗣 Lon Harrisconviction: mediumClaudeZach
So what: Near-term effect is task automation and role redefinition, not mass layoffs; the human-in-the-loop skill-maintainer is the emergent job.
“It is probably like 20% less work over the course of the week, but it is still a job.” ▶ 1:01:08
Alex Wilhelm predicts slow erosion of high-touch knowledge-work categories rather than a thunderclap within 18 months, with net more jobs and medium-term churn, and notes expert-feedback vendors like Micro One and Taste Labs may become unnecessary in 3-5 years once expertise is baked into models.
prediction🗣 Alex Wilhelmconviction: mediumMicro OneTaste Labs
So what: Expert-in-the-loop training/labelling markets may be time-limited; displacement is gradual.
Wilhelm argues AI has ratcheted up the intensity and duration of work, leaving people too busy to benefit from the predicted revival of in-person experiences.
causal🗣 Alex Wilhelmconviction: mediumAI
So what: Weak evidence so far for an AI-driven offline/authenticity consumer boom; demand shift is attitudinal (hostility to slop) rather than behavioral.
Agents and software abundance 5
Brian Chesky said in 2023 that AI makes programming possible in natural language, analogous to cameras democratizing photography, so the world will see an abundance of software and millions of new startups, a prediction that panelists find only partly borne out: serial founders are starting three to five companies, but first-time founders (bagel shop, dog groomer) are not yet visible.
prediction🗣 Brian Chesky / Lon Harrisconviction: mediumCheskyAirbnbAWS
So what: Software-creation gains are accruing to existing builders and to employees building internal micro-tools more than to new-entrant entrepreneurs.
“We're going to live in an abundance of software.” ▶ 1:05:43
Non-technical people are building their own apps with Claude inside their jobs, suggesting AI is creating internal micro software 'companies' within roles rather than a measurable startup explosion.
thesis🗣 Alex Wilhelmconviction: mediumClaude
So what: Productivity impact may show up as bespoke internal tooling, which reduces demand for some off-the-shelf SaaS.
Chesky argued in 2023 that the physical world and authentication of identity would be Airbnb's brand moat as AI makes it hard to tell humans from machines, and that search would become a matching problem solved by AI.
prediction🗣 Brian Cheskyconviction: mediumAirbnbAI
So what: Trust and verified identity are positioned as scarce assets in an AI-saturated internet.
“we don't have a search problem in the future. We have a matching problem.” ▶ 54:16
Lon Harris uses an X API-connected Grockbot to produce daily trending-demo research (finding three times as many Sonnet 5.5 demos), timestamps and clip creation, as well as to find contact info (found Fei-Fei Li of World Labs' email), reducing research time from about an hour.
anecdote🗣 Lon Harrisconviction: lowGrockbotSonnet 5.5World LabsX
So what: Agent workflows tied to social data APIs are replacing manual research; media/sales prospecting is an early use case.
Wilhelm says AI can take the inherited knowledge out of analytics work: feed Google Analytics or YouTube data to Claude or Grockbot and it recommends what to publish with no analytics expertise, though only for tasks already ingested into training data.
thesis🗣 Alex Wilhelmconviction: mediumClaudeGoogle AnalyticsGrockbot
So what: Tacit-skill moats (analytics fluency) are eroding, favoring those with taste and domain judgment.

Capital Allocators · Ted Seides 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

Nancy Zimmerman: People, Process, and Fixed Income Arbitrage at Bracebridge Capital50 min25 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Nancy Zimmerman of Bracebridge Capital explains why fixed income arbitrage inefficiencies persist, how the firm sizes positions and manages crisis risk, and why hyperscaler debt issuance and policy experimentation create today's opportunities.
Why fixed income inefficiencies persist · 7Portfolio construction and risk · 5Current market regime and AI debt issuance · 7Deal sourcing and structure · 3People, process and alignment · 3
Why fixed income inefficiencies persist 7
Bracebridge's founders pitched Yale's David Swenson in 1994 on fixed income arbitrage that avoids rate-direction bets, and Swenson replied that other managers claiming to do this spent much of their risk capital and time guessing which way rates were going.
anecdote🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumBracebridge CapitalDavid SwensonYale
So what: True market-neutral fixed income arb was scarce in the 1990s, and the pure-relative-value niche has been a durable edge.
Fixed income inefficiencies persist for decades because of constrained agents: investor habitat, market segmentation, narrow mandates, and indexes that quickly become strategies themselves.
causal🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: highBracebridge Capital
So what: Structural constraints, not information gaps, create the persistent mispricings; look for forced or mandate-bound holders.
“A lot of inefficiencies come from places where you have a constrained economic agent.” ▶ 20:12
Ratings are positively serially correlated and backward looking, and index construction depends on them, which creates mispricing at ratings and index boundaries.
causal🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumratings agencies
So what: Benchmark and ratings-driven flows are a recurring source of dislocations in credit.
Benchmark-driven passive investing and ETFs that must create and destroy units mechanically, especially with added leverage, produce unusual second-order effects that arbitrageurs can exploit.
causal🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumETFs
So what: Passive flows add mechanical price pressure that can be traded against.
Newness (new instruments), changes in rate or volatility levels, and shifts in market ownership or borrowing appetite are the triggers that create fixed income opportunities.
framework🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumBracebridge Capital
So what: Regime change and ownership turnover are the entry signals for relative-value hunting.
Zimmerman says she would have expected developed market rates to become more efficient since 1994, but opportunities persist because markets are bigger and more complex, and there are now about 48 instruments describing risk-free US rates in the first three years of the curve versus three at the start of her career.
data_point🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: highdeveloped market ratesUS Treasuries
So what: Instrument proliferation is itself a source of arbitrage in rates.
“If you told me in 1994 that I would still be finding opportunities in develop market rates today, I would have thought you were nuts.” ▶ 18:57
Emerging market arbitrage persists because many investors want only a little EM and benchmark indexes hold dollar bonds, leaving out-of-index bonds, such as same-obligor bonds in other currencies, inefficiently priced once swapped back to a single currency.
causal🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumemerging markets
So what: Pairing dollar and local-currency bonds of the same obligor lets investors harvest EM alpha without a view on the sovereign credit.
Portfolio construction and risk 5
Bracebridge sizes positions bottom-up and then aggregates by common driver, since many positions that look separate share one force or sector, with overarching concentration limits on how much balance sheet sits in a given market or name.
framework🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: highBracebridge Capital
So what: Position size should be set on correlated exposure, not on the line item.
Secured positions carry collateral that covaries with its sector, so collateral exposure must be counted in concentration limits, and identical arbitrages can be repriced because investors label one flavor good and another bad.
causal🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumBracebridge Capital
So what: Hedged positions still carry sector and sentiment risk through collateral and category labels.
Bracebridge keeps diversification and liquidity to retain buying power on days when forced sellers dump assets, and distinguishes drawdown from capital impairment.
framework🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: highBracebridge Capital
So what: Dry powder and liquidity are the real payoff of stress testing for relative-value funds.
“You want to think hard about what's the difference between a draw down and a capital impairment because those are different things.” ▶ 0:27
Crises teach arbitrageurs to examine reliance on institutions, courts, banks and counterparties; in the middle of COVID Bracebridge bought Treasuries in escrow at 80 cents on the dollar.
data_point🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: highCOVIDTreasuriesBracebridge Capital
So what: Liquid positions let a fund move quickly into indiscriminate selling.
“In the middle of CO, we're buying treasuries in escrow at 80 cents on the dollar.” ▶ 29:44
Zimmerman says the opportunity set must govern fund size: Bracebridge does not 'stock the lake' and was once closed to new capital for almost a decade.
thesis🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: highBracebridge Capital
So what: Capacity discipline in arbitrage strategies matters, and heavy QE periods leave little to do.
“We don't stock the lake. We just fish here.” ▶ 33:12
Current market regime and AI debt issuance 7
Zimmerman sees today's setting as rhyming with the dot-com era: risk capital has flowed elsewhere, and ordinary assets are enormously mispriced on a sunny day with low VIX and ample liquidity, with less price discrimination than expected.
thesis🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumVIXdot-com era
So what: Low-vol complacency coexists with relative-value dislocations because capital is concentrated in a few themes.
Hyperscalers are bringing about 250 billion of investment grade bonds, a size she compares with Treasury issuance, and are largely insensitive to basis points because they borrow as much, for as long, in whatever currency they can.
data_point🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumhyperscalersinvestment grade bonds
So what: Price-insensitive supply from hyperscalers should widen spreads and create cross-currency and curve relative-value trades.
Hyperscaler data center tenants sign leases and guarantees that form a complex of mathematically related obligations across a small group of corporates, giving a rich relative-value surface.
thesis🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumhyperscalersdata centers
So what: AI capex financing is creating structured, interlinked claims that can be mispriced.
Google's debut issuance included a hundred-year sterling bond, which Zimmerman calls not an easy set of cash flows to price or place.
data_point🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumGooglesterling
So what: Unusual maturities and currencies of AI-related debt create pricing gaps.
The long-standing marginal buyer of US bonds has been absent since QE ended: first Japanese, then Chinese buyers, then QE; the Treasury now plans buybacks and shortening of duration issuance, an era of policy experimentation she says is good for rates arbitrage.
thesis🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumUS TreasuryQE
So what: The absence of a natural buyer for duration raises odds of rates dislocations.
Because borrowers have to refinance debt issued in 2021-22 at higher rates, companies without a business model that supports rolling it face restructuring, and liability management exercises hinge on legal constraints and enforceability more than personalities.
causal🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumliability management exercises
So what: Distressed credit outcomes will turn on contract rights and security interests, not just math.
“If you're not at the table, you're on the menu” ▶ 38:36
Zimmerman says endowments hold fewer bonds because bonds are no longer reliably anti-correlated with equities, and no one is sure borrowers will act in a balanced way in a downturn given the huge demand to borrow.
thesis🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumbondsendowments
So what: The stock-bond hedge is questioned, which increases demand for uncorrelated alpha sources.
Deal sourcing and structure 3
Bracebridge lent against ring-fenced property to a healthcare REIT that could borrow but not pay interest, borrowing at 8.5 and lending at 11.25, and hedged REIT survival by shorting another part of the capital structure.
anecdote🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: highhealthcare REITBracebridge Capital
So what: Cross-team structured and corporate credit tie-ups can turn covenant constraints into secured, hedged high-carry loans.
“borrowing at 8 and a half, you're lending to the same name at 11 and a quarter and you're secured on your long side” ▶ 23:16
Zimmerman says Bracebridge's pricing question is whether a trade is true arbitrage with a catalyst and a defined exit via maturity or default, and compares each against what the firm earns for similar risk elsewhere on the platform.
framework🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: highBracebridge Capital
So what: Opportunities compete for capital on risk-adjusted return across the whole platform.
Single-name CDS, trading alongside deliverable bonds, let Bracebridge immunize corporate credit risk and price a capital stack, since default or payment probability must be the same at every level of the stack.
framework🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumCDSBracebridge Capital
So what: Capital-structure arbitrage relies on one coherent default probability across instruments.

Zimmerman says process plus people produces performance, and that curiosity is the trait that transcends all others in arbitrage investors.
thesis🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: highBracebridge Capital
So what: Hiring for curiosity and rigor, then building process, is the stated organizing principle.
“Process plus people is how you get performance.” ▶ 48:58
Bracebridge aligns staff with investors by making employees investors, paying seniors more when investors do well, and rewarding the production of public goods such as models and methods.
framework🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: mediumBracebridge Capital
So what: Compensation tied to shared tools and investor outcomes sustains a long-tenured team.

contrarian🗣 Nancy Zimmermanconviction: lowBrown University
So what: Institutional behavior is backward looking and path dependent, creating opportunities for the nimble.

Narrow breadth, AI single-factor market 5
The S&P 500 was about 0.5% off a record high with only 51.2% of stocks above their 200-day average, a combination last seen the day after the dot-com bubble peak; breadth was equally poor in 1998, after which the market ran two more years.
data_point🗣 Luke Kawaconviction: mediumS&P 500dot-com bubble

thesis🗣 Luke Kawaconviction: highS&P 500Russell 2000AI

Wealth effect and warning signs 3
Housing was the business cycle via home equity and consumer durables spillovers; the stock market now occupies that role as Americans' wealth from stocks crossed above wealth from housing earlier this year, and AI capex replaces the construction impulse, creating a circular perpetual motion machine of stock gains, spending and capex.
thesis🗣 Luke Kawaconviction: mediumhousingAI capexwealth effect
So what: The economy's dependence on equity gains makes a stock drawdown a direct macro risk, and housing weakness matters less.
Kawa's preferred warning signal is index-level earnings revisions cracking: waiting until S&P 500 revisions are 5% off their 52-week high would have cost little in most of the past 6-7 bear markets, with the 2020 COVID crash the main exception, so invested holders can wait for earnings to crack.

EXIM turnaround and mandate 5
The Export Import Bank of the US returned to profitability after a decade or more of losses, and now sends a dividend to the American taxpayer.
data_point🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: highExport Import Bank
So what: Evidence that a re-focused state lender can run profitably while pursuing industrial-policy goals.
“after a decade or more of being unprofitable, as of a couple weeks, Joe, we return the bank to profitability” ▶ 6:56
EXIM's new leadership reorganized the bank around four priorities: back to basics (jobs and competitiveness), energy dominance, supply chain security, and industries of the future such as dual-use tech, high-payload satellites and the global AI export plan.
framework🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: highExport Import Bankglobal AI export plan
So what: Shows where EXIM financing will be directed: energy, supply chains, defense-adjacent tech and AI exports.
EXIM does zero equity investing and only makes senior secured lending, so repayment is protected for the taxpayer; even a small amount of its capital in a deal changes counterparty behavior because the counterparty is effectively the US government.
thesis🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: highExport Import Bank
So what: US-government-backed senior debt acts as political risk insurance, lowering the cost of doing deals in contested markets.
“we do senior secured lending in a way that has reassurance of repayment” ▶ 14:22
Between 87% and 90% of EXIM's annual transaction activity goes to small and medium-sized businesses, and the bank is prioritizing regional banks and small community lenders as capital channels for them.
data_point🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: highExport Import Bank
So what: Small-business and regional-bank exposure is a major route for federal industrial finance, not just large cap exporters.
“87% of our transaction activity every single year between 87 and 90% is focused on small and medium-sized businesses” ▶ 19:37
The pre-turnaround EXIM problem was lack of purpose and metrics rather than understaffing: employees gave a dozen answers to what the priorities were, and senior underwriters had no target for deal counts; the fix was four priorities, an assembly-line process from origination to repayment, and clear KPIs.
causal🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: mediumExport Import Bank
So what: Turnaround of a dormant government tool came from management clarity, which suggests other dormant agencies may be reformable the same way.
Reindustrialization and the revenge of the physical world 4
America must manufacture more in order to innovate more, because iteration speed depends on proximity: a competitor who tweaks a prototype and sees results in a day or two beats one waiting two weeks for parts from across the globe.
causal🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: highAmerica
So what: Onshoring is framed as an innovation-velocity advantage, not only a security or jobs issue.
“You can't innovate if you can't iterate.” ▶ 0:04
Capital-intensive, formerly unglamorous sectors like energy, commodities, infrastructure and supply chains are in fashion again, a shift described as the revenge of the physical world after years when investors wanted only capital-light businesses.
thesis🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: highenergyinfrastructuresupply chains

“is the revenge of the physical world” ▶ 10:59
Entrepreneur and capital attention in America has shifted from trivial consumer apps toward hard problems like low-cost plentiful missiles, space satellite communications and high-payload launch.
thesis🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: mediumsatellites
