Critical minerals and Project Vault 5
Project Vault, announced in February by the president with EXIM, is a roughly $12 billion public-private critical minerals reserve designed to be big at the start, scalable, include US-operating manufacturers, and cost taxpayers nothing.
data_point🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: highProject VaultExport Import Bank
So what: A funded demand pool for stockpiled critical minerals underpins domestic manufacturers' supply resilience.
“can't cost the taxpayers anything” ▶ 29:17
Project Vault is deliberately not a centralized government stockpile like the Strategic Petroleum Reserve: it is demand-driven, with each participating manufacturer holding its own safety deposit box (for example titanium, gallium or copper) that can be drawn up and down, with full access in a crisis; EXIM is just a lender to the independent entity.
framework🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: highProject VaultStrategic Petroleum Reservetitaniumgalliumcopper
So what: Market-driven stockpiling lets companies, not bureaucrats, choose which minerals to hold, which creates private demand for storage and offtake of specific minerals.
“We wanted everybody to have a safety deposit box in the vault” ▶ 31:16
Project Vault is meant to pay for itself: EXIM lends at the fair cost of capital with private sector capital participation and companies paying for the service and having skin in the game, so taxpayers are not left holding losses.
causal🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: mediumProject Vault
So what: Template of aligning incentives with private capital that the administration is reusing across industrial policy.
“how can we all have skin in the game that will align incentives” ▶ 33:24
Refining and processing of critical minerals, especially rare earths, was offshored because it was a dirty process and rules pushed it abroad, where it is now dominated by strategic competitors who have solved the pollution issues with innovation; Project Vault is only a small part of the fix, and EXIM's Make More in America initiative aims to get mining, refining and processing projects to FID and COD with allies.
thesis🗣 John Javonovic (EXIM chairman)conviction: highChinaMake More in Americarare earth
So what: The binding bottleneck is processing capacity, so financing for refining projects is the real lever beyond stockpiles.

contrarian🗣 Joe Lonsdaleconviction: mediumChinarare earth

Why Everything is Going Wrong for Saudi Arabia25 min21 insights2026-09-30

Saudi Arabia's oil exports are down about 70% as Iran squeezes Hormuz, Iraqi militias hit the East-West pipeline, and a Houthi offensive takes Yemen's Red Sea coast and Perim Island near Bab el-Mandeb. Red Sea / Bab el-Mandeb chokepoint 4
Houthis overran about 5,400 sq km of Yemen's Red Sea coast in days, taking Mocha, the Hanish islands and Perim Island in the middle of the Bab el-Mandeb Strait, roughly 20 km from Djibouti.
data_point🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: highHouthisMochaHanish islandsPerim IslandBab el-MandebDjibouti
So what: Houthi control of nearly all Yemen's Red Sea coast raises the odds of a Bab el-Mandeb shutdown, a second chokepoint on top of Hormuz.

thesis🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: mediumHouthisBab el-Mandeb
So what: Tail risk of closing both Hormuz and Bab el-Mandeb at once is now credible; tanker and war-risk insurance pricing for the Red Sea is exposed.
Saudi oil exports through Bab el-Mandeb fell about 95% even before the latest Houthi advance, as Saudi Arabia shifted exports north through the Red Sea toward Suez.
data_point🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: highSaudi ArabiaBab el-MandebSuez Canal
So what: Southbound Saudi barrels to Asia are already effectively gone, so the remaining Red Sea route runs north to Suez.
The Houthis have also repeatedly attacked Yanbu, Saudi Arabia's main Red Sea oil export hub, in an attempt to halt flows north through Suez.
data_point🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: mediumHouthisYanbuSuez Canal
So what: Yanbu is the single point of failure for Saudi bypass exports, so it is the key asset to watch.
Saudi oil exports and East-West pipeline 5
Saudi Red Sea oil exports rose from about 120,000 bpd before the Iran war in February to over 4 million bpd by June (about 30x, roughly 4% of global supply) as the East-West pipeline replaced Hormuz as the export route.
data_point🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: highSaudi ArabiaEast-West pipelineYanbuStrait of Hormuz
So what: Roughly 4% of world oil supply now depends on one pipeline and one Red Sea port, which concentrates risk.
The East-West crude pipeline, built in the early 1980s as a hedge against Hormuz dependence, runs more than 12,200 km from the Gulf oil heartland to Yanbu and never ran at full capacity for an extended period before the Iran war.
data_point🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: highEast-West pipelineYanbuSaudi Arabia
So what: Its spare capacity was the Saudi buffer, and that buffer is what is now being attacked.
Iran-backed militias in Iraq fired missiles and drones at East-West pipeline pumping stations, causing damage visible from space and forcing Saudi Arabia to shut the pipeline. Saudi Arabia said limited operation would return in days and full operation in up to several weeks.
data_point🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: highIraqi militiasEast-West pipelineSaudi ArabiaIran
So what: The pipeline can be hit again after repair, so any recovery in Saudi export capacity is fragile.
Saudi exports were down about 70% by mid-September, from 7.5 million bpd in January-February to 2.1 million bpd, and the speaker expects further declines.
data_point🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: highSaudi Arabia
So what: One of the largest oil exporters is shipping about a quarter of its normal volume, which is a major supply shock.
Saudi production was cut to about 6 million bpd in August, the lowest in over three decades, against an average of about 9.4 million bpd in 2025, because the country cannot get its oil out.
data_point🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: highSaudi Arabia
So what: Further cuts are possible if Bab el-Mandeb pressure and pipeline attacks continue, which is bullish for the oil price and bearish for Saudi fiscal health.
Saudi fiscal and financial stress 2
About 60% of Saudi government budget revenue comes directly from oil exports, so deeper production cuts risk serious domestic unrest.
causal🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: mediumSaudi Arabia
So what: The fiscal hit from the export collapse is the transmission channel into Saudi domestic stability.
The speaker argues Saudi Arabia may soon be forced to sell assets such as US Treasuries, private equity fund stakes or AI investments to meet budget needs until oil flows again.
prediction🗣 RealLifeLore narratorconviction: mediumSaudi ArabiaUS Treasuriesprivate equityAI investments
So what: Saudi sovereign liquidation would be a forced-seller overhang for Treasuries, PE and AI-related holdings.

The Prof G Show · Scott Galloway 1 episode(s) · 21 insights

Meta’s Muse AI Agent Cost $0. What’s the Catch?34 min21 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Scott Galloway's Prof G show host Jack Reigns argues Meta's free Muse consumer AI agent, funded by ad profits, is the first mass-market agent and is already hitting inertia-driven businesses like Planet Fitness and Schwab. Guest JC Bar Stefano explains why platforms embrace or block agents depending on business model.
Muse launch and distribution · 5Ad-funded subsidy vs. lab economics · 6Agent adoption and platform conflict · 5Annoyance economy and consumer inertia · 5
Muse launch and distribution 5
Meta's Muse personal AI agent, announced September 8, reached an estimated 3.4 million downloads and over 76,000 App Store ratings in about three weeks, versus roughly 11 million ratings for ChatGPT and about 270,000 for Claude.
data_point🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: highMetaMuseChatGPTClaudeAnthropic
So what: Muse is the fastest consumer-agent uptake yet, but still a fraction of ChatGPT's installed base.
Muse onboarding connects calendar, email, iMessage, notes, Google Drive, Spotify, Peloton, and Plaid financial accounts within about five minutes, removing the setup burden (separate Mac Mini, dozens of programs, databases) that kept OpenClaw confined to tech insiders.
causal🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: highMuseOpenClawPlaidMeta
So what: Abstracting setup is what moves agents from nerd tool to mass-market product.
Muse is the first consumer agent platform combining broad distribution with the ability to give compute away free, outpacing earlier consumer agents: Instinct reached 100,000 users in 7 months of private beta and Grokbot reached 418,000 weekly users one month after its August launch.
thesis🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: highMuseInstinctGrokbotMetaSpaceX
So what: Distribution plus a subsidy engine, not model quality, is deciding the consumer agent race.
Instinct, an iMessage-based agent with a cult following, is rumored to be raising at a $10 billion valuation despite lacking Meta's distribution.
data_point🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: mediumInstinct
So what: Private markets are pricing consumer agents richly even as Meta enters.
Apple (partnering with Gemini for new Siri models) and Google (with Gmail, calendar, Android) are expected to launch consumer agents; the host finds it shocking Google did not beat Meta to market.
prediction🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: mediumAppleGoogleGeminiSiriMeta
So what: Expect a big-tech consumer agent wave; Google is the most obvious laggard with the assets.
Ad-funded subsidy vs. lab economics 6
Muse gives away 100 million tokens per week free, with paid tiers of $20/month for 500 million tokens per week and $100/month for 3 billion, a subsidy OpenAI and Anthropic cannot match because their free tiers rate-limit heavily.
data_point🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: highMetaMuseOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Rate limits on rival chatbots become a switching reason toward whichever tool does not break.
“Mark Zuckerberg is giving away a h 100red million tokens per week” ▶ 4:47
Meta can subsidize agent demand because its ad business generated $59 billion of advertising revenue last quarter versus $1.5 billion from the rest of the company, while OpenAI and Anthropic get paid only on usage via subscriptions or API tokens.
causal🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: highMetaOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Ad-funded cash flow is a structural moat for the AI consumer fight; usage-priced labs face costs that scale with demand.
OpenAI and Anthropic are unprofitable, burning cash, and say private markets are tapped out, so they need to go public, while frontier-model edges get commoditized within weeks (a Chinese model 95% as good at 5% of the price).
thesis🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Pressure on lab IPO timing and valuations as model leads erode; Meta and Google with free cash flow can outspend.
Meta's stock rose from $613 the day before Muse's launch to about $720 at recording, despite a roughly 10% after-hours drop in July when it raised the low end of capex guidance and a 4% drop the day of recording after announcing an enterprise AI business.
data_point🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: mediumMeta
So what: The market currently rewards Meta's AI push, but remains sensitive to spending guidance.

data_point🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: highMetaMongoDBCJ DesaiMuse
So what: Meta is moving to monetize AI directly in enterprise, competing with OpenAI and Anthropic's core revenue.

thesis🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: mediumMark ZuckerbergMetaScale AIAlexander Wang

Agent adoption and platform conflict 5
Amazon blocked Muse from accessing its platform citing terms of service, likely protecting its advertising business and its own purchase-history data for building Amazon agents and agent ads.
causal🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: mediumAmazonMuse
So what: Owners of inventory plus an ad business will wall off third-party agents; watch for agent-ad products.
Whether platforms embrace or block agents depends on business model: Expedia and Instacart fear disintermediation so they integrate, PayPal and Shopify profit from any transaction flow so they integrate, while Amazon and Resy, which own inventory or ad eyeballs, resist.
framework🗣 JC Bar Stefano (Better Tomorrow Ventures)conviction: mediumExpediaInstacartPayPalShopifyAmazonResyBooking.comKayakDoorDash
So what: A usable screen for which companies are exposed to agent disintermediation versus insulated by inventory.
An agent-first Resy is unlikely to be built because the hard part of the reservations business is restaurant relationships, not the booking tool, so Resy has little incentive to open to agents until volume makes it economic.
contrarian🗣 JC Bar Stefano (Better Tomorrow Ventures)conviction: mediumResyOpenTable
So what: Incumbents with supply-side relationships are safer from agent-native startups than demand aggregators.
Instinct, given only the prompt to get a reservation at a famously hard-to-book New York restaurant, hammered Resy's APIs hundreds of times per hour for one to two weeks without intelligent timing, getting its user banned for violating terms of service.
anecdote🗣 JC Bar Stefano (Better Tomorrow Ventures)conviction: highInstinctResy
So what: Naive agents create ToS and account-ban risk, which gives platforms a reason to block agents.
Instinct reportedly moved up a doctor's appointment from January to late September within ten minutes by logging into the doctor's portal using a one-time password pulled from Gmail.
anecdote🗣 JC Bar Stefano (Better Tomorrow Ventures)conviction: mediumInstinctGmail
So what: Agents with email access can bypass OTP-based authentication, a security surface for prompt-injection and abuse concerns.
Annoyance economy and consumer inertia 5
Groundwork Collaborative estimates the annoyance economy (time on hold, convoluted paperwork, junk fees, subscription cancellation friction) costs American families about $165 billion a year, and agents that do not get bored can drive the cost of chasing that money toward zero.
thesis🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: mediumGroundwork CollaborativeMuse
So what: Companies monetizing friction and inertia face margin erosion as agents automate disputes and cancellations.
Early Muse users report recovering money: about $730 of unclaimed Amazon credit, a $272 Sprint refund, $800 across closed accounts and settlements, and over $4,000 of billing errors found by auditing a medical provider portal.
data_point🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: mediumMuseAmazonSprint
So what: Viral savings stories are what will pull mainstream users into agents.
Agents constantly comparing interest rates and moving cash between accounts via Plaid could massively impact net interest margin at banks and similar companies.
prediction🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: lowPlaidMuseGrokbot
So what: Deposit stickiness at banks may erode if agents rate-shop automatically.
On September 22, Goldman Sachs's consumer inertia basket was crushed: Planet Fitness fell 9.5%, LPL Financial 7.5%, The New York Times 7.2%, Schwab 6.1%, and Allstate 5.5%, attributed to Muse and agent threats to inertia-driven revenue.
data_point🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: highGoldman SachsPlanet FitnessLPL FinancialThe New York TimesSchwabAllstate
So what: The market is repricing subscription and habit-revenue businesses; the host thinks it is still inefficient and more repricing may follow.
Planet Fitness's economics rely on members who sign up out of guilt and never attend, with cancellation requiring a letter or in-person visit; an agent that simplifies cancellation directly threatens that revenue.
causal🗣 Jack Reigns (host)conviction: mediumPlanet Fitness
So what: Friction-dependent subscription models are the most exposed to consumer agents.

Chat With Traders 1 episode(s) · 20 insights

Why Defense, Not Offense, Makes You a Profitable Trader · Tim Furey83 min20 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Tim Furey, CEO of retail broker Trade View Markets and former Chicago Board of Trade market maker, argues profitable trading comes from defense (strict loss limits, only trades with a clean exit) and trend following, and shares brokerage-business and near-death anecdotes.
Defense-first pit training · 5Trend following and relative strength · 6Discipline and discretionary conviction · 3Retail brokerage economics and business model · 4Crypto and Bitcoin skepticism · 2
Defense-first pit training 5
The clearing firm training floor market makers at the Chicago Board of Trade taught only loss control: a trader could lose at most about five ticks on a trade, anything over 10 ticks required an explanation, and 15 ticks meant suspension.
framework🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: highChicago Board of Trade
So what: Hard, enforced loss limits were the training mechanism that produced profitable floor traders.
“you had to learn how to lose” ▶ 2:02
Furey made $28,500 in one month as a late-1990s Dow market maker, and when he asked the managers how to make money they told him profit comes from not losing it; he took away that there are no tricks beyond defense.
anecdote🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: highDow indexChicago Board of Trade
So what: Consistent small losses with few outsized ones, not clever entries, drove his month.
“That's how you make money is by not losing it” ▶ 25:50
The 'power of the lean' means only entering trades where you can exit at break even: bid at 50, offer at 55 immediately, and if the market turns, get out at 50, so entries are chosen for exit certainty.
framework🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: highmarket makingDow index
So what: Pick trades by how cleanly you can exit rather than by how much you could make.
“It's called the power to lean” ▶ 24:10
Furey says the best trade can be not trading at all: walking away for a day or a week and waiting for the one setup where the gut says to put it on.
thesis🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: medium
So what: Inactivity is a position; it preserves capital for high-conviction setups.
“that could be your best trade is not trading to walk away” ▶ 45:41
A top Board of Trade bond trader reportedly earning about $100 million a year avoided CNBC, calling it a 50-minute infomercial with biased news, watched the sports channel on big report days, and traded reactively with no opinion on direction.
anecdote🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: mediumCNBCChicago Board of Trade
So what: Elite traders may deliberately avoid narrative input to keep a clear, reactive mind.
Trend following and relative strength 6
Furey is a trend trader using relative strength and weakness, says he does not use Bollinger bands, and holds that the strong get stronger and the weak get weaker.
thesis🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: highBollinger bands
So what: His rule set favors buying strength over indicator-based mean reversion.
“The strong gets stronger and the weak get weaker” ▶ 1:12:31
Chart test from a veteran 1990s trader: print the chart, put it on the floor, stand on a chair and squint; if it is going up, do not short it.
framework🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: medium
So what: A simple visual trend filter keeps traders from fading obvious trends.
Furey argues one should never try to trade a bubble burst: in the 1990s Yahoo ran from $10 to about $400 while analysts called it a bubble and even professional pit traders were tempted to short it and were wrong for a long time.
contrarian🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: highYahoo
So what: Shorting on valuation alone ahead of the top is a common and costly error in strong trends.
“You should never even try to go for a bubble burst in the market” ▶ 1:13:17
Market structure heuristic: a healthy market goes up like an escalator and down like an elevator, so sharp sell-offs in an uptrend should be treated as places to buy.
framework🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: medium
So what: Dips in strong trends are buy opportunities rather than reversal signals.
“escalator up, elevator down” ▶ 1:14:39
Across thousands of self-directed clients at his brokerage, Furey says the most common mistake is contrarian prediction: shorting because a market went up or buying because it went down.
data_point🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: mediumTrade View Markets
So what: Retail losses are linked to fading moves on the assumption of overvaluation; the opposite approach is trend-following.
“They think high and it can't go higher. It guess what? It can.” ▶ 1:15:01
Furey calls shorting gold last year his worst trade because it broke his trend-following rules; he shorted because it looked toppy while oil and other markets were behaving differently, and says nobody knew why gold was going to rise so much.
anecdote🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: mediumgoldoil
So what: Even a rule-based trader gets hurt when he fades relative strength, which supports his rule-discipline claim.
Discipline and discretionary conviction 3
Furey admits he holds a large, undefended long position in Tesla, with no profit target and no defense, which he calls the worst thing you could do in trading since he has an opinion on it, because he rates the technology and Elon Musk highly.
anecdote🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: mediumTeslaElon Musk
So what: The speaker contradicts his own defensive rules on this one name, so it is conviction and not a system signal; he explicitly says he could be wrong.
“It's all or nothing” ▶ 1:10:06
Furey says professional traders should not rely on trading income for a mortgage and family; his advice is to trade as a hobby or part-time because losing years are inevitable and a bad year with fixed obligations is ruinous.
thesis🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: medium
So what: Position sizing in life matters: personal financial leverage forces bad trading decisions.
A first outsized win can ruin a trader: Furey's parents forced him to sell LA Gear at about $14 from an $8 entry on $5,000, and he says the stock later reached $60 with splits, which would have been worth over a million, but he thinks an early windfall would have destroyed his drive.
anecdote🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: lowLA Gear
So what: Early big wins can undermine the hunger and discipline needed for durable trading.
Retail brokerage economics and business model 4
Furey likens a retail broker to the general-store owner in a gold rush: traders chase riches while the broker sells picks and shovels, and he says he gains a pulse on the market from onboarding about 300 new clients a day worldwide.
thesis🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: mediumTrade View Markets
So what: Venue and infrastructure providers capture steadier economics than the participants who trade.
“the guy that owns the general store” ▶ 43:29
Furey claims brokerage is far more scalable than a restaurant because it operates 24 hours with effectively unlimited seats and costs that barely rise as client accounts grow, and says he will only run scalable businesses.
thesis🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: mediumTrade View Markets
So what: Marginal-cost economics of digital brokerage underpin high operating leverage.
Furey says barriers to entry in retail brokerage have risen sharply: he self-funded Trade View around 2006-2007 with all his savings, but today he would need outside investors and a ton of capital, and cyber security has become a major new cost with expert staff on payroll.
data_point🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: mediumTrade View Markets
So what: Compliance, capital and cyber costs favor incumbents and scale in the brokerage industry.
Furey says a payment processor of his brokerage was hacked about eight or nine years ago and $3 million was taken, though it was caught early and recovered.
anecdote🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: lowTrade View Markets
So what: Illustrates operational and fraud risk for brokers beyond market risk.
Crypto and Bitcoin skepticism 2
Furey says he delayed entering Bitcoin because, running a brokerage with audited financials, he distrusted an asset with no audited financials and worried about hacking; he regrets missing the $300 and $3,000 levels and has since built his own crypto exchange, Yimmit.
anecdote🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: mediumBitcoinYimmitTrade View Markets
So what: A regulated-broker mindset kept some traditional financial firms out of crypto early, and many now build their own exchanges after the fact.
Furey says client trading volume at his brokerage flows to whatever market is moving, with equities at highs and crypto the hot area drawing the most activity.
data_point🗣 Tim Fureyconviction: lowTrade View Markets
So what: Retail activity is momentum-chasing, which suggests broker volumes are pro-cyclical.
“Whatever market is moving is where people gravitate to” ▶ 46:18

Bankless 1 episode(s) · 20 insights

Variational Wants to Build the Robinhood of Onchain Trading50 min20 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Variational's CEO and head of product explain its principal-broker model that hedges onto TradFi liquidity, its flat-carry RWA swaps, Arbitrum-based segregated settlement, and the delayed Q4 TGE with a targeted 32% airdrop. Roadmap covers a trading API, mobile/Privy onboarding, and a 2027 institutional Pro product.
Broker model vs order-book exchange · 4RWA perp swaps and TradFi liquidity on-chain · 5Architecture, risk segregation and OLP · 4Token launch and airdrop · 3Growth plan and user base · 4
Broker model vs order-book exchange 4
Variational runs a principal-broker model with no order book: its system takes the other side of every user trade as maker and hedges against aggregated liquidity from crypto exchanges, DEXs and off-chain TradFi sources.
framework🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: highVariationalHyperliquidLighter
So what: Liquidity depth becomes a routing problem rather than a market-maker-incentive problem, which is a structural cost advantage in thin on-chain markets.
“We take the other side of the trade and hedge it against aggregated global liquidity.” ▶ 1:13
Variational says it does not compete directly with Hyperliquid, which it has historically traded on and hedged through; it positions exchanges as price-discovery infrastructure and itself as a retail-facing aggregator, with Robinhood and Interactive Brokers as long-term competitors.
thesis🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: highVariationalHyperliquidRobinhoodInteractive Brokers
So what: Perp DEXs may be complements (hedging venues) to broker-style front ends, not zero-sum rivals.
Lucas argues retail should trade via brokers while HFTs and market makers belong on exchange order books, mirroring TradFi where Jump, Jane Street and HRT trade on NYSE/CME while retail uses Schwab, Robinhood or IBKR.
thesis🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: highJane StreetHRTSchwabRobinhoodInteractive BrokersJump
So what: Implies the retail on-chain trading UX layer could capture value separately from the matching-engine layer.
Variational's zero-fee model is funded by capturing the spread/flow-intermediation revenue that market makers normally earn; the founders liken it to Robinhood and Citadel being vertically combined, and say it is very profitable.
causal🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: mediumVariationalCitadelRobinhoodOLP
So what: Vertical integration of market making and the retail venue is the core economic bet; profitability claims are self-reported.
RWA perp swaps and TradFi liquidity on-chain 5
Variational hedges RWA listings directly onto TradFi rails, claiming on-chain liquidity roughly equal to TradFi and order-of-magnitude better spreads than competing platforms, on the premise that TradFi has trillions of dollars of daily liquidity that on-chain order books cannot rebuild soon.
thesis🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: highVariationalRWATradFi
So what: Routing to existing liquidity may beat incentivizing on-chain market makers for equities, indices and commodities perps.
Variational's 'swap' is a bilateral linear derivative that trades one-to-one with the underlying like a perp but with a flat, predictable carry cost instead of a volatile funding rate, can pay dividends, and cannot be listed on an exchange because it needs an issuer on the other side.
framework🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: highVariationalswapsperps
So what: Swaps are a product only a principal broker can offer, which differentiates it from order-book perp DEXs.
On the live US 100 swap, Variational cites about one-fifth the execution cost of the most liquid on-chain venue (perps on places like Trade XYZ, Binance, Bybit), sub-1bp spread on multi-million-dollar orders, and funding bounded below roughly 5%.
data_point🗣 Justin Brown (head of product)conviction: mediumUS 100Trade XYZBinanceBybitVariational
So what: Self-reported benchmark for RWA index exposure; verifying it independently would test whether the cost edge is real.
Tokenized spot RWAs remain fragmented across many competing standards with no clear winner (a search for Nvidia shows several non-dominant versions), so Lucas argues derivatives are the better route to liquid TradFi exposure on-chain.
contrarian🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: mediumtokenized RWAsDTCCNvidia
So what: Argues perp/swap wrappers may win RWA distribution before tokenized spot gets liquid.
Trade XYZ went from an early HIP-3 deployer to a main driver of Hyperliquid's growth, with billions and some days tens of billions in volume, in about a year; Lucas uses this as precedent for how fast Variational's swaps could scale.
data_point🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: mediumTrade XYZHyperliquidHIP-3Variational
So what: RWA perps on HIP-3 are the clearest comparable for a fast RWA ramp.
Architecture, risk segregation and OLP 4
Variational runs on Arbitrum One with settlement, clearing, liquidations and collateral/P&L movement on-chain, while hedging and pricing systems are off-chain; Lucas says it is among the top five DeFi protocols by Arbitrum gas use because it constantly rebalances USDC between per-user settlement pools and the OLP.
data_point🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: mediumArbitrumUSDCVariationalOLP
So what: A sizeable, persistent Arbitrum gas consumer tied to user activity rather than ZK circuits.
Each Variational user has a segregated on-chain USDC settlement pool rather than a shared hot wallet or cross-margined pool, a design shaped by the founders' experience of the FTX collapse; off-chain hedging risk is borne by OLP capital, not user funds.
thesis🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: highVariationalFTXUSDCArbitrum
So what: Counterparty-risk design is a selling point versus commingled venues, though off-chain hedge counterparty risk sits with the company.
OLP (Omni Liquidity Provider) is the maker side of all trades; it does not pass through each order but aggregates and nets like an intelligent market maker, and Variational has been the major depositor in OLP.
framework🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: mediumOLPVariational
So what: OLP is the profit engine and the main risk concentration of the platform.
Variational lists three revenue sources: OLP flow trading/intermediation (the lion's share), net interest income on open interest, and net interest on USDC balances; the team says revenue is disclosed in bi-weekly updates and is being routed toward the token and ecosystem.
data_point🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: mediumVariationalOLPVAR token
So what: Token value accrual is tied to market-making economics, so token valuation should be modeled on flow-trading margin.
Token launch and airdrop 3
Variational extended its points program through Q4 instead of ending it at Q3, delaying the V token TGE, because of partnerships and features it wants to land first and because of faster-than-expected user growth.
data_point🗣 Justin Brown (head of product)conviction: mediumVariationalV tokenTGE
So what: TGE timing slips into Q4; watch for the promised partnership and feature announcements as catalysts.
Variational targets a 32% airdrop of total token supply, proportional to points, which it says would be the largest percentage in crypto history versus roughly 31% for Hyperliquid and 25% for Lighter.
data_point🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: mediumVariationalHyperliquidLighterairdrop
So what: High community allocation follows the Hyperliquid playbook but implies significant eventual sell pressure from points farmers.
Lucas says the company has been immensely profitable as a prop firm and OLP operator, and that runway is measured in years and decades, so it can route value to the token rather than needing it for funding.
anecdote🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: lowVariationalOLP
So what: Unverified self-report, but it suggests no near-term treasury-selling pressure.
Growth plan and user base 4
Variational is still in private beta with invite codes, requiring a crypto wallet and Arbitrum USDC, and plans public mainnet, then a trading API, then Privy and fun.xyz onboarding to remove key and stablecoin management, targeting users beyond the roughly 30,000 to 100,000 active crypto traders.
prediction🗣 Justin Brown (head of product)conviction: mediumVariationalPrivyArbitrumFOMO
So what: API access and simpler onboarding are the stated volume unlocks, since most exchange volume comes via APIs.
Variational aims to compete longer term with Pluang in Indonesia, Groww in India, Futu in Hong Kong, Interactive Brokers and Robinhood, arguing that all on-chain perp platforms have tiny daily active user counts relative to those brokers.
thesis🗣 Lucas Sherman (CEO)conviction: mediumPluangGrowwFutuInteractive BrokersRobinhood
So what: The addressable market is TradFi retail brokerage migration, not the existing crypto perp cohort of about 100,000 to 200,000 daily users.
Justin argues perps are better than options for retail in principle but current perp UIs (liquidation price, isolated versus cross margin, funding rates) are too advanced, so Robinhood's options UX is simpler than any perp app today.
contrarian🗣 Justin Brown (head of product)conviction: mediumRobinhoodperpsoptions
So what: UX simplification of perps is a gating factor for mass adoption, and a differentiation opportunity.

Computer use capability leap 9
OpenAI's computer use agents are now faster than the average human at most tasks, and Ari Weinstein says the next frontier is superhuman speed, matching or beating expert computer users, which would enable real-time products.
prediction🗣 Ari Weinsteinconviction: highOpenAIcomputer use
So what: Speed parity unlocks real-time agent UX and defaulting to delegation instead of manual work.
“computer use is like faster at accomplishing tasks than like the average human probably in [music] in most cases” ▶ 0:00
The biggest computer-use improvement over the past year is recovery: models used to start tasks reliably but stall on problems, and now debug, retry and introspect on what is failing.
causal🗣 Ari Weinsteinconviction: highOpenAIcomputer use
So what: Error recovery, not initiation, was the reliability bottleneck for long-running agents.
Modern OpenAI computer use writes and executes JavaScript to perform many actions per step and mixes screenshots, accessibility trees and Playwright depending on the task, instead of one click per screenshot.
data_point🗣 Ari Weinsteinconviction: highOpenAIPlaywrightCodexaccessibility API
So what: Harness design (code-as-action plus multimodal interfaces) drives speed gains as much as the model.

causal🗣 Ari Weinsteinconviction: highOpenAIaccessibility APIDOM
So what: Representation efficiency (token-efficient accessibility dumps) is a moat-like engineering detail.
As computer use gets faster, it is increasingly bottlenecked by external latency such as waiting for sites like DoorDash to load, and by non-event-driven waits like customer-service bot replies taking 30 seconds to 3 minutes.
causal🗣 Ari Weinsteinconviction: mediumDoorDashOpenAI
So what: Further speedups depend on event-driven triggering and the speed of the third-party software itself, not only the model.

thesis🗣 Ari Weinsteinconviction: highCodexOpenAI
So what: Agent self-QA is a key productivity unlock for coding agents.

contrarianconviction: exploratoryCodexSaaS

anecdote🗣 Ari Weinsteinconviction: mediumYouTubeOpenAI

Decisions API as a Jev-style fast classifier 4
The Decisions API is built on the same Luna weights with no new model trained: reasoning off, structured output constraints, and an optimized inference stack that runs multiple questions in parallel as a batch; it is priced like Luna.
data_point🗣 Nikunj Handaconviction: highDecisions APILunaOpenAI
So what: A fast-classifier product was shipped in about four weeks with existing weights, showing how cheaply frontier labs can clone startup features.

anecdote🗣 Nikunj Handaconviction: highJevOpenAIDecisions API

thesis🗣 Nikunj Handaconviction: mediumJevDecisions API

API platform: caching, compaction, higher-level primitives 3

For context management OpenAI offers server-side compaction at a token threshold in the Responses API and manual /compact, and the agents API builds proprietary compaction into the harness; file-based compaction techniques are being tested in Codex.

Ex-NVIDIA Engineer: Why AI Is Running Out of Copper33 min17 insights2026-09-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Ex-Nvidia photonics engineer Yannick and Teema co-founder Herwig argue copper is hitting limits in AI clusters, energy is the binding constraint, and photonics supply chain gaps (lasers, tools, substrates) create an opening for a European foundry targeting 2027 production.
Copper to optics in AI networks · 3Power as the binding constraint · 3Nvidia's photonics push and supply chain gaps · 6Teema and European photonics industrialization · 5
Copper to optics in AI networks 3
AI models no longer fit on one GPU, so many GPUs must be linked; copper interconnects are running out of headroom as processors need more data at higher speeds, forcing a move to optical links with far higher bandwidth.
causal🗣 Yannick (ex-Nvidia, Teema)conviction: highcopperGPUoptical interconnect
So what: The interconnect, not just the compute, is the AI scaling bottleneck, which favors photonics suppliers.
“copper is gradually exhausting its resources” ▶ 4:48
Agentic AI makes the model call tools on other servers and return results to the GPU, creating heavy latency-sensitive east-west traffic; this pushes data centers toward flatter, much denser networks with many more direct switch-to-GPU connections than traditional deep switch trees.
framework🗣 Yannick (ex-Nvidia, Teema)conviction: highagentic AIdata center networking
So what: Flatter topologies multiply the number of links per GPU, increasing optical transceiver and laser demand per cluster.
Half of a modern AI factory is GPUs and the other half is the technology that connects them, which the speaker sees as a huge opportunity for optics and photonics.
thesis🗣 Herwig (Teema co-founder)conviction: highAI factoryphotonicsGPU
So what: Interconnect content per AI dollar is large and under-appreciated relative to GPU focus.
“half of it is GPUs, and the other half is the technology to connect them” ▶ 30:38
Power as the binding constraint 3
Data center CTOs say energy consumption is their number one worry, since CEOs demand growth but nobody can supply grid power; Google reportedly spent three years negotiating with Amsterdam and ended up moving plans to Belgium over grid access.
data_point🗣 Herwig (Teema co-founder)conviction: highGoogleAmsterdamBelgiumpower grid
So what: Grid access, not capital, gates data center siting and growth, so energy-efficient interconnect has real value.
“Google spent three years negotiating with Amsterdam” ▶ 7:31
Grid modernization or new power plants cannot arrive on the timeline data center buildouts require, so a more fundamental efficiency solution is needed; the speaker calls data center energy use the 'killer app' for photonics, a technology developed for 20-30 years on the science side.
thesis🗣 Herwig (Teema co-founder)conviction: highphotonicsdata center energy
So what: Photonics adoption is pulled by an energy-efficiency need rather than pure bandwidth.
“the energy consumption of data centers in the field of AI is the same killer application” ▶ 8:18
The speaker contrasts US hostility toward data centers with Europe, where stricter regulation and a smaller data center base mean less aversion, and argues Europe could build a different, more energy-efficient non-hyperscaler data center network.

Nvidia's photonics push and supply chain gaps 6
Nvidia escalated its photonics messaging from talk to action: louder in 2024, a market push in 2025, and in 2026 investments in Lumentum and Coherent that, in the first six months of the year, were reportedly four times the total market volume.
data_point🗣 Herwig (Teema co-founder)conviction: mediumNvidiaLumentumCoherent

“In the first 6 months of this year, they invested four times more than the total market volume.” ▶ 9:12
Lumentum and Coherent are the two leading Western laser makers and effectively the only ones with that capability at scale; the host noted Lumentum's shares rose roughly a thousandfold in a year and that its CEO is overwhelmed with demand.
data_point🗣 Herwig (Teema co-founder)conviction: mediumLumentumCoherent
So what: Laser supply is concentrated in two Western names, giving them pricing power until new capacity arrives.
Foundries such as TSMC, GlobalFoundries and Tower have invested in silicon photonics wafers, but delivering a wafer is not what Nvidia ordered, which is a finished chip; the missing middle needs lasers, packaging and scaled manufacturing, and no industrial player occupies that middle layer.
causal🗣 Herwig (Teema co-founder)conviction: mediumTSMCGlobalFoundriesTowerNvidia
So what: Value may accrue to specialist back-end photonics manufacturers between wafer and finished module.
Photonics scale-up is constrained by inadequate tool throughput, poor yield and efficiency of finished products, scarce substrates with only a handful of suppliers, and an East-West split; tool makers are not building nearly enough equipment.
causal🗣 Yannick (ex-Nvidia, Teema)conviction: highsubstratestool manufacturersCoherentLumentum
So what: Equipment and raw-material shortages cap photonics supply growth for the whole industry, not just startups.
“Tool manufacturers are not building even close to enough tools.” ▶ 19:44

Teema and European photonics industrialization 5
Teema bought a bankrupt large European plant in the 2023-24 period, about 20 km from IMEC, with permits, cleanrooms and infrastructure already in place; since a foundry of this size is normally a 3-5 year project, this shortcut gave a head start.
anecdote🗣 Herwig (Teema co-founder)conviction: mediumTeemaIMEC
So what: Distressed fab assets plus talent proximity let a startup compress the usual foundry timeline.
Teema plans to begin production in 2027 and reach full capacity by 2028.

So what: New European photonics capacity arrives only in 2027-28, so shortages likely persist until then.
The European Council's Chips Act II language reportedly prioritizes photonics, with a focus on industrialization and clean manufacturing, which the speaker says almost reads like a definition of Teema; sovereignty is a driver.

Between 2000 and 2015, about 4,000 peer-reviewed photonics papers were published worldwide and 35% came from Ghent University, evidence of Belgium's deep photonics research base.

European photonics innovations have historically been commercialized in Taiwan or the US; the speaker invokes ASML as the precedent of a European 40-year deep-tech success and argues Europe is more ready than assumed, while US prospects questioned Teema on financing, talent, equipment and speed.
